Sahin Demir
14 September 2026•Update: 14 September 2026
Qatar has agreed to help determine the fate of three Iranian pilots whose whereabouts remain unclear, Iran’s state news agency IRNA reported on Monday.
The case dates back to March 2, when two Iranian Su-24 fighter jets went down during attacks on a military base in Qatar. Iran says three pilots were captured alive, while another was killed.
Iranian Red Crescent Society chief Pir Hossein Kolivand said there was evidence suggesting that the three pilots may be in Qatar and asked the Qatar Red Crescent Society to use its channels to obtain information about their status.
Kolivand said the Qatar Red Crescent Society should seek information about the pilots to help their concerned families, citing the neutral humanitarian role of Red Crescent and Red Cross societies.
Qatar Red Crescent Society officials pledged to assist in following up on the case and obtaining information about the pilots.
The two sides also discussed expanding bilateral cooperation in rescue operations, training and the exchange of expertise.

====================================================================================================

Atokos: The tiny Greek island where people and wild boars swim in the sea and sunbathe together
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:23:45 UTC
16:23:45 KST

Thanks to its swimming pigs, the small island near Ithaca has become a major attraction for tourists from all over the world.
A small Ionian island has unexpectedly become a popular tourist destination, thanks to some unusual "residents". On the shores of Atokos, near Ithaca, visitors find not only crystal-clear waters and natural scenery, but also herds of black pigs roaming freely along the beach.
Atokos is a private island and until recently was relatively unknown to the general public. That changed when photos and videos of the pigs started going viral on Instagram and TikTok. Now travellers from many countries visit the island specifically to see the animals up close, and some even swim alongside them or feed them.
The pigs appear to have become accustomed to human presence. They wander along the shore in search of food, seemingly unbothered by the tourists. When the temperature rises, they head into the sea for a short dip to cool down.
Access to Atokos is mainly by tourist boats. Renato Bisa, who organises excursions from Ithaca, makes several trips to the island each day as demand has risen sharply.
The island's popularity on social media has even spawned an entire tourist experience centred on the animals, with the island now jokingly referred to as "Piggy Island". Trips to the area are also run from Lefkada and Kefalonia.
Bisa explains that many travellers contact him with the express wish of visiting Atokos and meeting the pigs. In some cases, organisers use fruit to lure the animals closer to the boats.
For most visitors, encountering the pigs is an unexpected experience they are unlikely to forget. The sudden attention has transformed the image of the small island. From an almost unknown destination, Atokos has grown into a major draw for thousands of tourists, particularly during the summer season.

TOKYO, Sept. 14 (Xinhua) -- A ground vehicle collided with a parked passenger plane at Fukuoka Airport in southwestern Japan on Monday, with no injuries reported so far, local media said.
The incident occurred shortly after 10 a.m. local time when a vehicle equipped with boarding stairs collided with an All Nippon Airways (ANA) passenger jet parked at the airport, public broadcaster NHK reported, citing the airport's operating company and ANA.
The vehicle was attempting to pass behind the aircraft when it came into contact with the plane near its tail. Passengers had already boarded the aircraft, which was preparing to depart for New Chitose Airport in Hokkaido, but the flight was canceled due to the incident, and arrangements were made to transfer the passengers to another flight, the report said.
According to the airport's operating company, no other flights were affected. ■

====================================================================================================

Israel accused of trying to ‘eliminate’ living conditions for Palestinians in West Bank
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:23:04 UTC
16:23:04 KST

Israel is working towards the systematic “elimination” of the conditions that sustain Palestinian collective life in the occupied West Bank, according to a major report published by the human rights group B’Tselem.
The Israeli organisation’s hard-hitting report, shared with the Guardian in advance of publication, says Israel is “dramatically intensifying its attacks on every aspect of Palestinian life in the West Bank”, including “the ability to get to work, take children to school or university, receive medical treatment or municipal services, meet family and friends, cultivate land or go on vacation … routine activities that make up human life”.
According to B’Tselem, the assault on Palestinians is “governed by a coherent organising logic” and comprises five “interwoven domains of action”: violence and intimidation, movement restrictions, economic strangulation, social and political destruction, and ethnic cleansing and territorial takeover.
The publication of the Elimination Project, B’Tselem’s most comprehensive piece of work on the West Bank since its launch in 1989, comes less than a week after the British foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, accused settler terrorists, supported by Benjamin Netanyahu’s government, of committing ethnic cleansing in the West Bank, in the UK’s starkest condemnation of Israel since the foundation of the state.
 1,107
Number of Palestinians killed by Israel in the West Bank since October 2023
By looking more broadly at the conditions required for Palestinians’ collective existence, however, the B’Tselem report goes further still. It says various arms of the Israeli state – led by Bezalel Smotrich, the far-right finance minister with broad powers over civilian matters in the West Bank – are seeking to “create a reality in which millions of Palestinians live in constant fear of Israeli military and civilian violence”.
“Palestinian communities are being pushed out in an accelerating process of ethnic cleansing, while an ever-expanding Israeli presence is being established in their place in the form of settlements, outposts, farms and Jewish-only civilian infrastructure,” the report says. “Elimination in this context means sustained harm to the conditions that enable Palestinians to exist as a people with land, institutions, language, culture, social ties, leadership, shared memory and history, and a social and political horizon.
“It is an ongoing political project that seeks to re-organise space and the conditions of life so that Jewish presence becomes contiguous, institutionalised and permanent, while Palestinian existence becomes fragmented, vulnerable and dependent.”
One of the most obvious aspects of this fragmentation is the closure of roads and villages to Palestinian passage, and the abandonment of Palestinian homes under relentless pressure.
On a recent afternoon outside the village of Sair, Haytham Adnan Murad, 49, was returning from a hospital appointment in Hebron with his 12-year-old daughter, Balqis, who is unable to walk because of recent surgery. A gate to the road accessing his village, open in the morning, had been closed by the Israeli army, forcing Murad to get out of the taxi he was travelling in with his daughter and carry her around the roadblock.
“When we left, the gate was open. Now it’s closed, but this is daily life,” he said. “In the morning, things are different from the afternoon. There’s no dignity in this.”
The authors of the report link the acceleration of the Israeli assault on life in the West Bank to the period after the Hamas attack on southern Israeli communities on 7 October 2023. However, they argue that the underlying motivating ideology predates this period.
In particular they point to a document known as the Decisive Plan, published by Smotrich in 2017, before he became a minister, which they describe as the “key document for understanding Israeli action in the West Bank.”
 64
  Number of entire communities forcibly displaced in the West Bank since October 2023
B’Tselem says the plan gave Palestinians three options: “Live under Jewish supremacy while relinquishing their national claims, leave the territory, or face violent suppression by the Israeli military if they resist”. It has been translated, the report adds, “into policy, budgets, powers and practices exercised by different arms of the state” with “unprecedented speed and openness”.
As such, the report goes on, settler violence is no longer “operating only as private violence or a breach of public order” but as part of a “broader system of control”.
“It is sometimes carried out alongside soldiers, with the backing of security forces, using weapons, equipment and infrastructure supplied by the state, and with almost complete impunity.”
Beyond the already well-documented incidents of escalating settler violence, the report highlights other corrosive aspects of the tightening Israeli grip on the West Bank, including devastating economic restrictions, and impacts on education and culture.
 31.3%
Unemployment rate in the West Bank in 2024, up from 13.1% in 2022
Israel, it says, has enacted a systematic policy of “full economic strangulation” since 2023, which has denied livelihood opportunities, destroyed civilian and agricultural infrastructure, tightened control over resources and the banking system, and undermined the ability of public institutions to provide basic services.
Smotrich said in September 2025 he would employ every means possible “to prevent the danger of a Palestinian state, including bringing about the collapse of the Palestinian Authority … through economic strangulation”.
Mass work permit revocations have left workers and their families without a stable income. This is reflected in data from the West Bank cited in the report. The unemployment rate, for instance, rose from 13.1% in 2022 to 31.3% in 2024, and in 2025, nearly half of all young people were neither employed nor enrolled in education or vocational training.
The UN has estimated that in 2024, 700,000 Palestinians in the West Bank needed assistance to buy food, a 17% increase from the previous year.
Since October 2023, Israel has also intensified attacks on various Palestinian organisations and associations in the West Bank. Grassroots and community groups, as well as organisations that protect workers, people in need and children, have faced frequent raids on their offices, forced closure, systematic harassment and the arrest of employees, volunteers and activists without cause.
Cogat, the Israeli military unit responsible for overseeing civilian policy in the West Bank and Gaza, has been approached for comment.
 8 to 20%
Proportion of West Bank schools closed in the 2023-24 academic year
In an interview conducted to coincide with the report’s publication, Kareem Jubran, the head of B’Tselem’s field research on the West Bank, said: “What we uncovered is that these are not separate incidents. All the disparate elements have one character, one goal. When you look at the picture from above, it leads to one conclusion: that the main goal is to eliminate Palestinians as a collective.”
Yuli Novak, the executive director of B’Tselem, said: “What we are saying in the report is that this is ongoing. The project was there before, but now we are in a new phase.
“It started at the end of 2022, with the change of the government [to a right-far right coalition], and we saw another marked escalation after October 2023. What we are talking about is an attack on Palestinian life in the West Bank that changed in scale. It’s as much a quantitative element that has changed, making the West Bank more and more unlivable for many Palestinians.”
Novak described Smotrich’s plan from 2017 as the “blueprint”. “It shows the ideological and the operational way to get to what we are calling an elimination,” he said. “And he calls it victory by settlement. To win in a very final way so that nothing [such as a Palestinian state] can happen afterwards.”
All data and figures provided by B’Tselem

====================================================================================================

China to create more friendly environment for childbearing with better childcare services
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
15:23:02 CST
16:23:02 KST

The State Council Information Office holds a press conference on making decisive progress in the Healthy China initiative in Beijing, capital of China, Sept. 14, 2026. (Xinhua/Zhang Yuwei)
BEIJING, Sept. 14 (Xinhua) -- China will take further steps to support childbearing, including making childcare more accessible and affordable, a senior health official announced Monday, as the country seeks to ease costs of raising children and boost its birth rate.
Lei Haichao, head of the National Health Commission, told a media conference that continuous efforts will be made to promote the integrated development of services at kindergartens and nursery centers and to support communities and employers in setting up childcare facilities.
The aim is to ensure that families can access childcare services close to their homes or workplaces and at relatively affordable prices, Lei said.
The commission will work to raise coverage of childcare services among young children to effectively reduce families' costs of having, raising and educating children, the official said.
The country will also work to improve access to pediatric care at grassroots medical institutions, according to Lei.
By the end of this year, 1,000 more community-level medical and health institutions will be capable of offering pediatric care compared with last year, Lei said, adding that by that time, more than 95 percent of the country's township-level clinics and community health service centers are expected to have the capacity to provide relevant services.
Over 3,000 pediatricians have been sent from higher-level hospitals to grassroots medical institutions to strengthen pediatric services at the community level, Lei said.
China has developed a full-cycle maternity support system covering pre-pregnancy, pregnancy and childbirth, and child-rearing, with measures that include maternity allowances, childcare subsidies, medical insurance reimbursement and affordable childcare services to ease the financial burden on families with children.
The country introduced a nationwide childcare subsidy program in 2025, offering an annual tax-free subsidy of 3,600 yuan (about 530 U.S. dollars) for each child under the age of three. It also began waiving childcare and education fees for the final year of kindergarten in the 2025 autumn semester.
As of August, more than 25 million infants and toddlers have received childcare subsidies for 2026, and about 24 million children enrolled in kindergartens have benefited from the fee-waiving policy.
Also, 260 million people are currently covered by maternity insurance, and many regions are expanding coverage to allow flexible workers to enroll in maternity insurance alongside employee medical insurance.
These policies are among China's broader efforts to build a birth-friendly society amid declining birth rates and an aging population.
Building a "birth-friendly society" has become a key focus of China's 15th Five-Year Plan. The outline sets out measures, including incentives to encourage births and efforts to reduce the costs of childbirth, childcare and education.
It says the goal is to stabilize the number of newborns and support long-term balanced population development. ■

European gas price tops $1,000 first time since Dec 2022
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:22:29 UTC
16:22:29 KST

MOSCOW, September 14. /TASS/. The price of gas in Europe exceeded $1,000 per 1,000 cubic meters during trading on Monday for the first time since December 2022, according to data from London's ICE exchange.
The price of October futures at the TTF hub in the Netherlands rose to around $1,004 per 1,000 cubic meters, or 84.07 euros per MWh.
Meanwhile, spot gas prices at Europe's main trading hubs had already exceeded $1,000 per 1,000 cubic meters last week. In particular, day-ahead prices at the TTF hub reached $1,005, or 83.685 euros per MWh.
Gazprom noted that the approaching heating season is rapidly reducing the opportunity to replenish gas reserves in Europe's underground storage facilities, while gas prices have already reached levels seen during the energy crisis in late 2022.
European gas prices are rising amid record-low storage levels ahead of the coming winter and the ongoing conflict in the Middle East.
Russian President Vladimir Putin said earlier that gas prices in Europe could rise as high as $1,500 per 1,000 cubic meters.

====================================================================================================

LSA warns: unilateral grain transit restrictions will not stop Russian exports – the question is who will pay for the decision
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:22:19 UTC
16:22:19 KST

Unilateral restrictions imposed by Latvia on the transit of Russian- and Belarusian-origin grain would not stop Russian exports from reaching global markets but would instead redirect cargo to other transport corridors, while Latvia could lose legal cargo, port and railway revenues, international customers and competitiveness, the Latvian Stevedoring Companies Association (LSA) warns. The Association is urging policymakers to assess who would actually bear the cost of such a decision before introducing new restrictions.
The LSA stresses that Latvia’s security and support for Ukraine are priorities and calls on the state, at both national and European Union level, to use sanctions, controls and economic instruments that genuinely reduce Russia’s and Belarus’s revenues and their ability to finance the war. The LSA supports legally justified restrictions that achieve this objective. However, it warns against choosing instruments whose main economic consequences fall on Latvia, its international partners and global food supply chains if their impact on the aggressor states’ revenues is not supported by data.
The LSA stresses that grain stolen from the occupied territories of Ukraine, as well as sanctioned, illegal or unverifiable-origin cargo, has no place in Latvia’s transport system. Port companies cooperate with the Customs Board of the State Revenue Service, the Food and Veterinary Service (PVD), the Financial Intelligence Unit (FID) and other competent authorities to prevent such cargo from entering Latvia’s transport chain.
The LSA supports the government’s decision to strengthen controls over the origin of grain in transit, including by giving the PVD the authority to take samples from transit cargo and conduct laboratory tests, as well as further improvements to control mechanisms in cooperation with Ukraine and other EU countries. The industry is already cooperating with the competent authorities by providing the necessary information and support for cargo controls and traceability and is prepared to continue jointly strengthening and improving these mechanisms.
At the same time, the LSA urges against imposing a unilateral ban on the currently permitted transit of Russian- and Belarusian-origin grain to third countries, or introducing an economic instrument whose actual purpose would be to make such transit through Latvia impossible, before its real impact and consequences have been assessed. Unilateral measures by Latvia alone will not prevent Russian grain from reaching the global market – cargo will seek alternative transport corridors. Latvia, meanwhile, risks losing legal cargo, port and railway revenues, international customers and its competitiveness.
“If the objective is to reduce Russia’s revenues and its ability to finance the war, we must choose an instrument that actually achieves that objective. A unilateral Latvian decision that merely redirects legal international cargo to corridors in other countries while leaving Latvia with the economic losses does not achieve this goal,” the LSA stresses.
Making the transportation of Russian- or Belarusian-origin grain through Latvia more expensive does not automatically mean that Russia or Belarus will bear the additional cost. Grain is bought and traded on international markets by international companies, which then deliver it to third countries, including countries in Africa. Depending on the specific contract, the additional costs may be borne by the cargo seller, the international trader, a participant in the transport chain or the final buyer.
Therefore, the impact of a tariff on the aggressor states’ revenues cannot be determined solely on the basis of the cargo’s origin – it is necessary to assess who actually bears the additional costs throughout the transaction and supply chain.
A tariff as a disguised ban is not a solution
The LSA calls for a clear distinction between customs tariffs, railway infrastructure charges, port infrastructure fees and commercial service prices, as each of these instruments is governed by its own legal framework. Latvia operates within the common EU and international transport market. Therefore, before introducing an economic instrument designed to make cargo of a particular origin more expensive, its legal basis, proportionality, implementation mechanism and the party that will ultimately bear the additional costs must be assessed.
If the actual purpose of increasing charges is to ensure that legal cargo of a particular origin no longer chooses Latvia as a transit route, the compatibility of such an instrument with Latvian, EU and international law must be carefully assessed, as must the potential consequences for contracts that have already been concluded.
Such decisions may result not only in economic and reputational losses but also in legal challenges and claims for damages. When the European Union significantly increased customs tariffs on imports of Russian and Belarusian grain into the EU in 2024, it deliberately did not extend those restrictions to transit through the EU to third countries. EU regulations specifically state that this approach was chosen with global food security considerations also taken into account.
Cargo will not disappear – it will change route
Ports in the Baltic states and other countries in the region compete for international cargo flows. When choosing a transport corridor, cargo owners assess price, available capacity, delivery times, predictability and risks. If one corridor becomes significantly more expensive or unavailable, companies look for alternative routes.
On some routes, the price difference between Latvian and competing transport corridors is already only a few euros per tonne. Therefore, even relatively small changes in costs or risks can influence cargo owners’ choices.
At the same time, discussions on Russian grain transit are also taking place in the other Baltic states, with countries strengthening origin controls and considering further restrictions. Solutions should therefore be coordinated as much as possible at Baltic and European Union level to prevent cargo from simply being shifted from one country to another.
Latvia has an opportunity to attract new Central Asian cargo
The Central Asian route is particularly important and represents one of the strategically significant areas of cooperation for both Latvia and the European Union. Central Asian cargo already accounts for part of the cargo turnover at Latvian ports, and Latvia has opportunities to increase these volumes. In recent weeks, Kazakh partners have expressed interest in additional routes for shipments of Kazakh wheat and oilseed cargo through Baltic ports, including those in Latvia.
Restrictions on goods originating from Russia or Belarus do not in themselves apply to cargo originating from Kazakhstan. However, a significant share of these shipments depends on the Russian railway network, meaning that possible Russian countermeasures or disruptions to the transport corridor could also affect Central Asian cargo flows to Latvia.
It is in Latvia’s interests to develop a secure, legal and competitive transport corridor that is capable of strictly controlling cargo origin while preserving Latvia’s position in international supply chains.
Impact on global food supply chains must also be assessed
The issue of grain transit is not merely a matter of Latvia’s domestic politics – the consequences of decisions may also affect broader food supply chains.
In its latest food price assessment, the UN Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) states that disruptions to Black Sea trade logistics, alongside other factors, are putting upward pressure on international food commodity prices. At a time when traditional export routes through the Black Sea and the Sea of Azov are severely disrupted, market participants are seeking alternative export corridors, including routes through the Baltic Sea region.
Reducing the number of available transport routes or artificially increasing their cost may raise logistics costs and risks. Depending on contractual arrangements, these costs may be passed further along the supply chain and ultimately reach final buyers, including countries where imported grain is an important part of food security. There is therefore no basis for automatically assuming that the economic costs of a Latvian decision would be borne directly by Russia or Belarus.
Before introducing new restrictions, the LSA calls for their expected outcome and actual impact on Russian and Belarusian revenues to be substantiated, compared with the economic costs to Latvia, and assessed alongside alternative cargo routes, legal risks, implementation timelines and the consequences for existing contracts and international partnerships.
If, following a unilateral Latvian ban or economic restriction, Russian grain continues to reach the global market via another route while Latvia loses cargo, revenue and customers, Central Asian cargo flows are put at risk and the costs of international food supply chains increase, then such an instrument has failed to achieve its stated objective.
Action is necessary to prevent grain stolen in Ukraine, sanctioned grain or other illegal cargo from entering Latvia. However, the solution must be strict, effective and evidence-based controls that stop illegal cargo, rather than disrupting legal global food supply chains.

FT: Canada seeks to join €90 billion EU loan for Ukraine to strengthen ties with bloc
Canada is negotiating to support the EU's loan for Ukraine as Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney seeks to strengthen transatlantic ties and reduce his country's dependence on the United States.
Source: Financial Times sources, as reported by European Pravda
Details: The sources noted that both sides hope to agree on the size of Canada's contribution to the €90 billion loan for Ukraine before the EU summit in Montreal at the end of October.
The sources said Carney had sought to build an alliance of liberal states committed to a multilateral order that had been thrown into chaos by US President Donald Trump's actions. He also hoped to demonstrate his commitment to European allies by contributing to the Ukraine Support Loan.
The United Kingdom is currently the only other non-EU country to have contributed to the loan.
"We are grateful for all the support that Canada is providing to Ukraine and we are ready to do more together," one European official said.
Carney is travelling to Europe, where he will meet European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen in Strasbourg on 16 September. He is then expected to travel to Liverpool to meet the UK's new Prime Minister Andy Burnham.
"The EU wants the closest possible relationship with Canada and that is what we are working towards," the European official added.
Background:
- Recently, it emerged that EU member states had agreed to purchase US-made interceptor missiles for Ukraine's Patriot systems worth a total of €3.2 billion as part of the EU's €90 billion loan.
- The EU also noted that the €90 billion loan would cover only two-thirds of Ukraine's needs in 2026-2027.

Social Democrats to call for official inquiry into purchase of F-35s from US
Unknown

Social Democrats to call for official inquiry into purchase of F-35s from US
The left-wing Social Democratic Party says it will call for the creation of a parliamentary commission of inquiry (CPI) into the purchase of F-35 jets from the US. A parliamentary motion to this effect will be tabled following a debate in the parliamentary group.
The CPI will have to clarify a number of issues and ascertain whether there was individual misconduct or institutional failings within the relevant department, the party told the Keystone-ATS news agency on Sunday, confirming an article in the Sonntagszeitung.
The party also believes the CPI should investigate whether bidders were treated discriminatorily and whether the purchase and operating costs were deliberately distorted in favour of the F-35. It must also be clarified whether the defence ministry provided incorrect information regarding dependence on the supplier.
The matter is being pursued by Senator Franziska Roth, an expert on security policy. When asked about support from other parliamentary groups, the party replied that anyone wishing to restore confidence must ensure a “full clarification”.
More
F-35 jets: fixed-price scandal will have no immediate consequences
Last Thursday, former Social Democrat parliamentarian Pierre-Alain Fridez called for the creation of a parliamentary commission of inquiry, raising the suspicion that there is far more than simple misunderstanding behind the controversial information on the fixed price.
In an interview with Tamedia publications, he said he was convinced that those responsible had deliberately made false statements to secure approval for the purchase of the F-35 fighter jets.
The CPI is expected to receive the support of the Greens. But the chances of convincing the centre-right majority to set it up appear slim.

Oil prices rose by more than 2% after new strikes on Saudi Arabia

Himalayan ‘glacier bankruptcy’ threatens water security for 2 billion in Asia
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:20:20 UTC
16:20:20 KST

Himalayan ‘glacier bankruptcy’ threatens water security for 2 billion in Asia
Glacier mass loss is causing the region to approach ‘peak water’, the point when river flows stop rising and start declining
The Himalayas are approaching a tipping point as glaciers melt faster than a decade ago, threatening water security as the region approaches “peak water” by mid-century, according to a study released this month.
As glaciers retreat, they are leaving behind unstable glacial lakes held back by little more than loose rock and ice, above valleys where millions of people live, the study found.
The study was produced by Systemiq, a sustainability advisory firm, together with the Integrated Mountain Initiative, the International Centre for Integrated Mountain Development, and India’s G.B. Pant National Institute of Himalayan Environment.
Himalayan glacier mass loss has accelerated in recent decades, while only 21 glaciers were currently monitored on the ground out of an estimated 40,000 glaciers across the Hindu Kush-Himalaya region, the study found.

Hong Kong consumer watchdog questions value of supermarket promotional deals
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:20:03 UTC
16:20:03 KST

Hong Kong consumer watchdog questions supermarkets’ buy-one-get-one-free deals
Consumer Council urges shoppers to compare unit prices after finding some promotional offers gave smaller-than-expected discounts or no savings at all
Hong Kong shoppers may have saved less than they expected – or nothing at all – through supermarkets’ “buy-one-get-one-free” and bulk-buy promotions, the city’s consumer watchdog has warned, urging residents to compare unit prices instead of relying on promotional labels.
The Consumer Council on Monday released the findings of a survey of 222 products sold by AEON, ParknShop and Wellcome between September 2025 and July this year. It found that the actual discounts offered in some promotional campaigns were less attractive than consumers might expect.
During the period, 48 products at two supermarkets were offered under bulk-buy promotional campaigns, such as ‘Buy 1 Get 1 Free’, ‘Choose Any 2 and Get 1 Free’ and ‘Get 1 More for $1’.
But for some of these products, the single-item price during the promotional period was higher than during non-promotional periods, meaning the actual discount fell well short of the roughly 50 per cent consumers might expect, according to the council.
The findings add to long-standing concerns over whether supermarket multi-buy promotions always offer the savings suggested by their labels.
“We hope the supermarkets in Hong Kong and grocery stores can list their unit price on their offline or online shops to make it easier for consumers to choose the products they want,” said Alaina Shum Jiu-fai, the watchdog’s chief executive.
“Hong Kong is a free market, and the merchants have the flexibility to adjust the prices anytime, but it’s not a prudent move to adjust the price immediately before the promotion – it will affect the consumers’ confidence.”

Russian Drone Strike Hits Kyiv-Warsaw Train Just 2 Kilometers From Polish Border
A Russian drone strike hit the locomotive of a Kyiv-Warsaw passenger train in Ukraine’s Volyn region on September 13, approximately two kilometers from the Polish border.
According to state railway operator Ukrzaliznytsia, no injuries were reported. Ukrzaliznytsia said a monitoring team had warned the train crew about the threat in advance.
We bring you stories from the ground. Your support keeps our team in the field.
The same Russian attack also struck infrastructure near the Yahodyn-Dorohusk international border crossing, one of the main road links between Ukraine and Poland.
Ukrainian Foreign Minister Andrii Sybiha reported the strike near the Yahodyn crossing on September 13. Valentyna Chernysh, a spokesperson for the Volyn Customs Service, told Suspilne that falling drone debris caused a fire at a nearby gas station and several trucks parked at the site.
Border operations were temporarily suspended during the air raid alert while personnel and other people at the crossing took shelter. The Yahodyn-Dorohusk checkpoint later resumed normal operations.
Sybiha said the strikes near the Polish border amounted to Putin’s terror “knocking” directly on the doors of the EU and NATO and called on Ukraine’s partners to impose broader sanctions on Russia.
“Act now: throw the full weight of your sanctions at Putin’s aggressive regime. Make Moscow feel the consequences of its terror. Not just half-decisions, but uncompromising, powerful steps. Full trade embargo. Full entry bans. Full sanctions on the military-industrial complex,” Sybiha wrote.
Earlier, on September 12, Russia launched a large-scale missile and drone attack across Ukraine, killing at least four people and injuring civilians in Odesa, Kryvyi Rih, Zaporizhzhia, and the Kyiv region.

Norway weighs up to 3 years in prison for trade with Israeli settlers in West Bank
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:13:18 UTC
16:13:18 KST

Norway is considering legislation that could impose prison sentences of up to three years on individuals or companies that trade with Israeli settlers in the West Bank, taking proposed restrictions on settlement commerce further than measures announced by a group of other Western countries.
Foreign Minister Espen Barth Eide told Norwegian broadcaster NRK that he expects parliament, the Storting, to approve legislation this fall targeting trade with Israeli settlers in what Norway considers the occupied West Bank.
The proposal, being advanced by the Foreign Ministry in Oslo, would apply to both individuals and companies. A public consultation on the measure is scheduled to end on September 19.
It would also apply to East Jerusalem, which Israel considers an inseparable part of its capital, as well as to construction and infrastructure services connected to settlements.
Eide acknowledged that Norway’s direct trade with settlements is limited and consists mainly of products such as wine and agricultural goods. He described the initiative as carrying primarily political and symbolic significance.
Jørgen Jensehaugen, a senior researcher at the Peace Research Institute Oslo, described the proposal as the lowest level of sanctions that could be imposed because it targets a relatively narrow range of economic activity rather than Israel’s economy more broadly.
He argued that wider measures against companies operating in settlements could have a significantly greater economic effect because of the close links between Israel’s wider economy and settlement activity.
‘Every Jew here will become a criminal’
The proposal has drawn sharp criticism from some Israelis living in Norway.
On Elpeleg, an Israeli resident who monitors antisemitic rhetoric in the country, said the key difference between Norway and other states considering settlement restrictions was Oslo’s willingness to impose criminal penalties.
“The meaning of Eide’s remarks is clear: This will turn every Jew here into a criminal,” he said. “Anyone who buys a bottle of wine or services from the settlements risks going to prison.”
He accused the Norwegian foreign minister of pursuing an extreme anti-Israel policy, saying, “I think the Norwegian foreign minister, who won an ‘antisemite of the year’ award here, has lost the plot.”
The allegation reflects Elpeleg’s characterization of Eide and is not part of the proposed legislation itself, which is framed around commercial activity linked to settlements rather than Jewish identity.
Part of a broader European push
Britain said last week that it would introduce legislation banning imports from settlements. Canada, Denmark, Finland, France, Iceland, Ireland, Poland, Portugal, Spain, Sweden and Norway have also said they would pursue measures in the same direction.
Eide argued that the international environment surrounding Israel is changing, including in Washington. He said the Trump administration had responded relatively cautiously to the British initiative, saying that while the United States was not adopting a similar policy, it understood the underlying concerns reflected by the measure.
According to Eide, that response carried particular weight because Washington has historically provided Israel with strong diplomatic backing against international pressure.
He also argued that Israel’s ability to dismiss or deter criticism by invoking antisemitism was weakening.
Jensehaugen said the measures were also intended to increase the economic cost of Israel’s continued presence in the West Bank, linking the growing international pressure to developments in both the West Bank and Gaza.
Eide framed the effort as part of a broader shift in which middle-sized countries may increasingly act together when larger powers do not uphold what they see as international law and human rights standards.
Norway’s proposal remains under review and has not yet been enacted. If approved in its current form, however, it would mark one of the toughest criminal enforcement mechanisms yet proposed by a Western government against trade linked to Israeli settlements.

====================================================================================================

Sweden heads to polls as welfare shapes race and right-wing seeks power
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:12:48 UTC
16:12:48 KST

Sweden heads to the polls Sunday in a closely contested general election that could determine whether the right-wing Sweden Democrats move from supporting the government from outside to becoming part of it.
Opinion polls have pointed to a victory for the center-left opposition led by the Social Democrats, but the governing center-right bloc has narrowed the gap in the final weeks of campaigning, leaving the outcome uncertain.
The Sweden Democrats, once isolated by Sweden's established parties because of their far-right roots, have become one of the country's largest political forces and a central player in the right-wing bloc.
The party is now seeking to go beyond supporting the government from outside and enter it itself.
Yet unlike the previous two elections, when migration and gang violence dominated political debate, this campaign has been increasingly defined by healthcare, education, welfare and national security.
Nicholas Aylott, associate professor at Stockholm's Sodertorn University, told Anadolu that "levels of migration have fallen in Sweden quite noticeably … and the rate of criminal violence, which was very high four years ago, is not as high now."
As a result, healthcare, education and elderly care have emerged as some of the issues most important to voters, according to PerOla Oberg, professor at Uppsala University.
The Social Democrats, who governed Sweden for much of the modern era before losing power in 2022, have sought to capitalize on those concerns by presenting themselves as defenders of the welfare state.
The party has proposed higher taxes on banks and high-income earners to finance investment in healthcare and schools while supporting households struggling with rising costs and economic inequality.
Sweden has spent recent years grappling with organized criminal networks, shootings and bombings, prompting governments to introduce tougher legislation and expand police and judicial powers.
Aylott said the Social Democrats would probably maintain much of the government's existing law-and-order policy if they returned to power.
Meanwhile, the Sweden Democrats have pushed for even tougher measures.
This convergence means the election is no longer simply a contest between parties offering fundamentally different approaches to crime and migration.
Instead, voters are increasingly being asked which parties they trust to manage security while also protecting the welfare state.
"This election may therefore be based on who you trust," Aylott said.
The most consequential political development remains the rise of the Sweden Democrats.
For years, mainstream parties sought to isolate the party.
However, the Sweden Democrats' transformation accelerated after the 2022 election, when the party became a key parliamentary ally of Prime Minister Ulf Kristersson's center-right government.
Despite remaining outside the Cabinet, they gained substantial influence over government policy, particularly on migration and law and order.
That arrangement could now change.
The four parties of the right-wing bloc have agreed that the Sweden Democrats can participate in a government if they secure a parliamentary majority.
For the party, that would represent a historic transition from parliamentary outsider to government participant.
Aylott said the Sweden Democrats have become "a very central part of Swedish political debate today."
The party has become "normalized to quite a large degree," although it remains controversial.
Oberg echoed the view. "The Sweden Democrats’ leaders have been very successful in transforming the party from a fringe group into a political party ready to act responsibly."
At the same time, the party has broadened its electoral appeal, with its support now appearing relatively stable at around 20%.
Swedish parties must cross a 4% threshold to secure representation in parliament through the national vote.
The Liberal Party has struggled to reach that level in opinion polls throughout much of the current parliamentary term.
If the Liberals fail to cross the threshold, the consequences for the right-wing bloc could be severe.
Their votes would not translate into parliamentary seats, potentially depriving the governing bloc of the numbers it needs to retain power.
Even if the right wins the popular vote among the major blocs, losing the Liberals could make forming a government considerably more difficult.
For the opposition, the challenge is almost the mirror image.
The Social Democrats could emerge as the largest party but still struggle to form a majority.
The opposition bloc consists of parties with significant ideological differences.
The Left Party wants representation in any government it supports, while the Center Party has said it will not participate in a Cabinet together with the Left Party.
That leaves Social Democrat leader Magdalena Andersson facing a difficult balancing act.
Aylott said the Social Democrats could need support from three other opposition parties to secure a parliamentary majority.
"The challenge for the Social Democrats would be to give the Left Party enough of what it wants, while at the same time keeping the Center Party .... also happy," he said.
One possible outcome is a minority government.
Under Sweden's "negative parliamentarism" system, a government can take office as long as a majority of lawmakers does not vote against it.
That could allow the Social Democrats to lead a government with the Greens while relying on outside support from the Left and Center parties.
Oberg said a stronger showing by the Center Party could also make a broader Social Democrat-Green-Center coalition possible.
Despite the opposition's polling advantage, the election is far from settled.
Three polls in the final week have put the four left-wing parties at roughly 50%, compared with around 47% for the four parties on the right.
The difference is narrow enough for the result to remain uncertain.
Oberg estimated that, based on their electoral strength, the Sweden Democrats could potentially seek as many as 12 ministerial positions.
That could place them in charge of major portfolios and make the party directly responsible for policy rather than merely influential from outside government.
If the right-wing bloc wins, government formation could be relatively straightforward.
The existing coalition would have a clear incentive to continue its relationship with the Sweden Democrats, although their demand for formal ministerial representation could generate difficult negotiations.
If the opposition wins, the process could be considerably more complicated.
The Social Democrats would have to navigate competing demands from the Left and Center parties while potentially relying on the Greens.
Sweden has experienced prolonged government negotiations before.
After the 2018 election, it took 134 days to form a government -- an exceptionally long period by Swedish standards.
Aylott does not expect negotiations to last that long this time, but he cautioned that a left-wing victory could produce an extended period of bargaining.
"There'll be lots of bluff, and there'll be lots of angry demands, and there'll be lots of complaints," he said. "And it might take time."
There is also a slim possibility that one of the right-wing parties could support a Social Democrat-led government without formally joining it.
Aylott regards such a scenario as unlikely but not impossible.
Whatever the result, analysts expect the election to produce evolution rather than revolution.

Russian ‘inspector’ satellite falls to Earth
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:11:29 UTC
16:11:29 KST

- Russia's Kosmos-2558, a co-orbital "inspector" satellite that shadowed a classified U.S. spy satellite since 2022, reentered the atmosphere on September 12, 2026.
- Satellite tracker Jonathan McDowell said the reentered object may not have been the real satellite, since a separate object, Object C, is still making orbital maneuvers.
A Russian satellite built to maneuver alongside and inspect other countries’ spacecraft up close fell back to Earth on September 12, but the object that came down may not have been the real satellite, according to Jonathan McDowell, an astrophysicist at the Harvard-Smithsonian Center for Astrophysics who tracks satellite activity.
The satellite, Kosmos-2558, launched from Plesetsk Cosmodrome on August 1, 2022, aboard a Soyuz-2-1v rocket. It belongs to Nivelir, a Russian military program of co-orbital “inspector” satellites, built to fly in close formation with a target spacecraft rather than photograph territory from a distance like a conventional reconnaissance satellite.
Within days of launch, independent satellite trackers found that Kosmos-2558’s orbital plane matched that of USA-326, a classified U.S. National Reconnaissance Office satellite launched earlier that year. Over the following three years, Kosmos-2558 repeatedly adjusted its orbit to stay close to USA-326, at one point passing within about 31 kilometers (19 miles) of the American satellite in April 2023.
Kosmos-2558 stopped performing its regular orbit-maintenance burns around April 2025, and its orbit began decaying naturally, according to space journalist Anatoly Zak. On June 26, 2025, U.S. Space Force trackers cataloged a new object, designated 2022-089C, that appeared to separate from the inactive satellite. That object, referred to by trackers as Object C, has continued making its own engine burns since, including a maneuver in July 2025 that brought it within roughly 81 kilometers (50 miles) of USA-326.
McDowell said he still questions whether Object C is actually the original Kosmos-2558 spacecraft, and whether what U.S. trackers had been following as Kosmos-2558 since April 2025 was really a discarded module or a smaller sub-satellite. He pointed to the fact that Russia never registered an additional payload for the mission with the United Nations, as required for new satellites, which he said supports that theory. Russian authorities have not commented on the satellite’s status or on McDowell’s assessment.
This is not the first Nivelir-family satellite to draw scrutiny from U.S. officials. In July 2020, a related satellite, Kosmos-2543, released a high-speed object near another Russian satellite in an incident U.S. Space Command said at the time amounted to a weapons test. “Russia conducted a non-destructive test of a space-based anti-satellite weapon,” the command said in a statement. Russia has described its Nivelir-series satellites as inspection systems meant to monitor the condition of its own spacecraft.
It remains unclear what became of the reentered object’s mission equipment, whether Object C carries any sensors of its own, or whether Russia plans further maneuvers near USA-326. Object C was continuing its periodic orbit-boosting burns as of McDowell’s September 13 update.

Nanwang Sisters showcase Taiwan Indigenous culture in Canada
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:09:03 UTC
16:09:03 KST

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — Pinuyumayan (Puyuma) Indigenous music group Nanwang Sisters and Pinuyumayan scholar Lin Sung-en (林頌恩) recently visited Toronto and Vancouver to share Taiwan Indigenous culture.
They also engaged in in-depth exchanges with Canadian Indigenous communities, CNA reported. Taiwan’s Council of Indigenous Peoples and the Canada Council for the Arts supported the exchanges.
The Nanwang Sisters blend traditional Pinuyumayan vocal rhythms with modern pop harmonies, creating a powerful and distinctive musical style, per CNA. Their performances were among the highlights of this year’s TAIWANfest in Canada. The group said connecting with Canadian Indigenous communities and cultures was an especially unforgettable experience.
Guitarist Wang Chih-po (汪智博) said the group reunited in Toronto with Indigenous Canadian singer Aysanabee, whom they met during his visit to Taiwan last year. Wang said Aysanabee observed that although Canada and Taiwan are far apart, their Indigenous peoples share remarkably similar historical experiences.
Aysanabee said Indigenous peoples in both places had previously been prevented from using their native languages or identifying themselves by their Indigenous group names, but are now able to speak proudly and share their stories in their own languages.
Lin, a research fellow at the National Museum of Prehistory, said she was moved by the passion of Canadian Indigenous youth working to preserve their cultures, per CNA. She said that at Indigenous cultural centers in Whistler and on Vancouver Island, she met young Indigenous people who spoke confidently about their cultures and their grandparents’ experiences.
Both Wang and Lin admired Canada’s efforts to acknowledge past injustices toward Indigenous peoples and its ongoing reconciliation policies.

All your docs in one app? New EU digital wallet could solve admin issues
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:08:59 UTC
16:08:59 KST

Would you trust a single, EU-backed digital service to hold all your legal, professional, and medical information? The EUDI Wallet wants to simplify administrative tasks by storing citizens’ documents in a single system that works across all member states.
Today, moving, working or studying in another EU country can involve separate national systems and a lot of paperwork. So, the EU has come up with the EU Digital Identity (EUDI) Wallet, a government-backed mobile app that will let people and businesses prove their identity online and in person across the entire European Union. The goal is to make cross-border services easier to use.
It's designed to store all official digital credentials in one place. These can include a national ID, driver's licence, medical prescriptions and academic diplomas. It can also support electronic signatures, payment methods and business certificates. Tasks such as opening a bank account, renting a car or enrolling at a university abroad should become simpler to complete digitally.
Each member state will provide its own wallet, built to common European standards so the different national versions can work across borders. Every EU country is required to make at least one certified wallet available by December 2026.
The project is raising concerns, with privacy and civil rights groups questioning whether putting all identity information into one digital system increases the risk of cyberattacks, surveillance or the wider use of biometric data.
Tere are practical concerns too. Some countries already have advanced national digital identity systems, and adapting them to a new EU-wide framework may be costly and complex.
Will you use this wallet when it becomes available in your country? Do you think it’s a good idea? Our poll is anonymous and takes only a few seconds to complete. The results will be featured across XL's EU-wide coverage, including videos, articles and newsletters, and will help shape our reporting as we examine how Europe can secure its position in the age of artificial intelligence.

Outrage in Australia over One Nation leader's racist remarks about Indigenous people

Faisal Mahmud
14 September 2026•Update: 14 September 2026
Australian lawmakers and Indigenous figures have condemned One Nation leader Pauline Hanson over resurfaced remarks in which she described Indigenous Australians as the "most primitive race on Earth."
Hanson made the comments during a podcast appearance last year while discussing the teaching of Indigenous history and culture in Australian schools, ABC News Australia reported Monday.
Indigenous Australians Minister Malarndirri McCarthy called the remarks "totally outrageous" and "completely racist," saying such rhetoric was divisive, and urged Hanson to apologize.
Indigenous author, union official and human rights activist Thomas Mayo described Hanson's comments as "shockingly racist" and "ignorantly wrong," while saying they were unsurprising given her political record.
Liberal Senator Kerrynne Liddle also condemned the remarks as "disgraceful," while Senator Jacinta Nampijinpa Price accused Hanson of "wilful ignorance."
One Nation, a right-wing populist minor party founded and led by Hanson, has long advocated tighter immigration controls and campaigned against multiculturalism.
The controversy emerged as One Nation unveiled proposals to sharply reduce migration, including cuts to visas for international students and graduates and restrictions affecting family members of students and migrant workers.
Hanson has been a prominent and frequently controversial figure in Australian politics since entering federal parliament in the 1990s.

Swedish election remains too close to call as left-wing opposition regains 3-seat lead
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:07:14 UTC
16:07:14 KST

Melike Pala
14 September 2026•Update: 14 September 2026
The distribution of parliamentary seats in Sweden swung back and forth between the opposition left-wing and the right-wing ruling parties overnight as vote counting continued following Sunday's general election.
At 5 am local time (0300 GMT) Monday, the opposition held 176 seats compared with 173 for the four-party right-wing bloc supporting current Prime Minister Ulf Kristersson's government, according to public broadcaster SVT.
Around 94% of the votes had been counted at that point, with the preliminary count set to continue until Wednesday with the inclusion of late-arriving early votes and ballots cast abroad.
The final distribution of seats could therefore still change, leaving the outcome of the next government uncertain.
The newly elected parliament is due to convene on Sep. 28 to conduct a roll call and elect a speaker.
The earliest the speaker can propose a candidate for prime minister is Sep. 29.
The proposed prime minister must then secure sufficient support in the parliament. If more than half of the 349-member parliament votes against the candidate, the proposal is rejected and the speaker begins a new round of consultations with party leaders.
Kristersson said on Sunday night that the election result remained unclear but argued that voters had sent a clear message.
He said he would contact other party leaders to explore whether a functioning government could be formed, adding that the process could be complicated and take time.
The balance of power also appeared largely unchanged in Sweden's three largest municipalities: Stockholm, Gothenburg and Malmo.
Voter turnout also appeared set to decline for a second consecutive election.
Preliminary figures at around 1.30 am local time indicated turnout of approximately 80.4%.
Turnout stood at 84% in the 2022 election, down from 87% in 2018.

Woman spared jail over forging PhD reference letter for Education University
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

16:04:09 KST

Woman spared jail over forging PhD reference letter for Education University
Mainland Chinese student was ordered to perform 160 hours of community service after admitting she used an altered letter to secure admission
A woman convicted of using a forged reference letter to gain admission to a Hong Kong doctoral programme has been spared jail and ordered to perform 160 hours of community service.
Fanling Magistrate Kenneth Chan Ping-chau said on Monday that he had decided to “make an exception” for 31-year-old Li Zuer, who earlier admitted falsely claiming that a former teaching staff member at the Education University of Hong Kong (EdUHK) had written a reference letter for her in March 2024.
The letter praised the mainland Chinese woman for demonstrating “exceptional academic performance”, but was actually drafted by a Shenzhen-based agent whom she had commissioned.
The original letter, written by Li’s teacher Ho Wai-yip in 2021, noted that she still had room to improve her English proficiency. Li subsequently applied unsuccessfully for doctoral programmes several times.
She was eventually admitted to the programme last year after submitting the altered letter. But when university staff conducted an inquiry, Ho confirmed that he had not signed the letter.
Li pleaded guilty to one count of obtaining services by deception.

France submits new proposal to ban social media for under-15s

France submits new proposal to ban social media for under-15s
PARIS – The French government submitted a reworked proposal to ban social media for children, after its previous ban was struck down by the country’s top constitutional authority, President Emmanuel Macron said on Sept 14.
France’s Constitutional Council struck down the previous legislation in mid-August, finding that part of it constituted an unnecessary infringement on the freedom of expression of those affected.
“After rigorous technical work the government today is notifying” the European Commission of the new draft, Macron wrote on X.
The notification is a key step, as it ensures that the legislation is in line with European Union laws.
Macron has made banning social media use for children and teens one of his top priorities and hopes to see a working ban in force by the time he leaves office in 2027.
He has called for similar legislation to be adopted throughout the European Union.
EU chief Ursula von der Leyen is due to address a possible bloc-wide social media ban for children in a speech in Brussels this week.
A growing number of countries are taking steps to restrict social media access amid multiplying warnings over its harmful effects on children. AFP

Swedish centre-left opposition in pole position to take power after tight election
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:01:36 UTC

Swedish centre-left opposition in pole position to take power after tight election
STOCKHOLM, Sept 14 - Sweden's centre-left opposition on Monday looked set to form the next government as preliminary election results showed it holding a narrow three-seat lead over the right-wing ruling parties with most of the votes counted after Sunday's vote.
A win for the opposition would halt a slide to the right over the last four years which has transformed Sweden from one of Europe's most welcoming countries for immigrants to one with among the toughest asylum regimes.
Left-leaning parties headed by Social Democrats leader Magdalena Andersson were projected to win 176 seats in the 349-seat parliament while Prime Minister Ulf Kristersson's right-wing bloc was seen winning 173 seats, the election authority said.
"If this result remains valid, Sweden will get a new government," Andersson told supporters after the preliminary results were published on Sunday night.
The far-right Sweden Democrats, who hoped to join a government for the first time in the event of a win for the ruling bloc, lost an estimated 11 seats, projections showed.
Party leaders, however, have been cautious about claiming victory or accepting defeat with 353 election districts still to be counted and overseas votes not likely to be tallied until Wednesday.
In 2022, exit polls initially gave a narrow victory to the centre-left which proved premature and Kristersson showed no sign of conceding when he spoke after Sunday's election.
The blocs were separated by just 0.45% of votes on Monday morning.
If a centre-left win is confirmed, forming a government could take some time.
While Social Democrat leader Magdalena Andersson will be prime minister, it remains unclear which other parties will be in her government. The Centre Party has refused to share power with the Left Party which says it must be a part of any coalition.
"It could very well be a complicated process to form a government," the speaker of the parliament, Andreas Norlen, told Swedish television.
The result of the election is unlikely to change the course of Sweden's pro-Western and pro-Ukraine stance and its effect on immigration and economic policy is unclear.
Andersson has promised voters more spending on welfare, a fairer tax system and a focus on social cohesion. But while the Greens and Left parties want the rich to contribute more, the Centre Party is against a billionaires' tax.
The Social Democrats are unlikely to ease immigration rules, as campaigned for by the Left and Green parties, with Andersson saying Sweden has to continue its tough line. REUTERS

National campaign aims to protect Swiss youth from online abuse
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

07:00:47 UTC
16:00:47 KST

National campaign aims to protect Swiss youth from online abuse
The NGO Child Protection Switzerland has launched a national campaign to prevent online abuse. The initiative aims to raise awareness and provide appropriate practical strategies.
From gaming platforms to social media, WhatsApp messages to research for school: children use the internet every day, often for many hours and across several platforms simultaneously. This digital presence also carries risks that adults are not aware of, according to the organisation Child Protection Switzerland.
Online abuse is a growing problem. Swiss online reporting service clickandstop.ch has received more than 10,000 reports of child sexual abuse material since its launch in 2022. More than one in four teenagers in Switzerland is asked to send intimate photos, according to the 2024 JAMES study.
More
Swiss youngsters targeted by deepfakes and sextortion
Setting clear rules
Under the theme “Being there”, the campaign aims to encourage adults to listen to young people, agree on clear rules for using digital media and lead by example. A video illustrates how adults can ensure young people’s safety in the digital world.
“Children and young people have the right to safe digital spaces, free from exploitation, violence and manipulation,” says Child Protection Switzerland in a statement. The NGO believes that the major platforms are not fully assuming their responsibilities.
It is therefore calling for effective legal provisions in the draft Federal Act on Communication Platforms (LPCom). The organisation believes that the responsibility for protecting children should not rest solely with parents.
The campaign is being run in collaboration with several partners, including the Federal Office of Police (fedpol), Swiss Crime Prevention (PSC) and the national “Youth and Media” platform run by the Federal Office of Social Insurance (FOSI).

====================================================================================================

Suicide rates are gradually falling in the EU, but men are still more drastically affected than women
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:00:39 UTC
16:00:39 KST

Mental health is a growing political and social priority in Europe, including suicide prevention, and the numbers have been slightly falling across the EU. However, member states are not affected equally.
More than 48,000 EU residents died as a result of intentional self-harm in 2023, according to the latest Eurostat data — a number that has been slowly dropping over the past decade.
It is equivalent to a standardised death rate of 10.4 per 100,000 people, declining slightly from 10.6 in 2022 and 12.2 in 2013.
The number of deaths by suicide per 100,000 residents was highest in Lithuania (18.8), Slovenia (16.4) and Hungary (16.1).
In contrast, the lowest rates were registered in Cyprus (3.5), Greece (4.3) and Malta (5.5).
"Suicide is complex and most of the time there isn't one event or factor that leads someone to take their own life," according to Samaritans, the suicide prevention charity.
"It is usually a combination of lots of different factors interacting with each other to increase risk. A combination of individual, community, and societal factors contributes to the risk of suicide."
Who is the most affected?
Suicide rates are higher among men than women.
The ratio of the number of suicides during 2023 to the average population was 16.9 per 100,000 men, while it was five per 100,000 women.
Men accounted for 76.4% of all deaths by suicide in the EU, with the share ranging from 70.2% in the Netherlands to 90.3% in Malta.
There isn't a clear explanation for why suicide tends to affect men so much more than women. However, part of it may be down to the way that certain risk factors for suicide affect men differently from how they affect women, according to Samaritans.
For instance, social expectations placed on men mean that they may be less likely to disclose or seek help for suicidal thoughts or behaviours. Relationship breakdown also increases suicide risk more significantly in men, Samaritans said.
"Other risk factors may be more common in one gender — for example, women are more likely to self-harm than men," the charity added.
Age is another factor that tends to affect suicide rates, according to Eurostat, with the ratio of the number of suicides to the average population increasing as they get older.
Those who were 65 and older registered a ratio of 17.3 per 100,000 people, while young people under 15 registered a death rate of 0.2 per 100,000 people.
Men had higher rates in all age groups, except among children under 15, where the rates were similar.
What is the EU doing to address mental health issues?
Since June 2023, the European Commission has bolstered its approach to mental health, recognising its link to "social and economic resilience".
The EU is addressing suicide prevention through a raft of mental health initiatives, backed by more than €1.2 billion worth of investment in EU4Health, Horizon Europe and Erasmus+.
The Commission has also backed member states with teacher training, peer-support models, and tools to detect and respond to mental distress early.
If you are contemplating suicide and need to talk, please reach out to Befrienders Worldwide, an international organisation with helplines in 32 countries. Visit befrienders.org to find the telephone number for your location.

====================================================================================================

Phone coverage across the London tube? That’s sent me hurtling down a tunnel of despair | Lucy Pasha-Robinson
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:00:25 UTC
16:00:25 KST

Rejoice! For by the end of 2026, 4G and 5G mobile coverage is expected across the whole London underground network. After months of delays and setbacks, 121 subterranean tube stations will be dragged kicking and screaming into the 21st century, joining Paris and Berlin in perma-connectivity bliss. But should we really be so happy about the rollout?
Going deep underground was once a way of escaping the incessant communications of this exhausting modern life. Emails to respond to? WhatsApp messages piling up? The tube used to be one of the last remaining bastions of resistance against the constant expectation to do. To optimise. A simpler place where you could justifiably and believably ignore messages and reply later with a “sorry, I was on the tube”.
I see the economic arguments for being better connected. Of course it’s good for business for us to be “on” all the time. And yes, I’ll admit, it is stressful when you get stuck in a tunnel and can’t let your boss know you’re running late. The rollout will also host the new Emergency Services Network (ESN), which will allow first responders to access vital information in an emergency – you can’t argue with that. And, credit where credit is due, the project inarguably represents a remarkable feat of engineering, involving 700 staff and the rollout of hundreds of miles of fibre cabling in the tube’s ancient tunnels.
But on an individual level, what good comes from permanent online connectedness? Studies have shown it’s really bad for our health. Perhaps you’ll be able to leave the office earlier if you’ve got ahead with your work during your morning commute. But it horrifies me that if clients want an instant response, or a bad boss is contacting you out of hours, there’ll soon be nowhere to plausibly hide. I know, I know, lots of people are already living in this brave new world. The tube has 60% connectivity as it is, and lots of commuters who use train networks outside London are used to tethering to a phone or connecting to the train wifi to get ahead on work during their journey. But wasn’t our inability to do the same once a huge point in Londoners’ favour? Yes, the tube is a sweaty sardine can but at least you could lose yourself in a (pre-downloaded) podcast or a book for half an hour, instead of fielding Slack messages from your colleagues.
I can’t understand why we aren’t all more perturbed by this frightening normalisation of being always on. We should be railing against it with all our might. Why must every minute of our time be accounted for? Boredom and daydreaming are good for our brains – we should all be doing more of that.
I’m aware that I sound like a luddite. And I know lots of people find it exceedingly useful being able to work through their to-do list in those idle minutes between leaving the house and arriving somewhere else. But it’s becoming abundantly clear with every new alarmist study that we need saving from ourselves when it comes to our phone addiction. After a day at work and one too many soul-sucking conversations with colleagues, who has the self-discipline not to doom scroll all the way down the Central line? Not me, that’s for sure.
And people can’t be trusted to use this newfound perma-connectivity politely either. My colleague Dan Hancox has recently been bemoaning the “astonishing number of grown adults who think it’s OK to surf the slopwave without headphones” – a phenomenon that has become noticeably and obnoxiously worse since the tube got internet. And don’t get me started on the number of people talking on the phone on loudspeaker these days. Must we all be made to listen to everyone’s personal conversations on the tube now too? Is this the future?
Give it up, I hear you say. That ship has already sailed. Maybe. But I don’t have to be pleased about it.
- 
  Lucy Pasha-Robinson is a Guardian assistant Opinion editor

How heatwaves are changing Switzerland’s major lakes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

How heatwaves are changing Switzerland’s major lakes
The summer of 2026 marked a turning point for Switzerland’s big lakes. Due to the heat and exceptional levels of sunshine, the waters of Lake Geneva and other major lakes reached record temperatures with consequences for ecosystems and leisure activities.
This summer, aquatic vegetation has proliferated to an extraordinary extent in the Geneva harbour, the wide inlet of Lake Geneva facing the city. At Collonge-Bellerive, the expanses of macrophytes have grown to an extent rarely seen in the past, to the point where the cantonal technical teams responsible for clearing passages for boats and keeping bathing areas clear have had to be called out almost daily.
Staff responsible for keeping aquatic vegetation in check at the far end of the lake also observed a higher number of dead fauna than in previous years, particularly fish and waterbirds. Experts believe these phenomena are compounded by other worrying signs, such as algal blooms, the presence of cyanobacteria and the spread of invasive species.
3.5°C above average in Lake Geneva
Data collected from Lake Geneva confirm the scale of the phenomenon. Between the surface and a depth of 10 metres, the average temperature in August reached 24°C, which is 3.5°C higher than the average recorded between 1991 and 2020. Even the deeper waters have warmed, with an increase of around 1°C. The figures released by the International Commission for the Protection of the Waters of Lake Geneva show levels never seen before.
One of the most visible effects of the warming waters is the proliferation of filamentous algae along the shores. These gelatinous masses, observed in large quantities throughout Lake Geneva, do not, however, pose a danger. Like the green mats of macrophytes, however, they are a considerable nuisance to bathers, some of whom report having been bitten by so-called “duck fleas” more than in any other summer.
Cyanobacteria are also a cause for particular concern. Some species can in fact produce toxins that are dangerous, particularly for dogs. Since June, warnings have been issued along various shores of Lake Geneva and Lake Neuchâtel, whilst researchers have noted the unusual presence of new cyanobacteria at various depths in the lake.
Unexpected species are appearing
Warming also encourages the appearance of unexpected species. In eastern France, the presence of freshwater jellyfish in the Lac d’Orient, in the Aube department, has attracted a great deal of attention. Also found in several Swiss lakes in the form of polyps, they are harmless and appear when the water temperature exceeds approximately 25°C.
This species is native to China and was introduced to Europe over a century ago alongside aquatic plants intended for botanical gardens. Lake Geneva, as well as Lakes Constance, Zurich and Lugano, have already been colonised by these jellyfish for short periods.
Finally, rising temperatures are altering the balance of fish populations. Species that prefer cold waters, such as whitefish and Alpine char, are in decline, whilst catfish and perch are thriving.
For some professional fishermen on Lake Neuchâtel, catfish now account for a significant proportion of their income. Behind this economic adaptation, however, lies a growing concern for the future of lake ecosystems.
Translated from French, sub-edited by Luigi Jorio/ac
