====================================================================================================

Switzerland’s record-breaking summer by numbers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Switzerland’s record-breaking summer by numbers
This year’s summer was the hottest and driest in Switzerland since measurements began in 1864. Only one national record remained unbroken.
The summer of 2026 rewrote Switzerland’s climate history. Average temperature, heat-alert days and tropical nights all exceeded those recorded during the famous “summer of the century” in 2003 – a season that had previously been an outlier. In addition, water levels in several of the country’s major lakes fell to unprecedented seasonal lows.
Data from the final summer report of the Swiss Federal Office of Meteorology and Climatology (MeteoSwiss), published on September 10, illustrate just how exceptional the past few months have been. And climate scientists say this anomaly could become the norm if greenhouse gas emissions are not reduced rapidly.
“With current global climate policies, we’re heading towards a world in which every second summer will be hotter than this one,” Reto Knutti, Professor of Climate Physics at federal technology institute ETH Zurich, told the news portal WatsonExternal link.
>> What Switzerland needs to learn from earlier and longer heatwaves:
More
What Switzerland needs to learn from earlier and longer heatwaves
3.4°C above the long-term average
The national average temperature for June, July and August was 17.2°C, which is 3.4°C above the 1991-2020 reference-period average. By comparison, the average temperature during the summer of 2003 was 16.9°C.
According to Knutti, five factors explain the exceptional heat: global warming, which accounts for 60-70% of the increase; dry soils, which eliminate the cooling effect of evaporation; cleaner air, which allows more solar radiation reaching the ground; persistent high-pressure systems over Europe; and a lack of thunderstorms.
Switzerland is among the countries warming most rapidly worldwide. In the Alpine nation at the heart of Europe, climate warming is occurring at roughly twice the global average rate. We explain why in this article.
39.7°C in Basel
Thirty of MeteoSwiss’s 160 or so automatic weather stations recorded new daily maximum temperature records this summer. Nearly half of these records were broken as early as June.
The national peak was registered at the Basel/Binningen station, where temperatures reached 39.7°C on July 30, equalling the all-time record for Switzerland north of the Alps, set in Geneva/Cointrin in 2015. New daily maximum temperature records were also registered in Buchs/Aarau (38.6°C), Zurich/Kloten (38.3°C) and Lucerne (36.8°C). South of the Alps, the highest temperature was measured in Biasca, Ticino, with 39.2°C on July 30.
Temperatures reached extraordinary levels in the mountains as well. In Scuol, in canton Graubünden, the mercury climbed to 35.1°C at an altitude of 1,304 metres.
One record did remain intact, however: Switzerland’s absolute temperature record. It still belongs to Grono, in southern Graubünden, where 41.5°C was recorded during the summer of 2003. However, this measurement is subject to some uncertainty, as it was not taken using an electronic sensor. Under current meteorological measurement and data assessment standards, that figure would be equivalent to 40.5°C.
>>This summer must serve as a wake-up call, according to a Swiss expert on the health impacts of heat:
More
Why Switzerland is still not ready to face heatwaves
Fifty-seven heat-alert days
The summer of 2026 will also be remembered for the number of heatwaves, defined as periods of at least three consecutive days with an average daily temperature of 25°C or higher. Between late May and late August, there were four such events.
Locarno-Monti in Ticino recorded the highest number of heat-alert days: 57 level three alerts (moderate danger), including 33 level four alerts (high danger), characterised by a daily average temperature of at least 27°C. The station also set a record for consecutive days above 30°C, reaching 24 days.
Several stations recorded at least two additional weeks of tropical days compared with previous records. Basel, Geneva and Sion exceeded 50 days with temperatures of at least 30°C, while Zurich and Bern recorded more than 40. The number of very hot days, with temperatures exceeding 35°C, also increased.
The summer that has just ended offers a glimpse of what future summers may look like. Climate models indicate that, under a scenario of 3°C global warming, Switzerland could experience an average of 45 level three heat-alert days per year, including 15 level four days.
Fifty tropical nights in Locarno-Monti
Temperatures remained high at night as well. A tropical night is defined as one in which temperatures do not fall below 20°C. Locarno-Monti recorded 50 such nights, eight more than during the summer of 2003. Residents experienced an uninterrupted sequence of 19 tropical nights between late July and mid-August.
Heat stress was also severe in Neuchâtel, where the number of tropical nights doubled from 15 in 2003 to 30 this year. When temperatures remain above 20°C throughout the night, the human body cannot cool down and recover from the heat accumulated during the day. This increases health risks for vulnerable groups such as the elderly and people with chronic illnesses.
Since the 1980s, the average increase has been around five to eight additional tropical nights per decade, meteorologist Luca Nisi explainedExternal link to Swiss public broadcaster RSI. Over the past two decades, however, the rate of increase has accelerated to roughly ten additional tropical nights per decade.
According to climate scenarios for SwitzerlandExternal link, Locarno-Monti could experience more than 70 tropical nights per year under a 3°C global warming scenario. In Neuchâtel and other Swiss cities, the number could approach 50.
Lake Constance 50cm lower
The year 2026 was also exceptional in terms of drought. The period from April to August was the driest since 1901. Rainfall across Switzerland amounted to only about half (61%) of the usual total.
Some locations recorded particularly low precipitation: 318mm in Herisau (compared with 736mm during the 1991-2020 reference period), 282mm in Fribourg (490mm), and 371mm in Faido (692mm).
Many rivers across Switzerland recorded flow rates well below the long-term average. Some, such as the River Emme in central Switzerland, dried up along certain stretches. When water levels are low, streams and rivers warm up more quickly under intense solar radiation, increasing stress on many aquatic species.
The situation was particularly critical on the River Rhine, where exceptionally low water levels hampered navigation. Many vessels were forced to reduce their cargo loads, driving up transport costs and complicating the supply of goods and raw materials.
More
Drought in Switzerland: Europe’s ‘water tower’ runs dry
Switzerland’s major lakes were also hit hard by the drought. During the summer, water levels in Lake Constance and Lake Zurich fell below the historical minimums normally observed at that time of year, with deficits approaching 50cm.
The summer of 2026 entered the history books as the hottest in the past 162 years. Yet according to MeteoSwiss, if the planet continues to warm, it may eventually be remembered as one of the coolest summers of the century.
Edited by Gabe Bullard/Vdv/ts

Switzerland feels cost of record summer heat
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Switzerland feels cost of record summer heat
Record temperatures and historic drought made April to July the hottest and driest period ever recorded in Switzerland. Here’s how the unprecedented summer heatwave has affected elderly residents, farmers, businesses and nature.
1. Agricultural sector faces heavy losses
Swiss agriculture is facing climate-related losses estimated by the Swiss Farmers’ Association at CHF522 million ($645 million), exceeding the damage caused by the 2003 heatwave. The government has responded with a CHF70 million support package for farmers and forests.
A severe fodder shortage has emerged after Alpine pastures dried out, forcing farmers to bring livestock down from mountain grazing areas early and use winter hay reserves. To ease the crisis, authorities have released up to 16,000 tonnes of compulsory feed stocks and suspended customs duties on imported hay and silage. Some farmers have even importedExternal link hay from Canada.
The fodder shortage has also hit livestock production. According to Proviande data cited by the Federal Office for Agriculture, the number of cows slaughtered between June and August rose 45% year-on-year, an increase of 10,275 animals, likely linked to feed shortages.
The drought and successive heatwaves have led to major crop losses and higher production costs for farmers. Many crop yields are expected to be down.
Alongside lower productivity, farmers have had to make extra efforts to irrigate their crops, which has also led to higher production costs. Market prices are expected to rise, in particular milk and potatoes.
2. Fish suffer unprecedented heat stress
Several rivers and streams broke temperature records set during the hot summers of 2003 and 2018. On July 14, the Aare in Bern reached 24.82°C, while low water levels and reduced flows accelerated warming and depleted oxygen levels.
The drought caused dozens, possibly hundreds, of streams to dry up, particularly on the Swiss Plateau and in the Pre-Alps.
Major Swiss lakes also recordedExternal link their warmest average summer surface temperatures since records began in 1981.
The consequences for aquatic life have been severe. David Bittner, head of the Swiss Fisheries Association, saysExternal link the final toll could reach “millions of dead fish”, with recovery taking years. Cold-water species such as trout, grayling and burbot were especially vulnerable.
Authorities intervened in some regions to prevent mass losses. In canton Bern, over 100,000 fish were manually relocatedExternal link from threatened waterways. Temporary fishing bans were introduced in several areas.
3. Human health: pressure on the health system
Between mid-July and mid-August, the EuroMomo monitoring platformExternal link estimates Europe recorded more than 32,000 excess deaths.
Switzerland has yet to produce consolidated figures for this summer, but the blistering back-to-back heatwaves are thought to have claimed the lives of numerous elderly people.
According to weekly figures published by the Federal Statistical Office, the mortality rate for over-65-year-olds was higher than projected for several weeks in summer. A total of 1,281 people aged over 65 died during the last week of June, for example. The statistics had forecast 1,104 deaths, 177 fewer.
The Swiss Tropical and Public Health Institute (Swiss TPH) estimates around 500 heat-related deaths occur each summer in Switzerland in recent years. During the summer of 2003, Swiss TPH recorded around 1,400 additional deaths. In 2015, it was 747.
A 20 Minutes/Tamedia surveyExternal link found that the extreme summer heat took a heavy toll on Swiss residents, with 74% describing it as “rather” or “very” difficult. Women were disproportionately affected, with 46% reporting the heat as very difficult to cope with, compared with 31% of men. Over a third of respondents reported major physical effects, including sleep disturbances and circulatory problems, with women again reporting higher rates than men (46% versus 29%).
Lausanne University Hospital (CHUV) reported a 10-15% increase in emergency activity this summer, a spokesperson told Swissinfo.
4. As the Rhine drops, fuel costs soar
Low water levels on the River Rhine this summer disrupted a key transport route for Swiss imports, driving up logistics costs and fuel prices.
Switzerland imports fuel, chemicals, consumer and industrial goods, grain/feed products, metals, and construction materials via the Rhine. In 2024, Swiss Rhine Ports handledExternal link 5.4 million tonnes of goods, or 8% of Swiss imports and exports.
This summer, freight tariffs for Rhine barges jumped almost tenfold from around CHF25 to CHF240 per tonne, as vessels were forced to reduce loads in shallow waters.
According to the non-profit Touring Club Switzerland (TCS), transport costs alone added roughly CHF0.16 per litre to fuel prices. Unleaded 98 roseExternal link to CHF2.13 per litre while diesel reached CHF2.27 in late August.
5. Energy: more cooling, less water… but more solar
Record temperatures created headaches for energy officials: increased electricity demand for cooling, while reducing nuclear and hydro production capacity.
Air conditioning and fan sales surgedExternal link. Meanwhile, the drought has had a major impact on electricity production. Water levels in Swiss hydropower reservoirs dropped to their lowest in 20 years, according to the Swiss Federal Office of Energy. Small rivers were reduced to mere trickles. River flows on major waterways, including the Aare, Rhine and Rhône, fell to record lows, reducing electricity output. Hydropower supplies just over half of Switzerland’s electricity.
More
Drought in Switzerland: Europe’s ‘water tower’ runs dry
Warm rivers and low water levels forced nuclear power plants to reduce output across Europe. Switzerland has three active nuclear power plants with four operating reactors, which generate about a quarter to a third of Switzerland’s electricity. Warm river water also forced temporary output reductions at the Beznau nuclear power plant. Both reactors returnedExternal link to full operation only in late August after months of restrictions and shutdowns.
Despite these pressures, Switzerland avoided energy supply shortages partly thanks to the abundant sunshine and strong growth in solar energy.
“Solar energy currently acts as a significant buffer against the unfavourable hydrological conditions in Switzerland,” the Federal Electricity Commission Elcom told Swiss public broadcaster SRF.
Edited by Reto Gysi von Wartburg/ts

NEW DELHI: Former Tehelka editor-in-chief Tarun Tejpal surrendered before the Mapusa court on Monday after the Bombay high court sentenced him to 10 years in prison for raping a junior colleague in a hotel lift in Goa in 2013.Tejpal had challenged the high court’s verdict in the Supreme Court, which directed him to surrender within two weeks. The high court’s ruling on August 6 overturned his acquittal by a Goa sessions court in 2021 and convicted him of the offence.The Supreme Court’s direction means Tejpal must begin serving his sentence, subject to any further orders in his appeal.

The Goa government challenged that decision before the Bombay High Court, which later overturned the acquittal and imposed a 10-year rigorous imprisonment sentence.Tejpal approached the Supreme Court through advocate Aditya Samaddar. The Goa government has also challenged the high court’s sentence, seeking life imprisonment on the grounds that the punishment was inadequate considering the seriousness of the offences.

Ukraine’s Prosecutor General Leaves Country Amid Anti-Corruption Showdown
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:51:30 UTC
15:51:30 KST

Ukraine’s Prosecutor General Ruslan Kravchenko reportedly left the country overnight on Monday, Sept. 14 in an official vehicle, as a confrontation with Ukraine’s anti-corruption agencies escalated following the “Carthage” investigation into an alleged criminal network operating inside the Prosecutor General’s Office.
Ukrainian media reported that Kravchenko left Ukraine overnight, citing sources in law enforcement. The Anti-Corruption Action Center (ANTAC) also reported his departure.
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
The reported exit came hours after Kravchenko announced that he had signed a notice of suspicion against National Anti-Corruption Bureau (NABU) Director Semen Kryvonos, accusing him of forging official documents to obtain an especially large improper benefit and of fictitious adoption allegedly intended to create grounds to avoid legal responsibility.
Kravchenko also announced a separate suspicion against a person close to Specialized Anti-Corruption Prosecutor’s Office (SAPO) chief Oleksandr Klymenko, alleging an attempt to offer an improper benefit to High Anti-Corruption Court judges and facilitate draft evasion.
NABU and SAPO rejected the allegations, saying no suspicion had officially been served on Kryvonos and denying Kravchenko’s claims that NABU had seized criminal cases referenced in his statement.
The developments are the latest escalation in a crisis that began with the “Carthage” operation launched by NABU and SAPO on Sept. 4.
Putin Rejects Zelensky’s Proposal for Miami G20 Talks
How the ‘Carthage’ investigation sparked the crisis
NABU and SAPO said on Sept. 4 that they had exposed a criminal group allegedly operating inside the Prosecutor General’s Office and providing protection to a network of fraudulent call centers.
The investigation led to the detention of a senior Prosecutor General’s Office official and triggered a political crisis around Kravchenko.
Kravchenko denied involvement in the alleged scheme and said on Sept. 6 that the employee implicated in the case had been suspended and that the relevant department would be reviewed.
On Sept. 7, President Volodymyr Zelensky met separately with Kravchenko and the heads of NABU and SAPO. Kravchenko subsequently submitted his resignation.
Three more senior prosecutors were dismissed the following day.
Despite announcing his resignation, Kravchenko remained in office pending parliament’s decision. On Monday, Sept. 14, he escalated the confrontation by announcing the suspicions against Kryvonos and the person close to Klymenko.
Zelensky сalls for Kravchenko’s dismissal
Zelensky on Monday called on parliament to dismiss Kravchenko, saying the prosecutor general had only one option.
“This prosecutor general has only one path: to leave his post. That is exactly what I told him,” Zelensky said.
He added that Ukraine had “all the reasons” for the decision and urged lawmakers to approve Kravchenko’s dismissal without delay.
The speculation: did Kravchenko leave in exchange for targeting NABU?
The ANTAC has suggested a far more controversial explanation for Kravchenko’s departure.
ANTAC said Kravchenko could have left Ukraine only with the consent and permission of Zelensky and alleged that he received authorization to travel abroad in exchange for signing the suspicion against Kryvonos.
The group described the allegations against NABU and SAPO as retaliation and an attempt to divert attention from the “Carthage” investigation.
However, there is currently no publicly established evidence that Zelensky authorized Kravchenko’s departure in exchange for the suspicion against Kryvonos.
Ukrainska Pravda reported that Kravchenko’s trip was a planned business trip to Lithuania.

Estonia-linked 'Woman Unknown' wins Golden Lion at Venice Film Festival
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:51:00 UTC
15:51:00 KST

Estonia-linked 'Woman Unknown' wins Golden Lion at Venice Film Festival
The Estonia co-produced feature "Woman Unknown" has won the Golden Lion, the top prize at the Venice Film Festival, days after receiving a seven-and-a-half-minute standing ovation at its premiere.
The film, directed by Danish filmmaker May el-Toukhy, is a psychological drama filmed primarily in Latvia. Of its 28 shooting days, 25 took place in Riga, with Estonia's Nafta Films among the co-producers. It was also the only film in competition solely directed by a woman, bringing a double meaning to the movie title and themes it explored.
In an emotional acceptance speech, el-Toukhy addressed the shortage of female directors — an issue jury president and Hollywood actor Maggie Gyllenhaal had raised on the festival's opening day, describing it as a systemic issue and noting that women often find it harder to secure financing for their films than do men. Gyllehnaal was also full of praise for the movie itself, calling it a "laser beam," Variety.com reported.
El-Toukhy thanked the jury for the honour, and Gyllenhaal in particular for using her voice to draw attention to the unequal opportunities facing female filmmakers. She said making the film had been extremely difficult financially, logistically and emotionally, but that she had been driven by an urgent need to bring the unknown women of shared history into the light and make them known. She said she felt she had succeeded in doing just that.
Earlier in the evening, the film's star, Danish actor Mathilde Arcel, won the Volpi Cup for Best Actress. She said it was her first leading role in a film, her first time at a film festival and the first time she had won a film award, and that she felt deeply honored and fortunate to experience it in Venice among people she considered some of the greatest filmmakers and actors in the world.
Of other award recipients, John Malkovich won the Volpi Cup for Best Actor for his role as an eccentric CIA agent in "Wild Horse Nine." He said he was deeply humbled to receive an award given to so many great actors over the festival's 83 editions, and thanked writer-director Martin McDonagh and co-star Sam Rockwell.
The Grand Jury Prize went to Lee Chang-dong's "Possible Love," South Korea's Oscar submission. The Silver Lion for Best Director went to Russian dissident Ilya Khrzhanovsky for "DAU," which he dedicated to Ukrainians. The Special Jury Prize went to "Naza," a documentary on the Gaza conflict by Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor.
"Woman Unknown" is set in Denmark at the end of the Second World War. Its story centres on Marie, a young nanny and housekeeper preparing to marry her wealthy employer, an older widower. A secret from her past — an intimate relationship with a German soldier during the Nazi occupation — threatens her new life.
Esko Rips, CEO of Nafta Films and a co-producer, previously said the film's selection was recognition of the Estonian company's three years of work, noting the production was a truly pan-Baltic states affair.
The Danish-Swedish-Latvian-Estonian co-production was locally managed by Nafta Films' Latvian team, with co-producers including Rips and Latvia's Madara Kalniņa.

Cambodia files complaint over alleged Thai chemical weapons use
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:50:58 UTC
15:50:58 KST

Faisal Mahmud
14 September 2026•Update: 14 September 2026
-Thailand has denied using chemical weapons
Cambodia has filed a complaint with the Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons over allegations that the Thai military used chemical weapons and chemical substances during last year's border conflict, Cambodian media reported.
The complaint was filed on Sept. 11 following findings by the Cambodian Mine Action Centre, which said 395 Cambodian civilians and security personnel were affected by the alleged substances, according to the Phnom Penh Post.
Heng Ratana, director-general of the center, said the substances were allegedly delivered by aircraft and drones as well as through 155mm and 120mm artillery shells.
Evidence was found in civilian areas, including a market, villages and farmland, he said.
“Chemical weapons and chemical substances were used. From a technical perspective, these are two different terms,” the Phnom Penh Post quoted Ratana as saying.
The center also cited an analysis of 61 chemical samples from August 2025, classifying one finding as a chemical weapon under the Chemical Weapons Convention and 22 as toxic or hazardous chemicals not prohibited by the treaty.
The complete laboratory report and sampling methodology were not presented during the briefing.
Thailand has previously rejected Cambodian allegations that its forces used chemical weapons.
Thailand's Foreign Ministry in July 2025 called the allegations “groundless” and part of a campaign of disinformation.
The Royal Thai Army later also rejected claims involving white phosphorus munitions, saying they are not classified as chemical weapons under the Chemical Weapons Convention.

US Kennedy Center faces bankruptcy, Washington Post reports
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:50:35 UTC
15:50:35 KST

US Kennedy Center faces bankruptcy, Washington Post reports
Board of trustees recommending that the main building be closed immediately due to costs, US newspaper reports.
The John F Kennedy Center for the Performing Arts in Washington, DC, is on the brink of bankruptcy and could close as soon as Tuesday, The Washington Post has reported.
According to the report in the United States newspaper on Sunday, the institution’s board of trustees, of which President Donald Trump is the chairman, has argued that putting the president’s name on the facade could be the only way to avoid imminent and “certain fiscal collapse”.
Recommended Stories
list of 3 items
- list 1 of 3US will scrap Irish whisky tariff, Trump says
- list 2 of 3US Envoy Jared Kushner says Doha attack left Israel ‘globally isolated’
- list 3 of 3Charlie Kirk statue vandalised outside Turning Point USA headquarters
The trustees also say the institution will not be able to pay employees or meet maintenance costs within a few weeks.
The assessment was set out in a 57-page report before a special meeting on Tuesday, when the Trump-led board is expected to consider the two main issues affecting the performing arts centre: A financial crisis and a physical one, as the building needs renovation.
The Post reported that board members are recommending that the main building be closed immediately due to costs.
The Post also said Trump would help the Kennedy Center if his involvement is acknowledged. The trustees’ report represents “a remarkable new phase in Trump’s takeover of the Kennedy Center, which has been engulfed by financial turmoil, leadership upheaval and litigation since he installed himself as chairman last year”, the paper said.
The Kennedy Center was named in honour of the late US president who championed civil rights before he was assassinated in 1963.
Trump placed himself as chairman of the Kennedy Centre shortly after beginning his second term last year, and in December his hand-picked board voted to rename it the “Trump-Kennedy” Centre, a move later blocked in court.
In response to Trump’s takeover, a host of artists have cancelled concerts, with US media reporting that ticket sales had fallen to their lowest levels since the COVID-19 pandemic.
The Post reported that the centre was expected to collect about $124m of the projected $220m in revenue that it had budgeted, “leaving a roughly $23 million deficit even after substantial spending cuts” this past fiscal year.
It added that a spokesperson for the centre blamed the problems on “financial mismanagement by previous leadership” and said Trump’s name had attracted new donors.

US travel website highlights Plovdiv as an ancient destination
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:46:48 UTC
15:46:48 KST

Monday 14 September 2026 09:01
Monday, 14 September 2026, 09:01
PHOTO traveloffpath.com
Font size
Plovdiv is one of four European cities that captivate the hearts of history lovers, without the tourist crowds of Rome and Athens. The American online travel portal Travel Off Path considers these cities unjustly underrated, despite offering foreign visitors everything they seek in terms of ancient history and landmarks—from historic temples and ancient amphitheaters to the stories of emperors who built entire civilizations.
''Bulgaria’s second-largest city is a sprawling open-air museum of Greco-Roman remains, Byzantine churches, Ottoman mosques and Bulgarian revival mansions that often gets overlooked by travelers'', the portal writes.
‘Built across seven hills, much like Rome, it is best known for its hillside ancient theater, perched, with row upon row of restored marble seating and a reconstructed stage that still provides the backdrop of summer plays and sold-out open air concerts. Buried beneath the main thoroughfare, the Roman Stadium is yet another major highlight: built in the early 2nd century AD, during the reign of Emperor Hadrian, much of it now lies beneath the modern shops and cafés lining Knyaz Alexander I Street. Plovdiv is pure, unadulterated, in-your-face ancient history, from the Roman remains scattered around the Historic Center that crop up when you least expect them, to the climb up Nebet Tepe, a prominent hill home to traces of a Chalcolithic settlement and fortifications built and rebuilt by Thracians, Romans, Byzantines, and medieval rulers''. The other cities featured are Nîmes in France, Évora in Portugal, and Mérida in Spain.

Medvedev assesses risks of AI destroying mankind
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:45:38 UTC
15:45:38 KST

MOSCOW, September 14. /TASS/. Artificial intelligence (AI) is unlikely to destroy mankind but severe damage is possible, Russian Security Council Deputy Chairman Dmitry Medvedev said.
Commenting on speculations by AI creators "bemoaning humanity’s haste" and warning "that AI is dangerous, unpredictable, and developing too fast according to laws we don’t quite fathom," he said: "The creators of various sorts of generative AI are not being sincere. It’s not you and I or Homo sapiens as a whole that they fear for. They’re not afraid that an unhinged AI, drunk on its power, will destroy humankind. This kind of outcome is not all that likely."
"What’s much more realistic is a scenario where software that uses advanced AI for running a major production facility or a transport hub will cause severe damage. Such as allowing planes to collide in midair or destroying a nuclear power plant. And then there will be someone coming for the creators of generative AI - to slap them with multibillion lawsuits or simply to knock their (human) brains out," Medvedev added.
"Are these fears real? Especially given that many smarty-pants like Musk and others have been saying this for a long time. The answer is simple: they’re real. By the way, I myself wrote about it in a recent article for the Expert magazine," he said.
Medvedev believes that "the most obvious solution would be for countries to agree on general principles of AI regulation." "That would give us all at least some chance of keeping AI development under control. But it won’t happen due to corporate greed and insatiable human passion," he specified.
"So our only hope is to throw ourselves at the mercy of the winner and count on the humanism that AI, our beloved man-made prodigy, might show us. It doesn’t have overt emotions yet, but we don’t know what this great superintelligence will be like. Let’s hope it will spare its parents," the Russian Security Council deputy chairman concluded.

a series of joinery elements replaces walls in 1970s athens apartment renovation
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:45:33 UTC
15:45:33 KST

Kifisia Apartment: A reconfiguration of domestic boundaries
Georgios Apostolopoulos Architects’ renovation of this 1970s apartment in Kifisia, Athens, begins with subtraction. All internal partitions are removed to reveal the apartment’s spatial potential and expose the existing structure. The project questions the conventional role of room-making in domestic space. Instead of rebuilding walls, the apartment is conceived as a continuous interior where light and movement flow without interruption. What remains is the apartment’s essential form, organized by a series of joinery elements that define use without enclosing space.
Apartment rooms function as adaptable domestic devices
At the center of the plan, the kitchen becomes the main social device. Treated less as a room and more as an inhabited object, it forms a solid volume that gathers cooking, working, and meeting around it. The island anchors the space, inviting people to move around it and use it from all sides. It supports daily rituals without prescribing them, encouraging a more open and informal way of living. Around it, living and dining areas unfold through gentle transitions instead of separate rooms.
Georgios Apostolopoulos Architects extends this continuity into the main bedroom, which feels like a natural expansion of the living area, not a self-contained room. The vanity extends beyond the bathroom enclosure, wardrobes act as layered filters, and privacy becomes adaptable, negotiated through occupation and use rather than predetermined by walls. At the opposite end of the apartment, the idea of a second bedroom is replaced by a flexible joinery system. A fold-down bed, integrated desk, and sliding panels allow the space to shift between sleeping, working, and storing. It functions less like a room and more like an adaptable domestic device.
Georgios Apostolopoulos Architects renovates a 1970s apartment in Kifisia
The domestic interior as a system of spatial relationships
Materials are kept simple, with a monochromatic palette that keeps the interior quiet and allows the original structure to remain present. Slim-frame windows bring maximum light into the interiors, reinforcing the sense of continuity between spaces. The project rethinks the domestic interior as a system of spatial relationships, not a collection of rooms. Boundaries remain fluid, privacy is adjustable, and the organization of everyday life emerges through occupation.

High pay, high peril: Indian Kashmir still attracts migrant workers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

High pay, high peril: conflict-torn Indian Kashmir still attracts migrant workers
Despite militant killings and security patrols, half a million Indian labourers flock to the region every year for double their home wages
Every spring, mason Mohammad Ghulam Sarwar boards a train to Indian Kashmir, seeking higher wages despite the risk of being targeted by militants in the disputed Himalayan region.
Sarwar, 25, is one of an estimated half a million migrant workers who stream into the Himalayan region each year, forming the backbone of an economy increasingly reliant on labour from some of India’s poorest states.
The dependence has endured despite decades of insurgency and a series of militant attacks that have made migrant workers vulnerable targets.
Since 2019, at least 24 migrant workers have been killed in militant attacks across the territory, according to local media reports. More than 1,500 people were killed in the same period, according to the South Asia Terrorism Portal, 219 of them civilians.
The latest attack last month saw gunmen shoot dead two brick kiln workers from the central state of Chhattisgarh.

Lithuania investigates unidentified object that crossed airspace from Belarus
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:41:00 UTC
15:41:00 KST

Lithuanian authorities are investigating an unidentified object, likely a stray drone, that crossed 60 to 70 kilometres through the country’s airspace early Sunday. Meanwhile, an air alert that was issued Sunday afternoon in eastern areas of the country was probably triggered by birds.
Vilmantas Vitkauskas, head of Lithuania’s National Crisis Management Centre, said several scenarios were being considered and that investigators could have more information as early as Monday.
“A stray drone is probably one of the possible scenarios, as we’ve seen before,” Vitkauskas told reporters Sunday. “Another possibility is that it could be related to smuggling [...] smugglers’ aircraft do occur as well. It could be a hostile reconnaissance flight.”
“There are various versions, certainly a great many,” he added.
Lithuanian military radars detected the object at about 04:05 Sunday morning near Varėna in southern Lithuania. The military said the object travelled across Lithuanian territory before entering the airspace of Russia’s Kaliningrad region and likely came from Belarus.
Vitkauskas told LRT RADIO that morning that the object was most likely a drone.
The Lithuanian Armed Forces said available data was insufficient to establish exactly what the object was. No immediate threat to residents or property was identified.
Authorities were analysing radar data and other parameters to determine how the object operated before, during and after its incursion into Lithuanian airspace, Vitkauskas said.
Lithuania has previously recorded several incursions by Ukrainian drones carrying explosives that authorities said may have been diverted or disoriented by Russian electronic warfare. Seven such drones have entered Lithuanian airspace in recent years.
Afternoon air alert was triggered by birds
A separate air threat alert issued Sunday afternoon in Vilnius and surrounding areas turned out to be a false alarm after the detected objects were identified as a flock of birds.
The yellow-level alert lasted about 38 minutes, from 12:59 to 13:37, in Vilnius and Vilnius district, as well as the districts of Trakai and Elektrėnai. NATO fighter jets were scrambled in response.
Vitkauskas said the rapid response demonstrated that Lithuania’s procedures for dealing with potential air threats were working.
“The defence mission activated from Šiauliai; there was a rapid response, and the objects were identified,” he said.
He expressed regret that authorities were unable to establish sooner that the objects were birds rather than a potential threat.
“To sum up, I think we did a good job of issuing the alerts,” Vitkauskas said. “Of course, it’s a pity that we weren’t able to identify even sooner that it was a bird and not some object posing a threat.”
Emergency notifications about both the alert and its cancellation were sent to residents’ mobile phones within minutes, he said. The incident also provided an opportunity to test emergency information procedures involving mobile alerts, radio and television.
“I think this system worked quite well,” Vitkauskas said, adding that residents responded calmly and authorities saw no crowds or panic.
Vilnius Airport temporarily suspended operations during the alert, affecting three flights.

Carney's EU courtship shifts up a gear ahead of Strasbourg visit
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:40:23 UTC
15:40:23 KST

Good morning and welcome to Monday. I’m Mared Gwyn. Today: Canada’s EU charm offensive steps up, Latvia’s Prime Minister weighs in on the Russian threat, and Trump unexpectedly reignites Irish reunification debate.
Carney’s EU courtship: Expectations are rising ahead of Mark Carney’s appearance at the European Parliament later this week, with the Wall Street Journal (WSJ) reporting on Sunday that he’s seeking “associate” membership of the EU.
‘Unique Alliance’: Speaking overnight, Carney denied that Ottawa was seeking to become a fully-fledged EU member, but said that it was looking to “begin discussions for (a) unique alliance with the European Union”.
‘Associate Membership’: The status doesn’t currently exist but may ring a bell: German Chancellor Friedrich Merz floated the idea for Ukraine in May, pitching a seat at the table in summits and ministerial meetings without voting rights, coverage under the bloc's mutual assistance clause, and eligibility for funding under EU programmes.
The Trump effect?: Carney’s overture is perhaps the clearest sign yet that Ottawa sees Europe as part of the answer to its increasingly fraught relationship with Washington. Trump has made Canada a prime target in his tariff war while repeating threats to make the country the US’s 51st state.
But, while Canada shares the EU’s values, translating that alignment into closer cooperation has proved difficult. A decade after the EU-Canada trade deal, CETA, was signed, it is still not fully ratified by all EU member states, meaning several chapters of the deal are still not in force.
Carney is expected in Strasbourg later this week and will meet President Emmanuel Macron in Saint-Pierre-et-Miquelon next Sunday for talks on deepening France-Canada ties.
Latvian PM wants to prepare for the ‘worst’: Meanwhile, amid mounting signs that the Kremlin is escalating its hybrid offensive against the Baltic states, Latvia’s Prime Minister Andris Kulbergs has told Europe Today that Europe needs to “prepare for the worst” to guarantee peace — even though he does not believe Russia has the capacity to open a “second front”.
“The one who prepares for the worst has peace,” Kulbergs told my colleague Angela Skujins in Riga. “There cannot be any weakest link. We all have to remain strong.”
Asked about Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy’s warning of a €23 billion gap in Ukraine’s defence budget, Kulbergs argued that the West was fuelling a vicious cycle: Western-made components find their way into Russian ballistic missiles, while Western governments then spend billions helping Kyiv defend itself against them.
He argued Russia should be “fully banned” from accessing the technology it needs to produce the missiles in the first place. Watch.
His warning comes as the war creeps uncomfortably close to NATO’s eastern flank, with a Russian drone striking a passenger train just two kilometres from the Polish border on Sunday. More on that in our top stories below.
Trump reignites Irish reunification debate: Meanwhile, US President Donald Trump’s apparent backing of Irish reunification during his visit to Ireland over the weekend is causing ripples on both sides of the Irish Sea.
While it’s unclear whether his remarks reflect his administration’s official position, Trump raised eyebrows when he said he “would love” to see a united Ireland, adding that “it would happen eventually”.
Trump first made the remarks following a bilateral meeting with Taoiseach Micheál Martin in Dublin, before travelling on to the Irish Open at a golf course he co-owns in County Clare. He has since doubled down on the remarks, while also pledging to remove a 15% US tariff on Irish whiskey.
Asked by Euronews for an official response, a UK government spokesperson pointed to a previous statement by Prime Minister Andy Burnham, who said he was unaware of majority public support for a referendum and that, “until that changes, there will not be one.”
The response from the UK opposition has been fierce, with Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch accusing Trump of “shooting from the hip as usual, just like he did with Greenland and Canada becoming the 51st state”, and describing the comments as “deeply unhelpful”.
Sanctions push: Meanwhile, EU ambassadors reconvene in Brussels this morning in an eleventh-hour bid to renew the bloc’s sanctions regime against Russia – with a looming deadline in just 48 hours.
‘Frustration’: As scooped by my colleagues, France has joined Slovakia in seeking the delisting of Russian-Uzbek businessman Alisher Usmanov from the regime, causing “huge frustration” among other EU countries, four EU diplomats told Euronews.
A French diplomatic source told my colleague Peggy Corlin that the issue was related to “national security” and assured that France was committed to upping the pressure on the Russian war machine.
Back without a bang: In other news, my colleague Sándor Zsiros reports that former Hungarian Prime Minister Viktor Orbán made a return to politics with an annual closed-door speech to his supporters in the small village of Kötcse, vowing to step tp the resistance against Péter Magyar’s government.
Orbán reportedly denied allegations of corruption and accused Magyar of helping Brussels to “colonise” the country. Polls suggest Magyar still enjoys broad support despite his approval ratings dipping recently. Orbán is often surveyed as the most unpopular politician in Hungary – but seems to bet that frustration with rising energy prices and the budget deficit will spark public anger and create an opening for him once more, Sándor writes.
Sweden’s centre-left bloc edges ahead by three seats in close preliminary election results
Sweden’s centre-left opposition bloc, which backs Social Democrat leader Magdalena Andersson for prime minister, was leading Sunday’s parliamentary election early on Monday, with the Social Democrats on 28.1% of the vote in preliminary results from the Swedish Election Authority.
Based on early results, the Swedish Election Authority’s preliminary seat distribution gave the centre-left bloc 176 seats, three more than the right-wing bloc’s 173 in the 349-seat parliament.
The narrow margin leaves the race finely balanced and could make negotiations over the next government difficult.
Although the Social Democrats remained Sweden’s largest party and could lead the next government, their roughly 28% share would mark one of their weakest results in more than a century, down from 30.3% in 2022.
Our reporters Roselyne Min, Malek Fouda and Ioannis Karagiorgas have more on this fast-moving story.
Russian drone strikes Kyiv-Warsaw train shortly after European delegation passes
A jet-powered Russian drone struck the locomotive of a Kyiv-Warsaw passenger train about 2km from the Polish border on Sunday, with no passengers injured, my colleague Sasha Vakulina reports.
The strike came during a massive overnight attack in which Russia launched 453 Shahed and Gerbera drones, including some jet-powered Shaheds, according to Ukraine’s Air Force.
“This is (Vladimir) Putin's terror, 'knocking' directly on the doors of the EU and NATO. Russian barbarians are at your gate, dear partners,” Foreign Minister Andrii Sybiha said.
Shortly before the strike, a diplomatic train carrying former British prime minister Boris Johnson, former Swedish prime minister Carl Bildt, Günter Sautter, one of German Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s closest advisers, and other European security officials had passed through on the same route. Ukrainian Railways said it was “entirely plausible” that the diplomatic train could also have been the drone’s target.
Former CIA director David Petraeus was on another train at the targeted station at the time of the attack.

2026-09-14

15:39:57 KST

Accordingly, the chairman of Iran Chamber of Commerce, Industries, Mines and Agriculture (ICCIMA) and Mexican ambassador to Tehran held an expert-level meeting in Tehran in line with expanding and strengthening bilateral trade and economic activities.
To this, the two sides agreed to set up a joint economic and trade committee with the aim of establishing more coordination and interaction in their economic relations.
According to the available statistics, a significant gap exists between the two countries' economic potential and their actual trade volume.
Based on the data released from Mexico's Ministry of Economy, Mexico's exports to Iran dropped to zero in July 2026, while its imports from Iran stood at just $17,500.
Similarly, Mexico's exports to Iran were recorded at approximately $147,000 in May 2026, with imports from Iran totaling $16,600.
According to UN Comtrade data, the value of Mexico's imports from Iran in 2025 was approximately $1.25 million—a figure considered very modest given the size of the two countries' economies and their trade potential.
In this regard, Mexico is one of Latin America's most significant economies and a major hub for industrial manufacturing in North America. Sectors such as automotive manufacturing, auto parts, machinery and equipment, electrical and electronic industries, and general industrial production constitute a vital part of the country's economic structure.
Generally speaking, the two countries enjoy high potentials and these two countries need to use their potentials to expand ties in the fields of trade and economy.

Why ‘Sanctioning Russia Act’ Is Putin’s Biggest Nightmare
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:38:29 UTC
15:38:29 KST

Russia’s biggest nightmare surfaced last week when Kremlin spokesman Dimitry Peskov condemned the Lindsay Graham Sanctioning Russia Act. This sanction bill is an economic wrecking ball aimed at Russia and those buying their energy products.
This extensive sanction bill mandates a 100% maximum tariff on the five largest countries buying Russian oil and gas and punishes countries helping Russia evade sanctions. It hammers Russia’s shadow fleet, targets banks, defense industries, oligarchs and government officials. It sanctions “any foreign person” committing human rights abuses including terror. This includes 20,000 or more kidnapped Ukrainian children and 1.6 million children suffering Russification atrocities in Russian occupied Ukraine.
Depriving Russia of oil and gas revenues which constitute about 40% of Russia’s budget would cripple Russia’s war effort. A plethora of Western sanctions enacted since Russia’s invasion have undeterred Russia’s war in Ukraine. This sanction bill is different because it leverages America’s $30 trillion annual economy to reassert America’s dominant peacemaker role.
A host of previously enacted Western sanction packages were grounded in the fallacy that Russia’s trading partners would play fairly. Instead, they created a booming new underground sanctions-evading industry. Some American companies are providing components for Russian military equipment and drone technology.
9/11: Geopolitical Legacy 25 Years On
Unsurprisingly, the Sanction Russia Act (SRA) overwhelmingly passed Senate muster (86-11). It has been endorsed by practically everyone including European partners and White House.
But there’s a developing problem. Since its Senate passage in early August, the bill has faced apathy and pushback in the US House. Opposition to the bill includes Foreign Affairs Committee ranking member Gregory Meeks (D-NY) and Don Beyer (D-VA) on the Joint Economic Committee. In August they publicly stated concerns that it gifts President Donald Trump “sweeping new tariff authorities that the President could weaponize with abandon.” Previously, Meeks stated: “it would hurt our European partners, and Americans as well.”
However, the Sanction Russia Act (SRA) is narrowly tailored to punish just a few of the largest buyers of Russian energy. It also provides an exemption for our European partners. Many of these sanctions already exist within previous Executive Orders. SRA merely codifies them into law giving Congress more oversight. President Trump could waive these sanctions citing National Security reasons but it would require Congressional notice, input and possible hearings to justify this.
House members obsessed with tariff concerns are missing something. The 100% tariff will force buyers of Russian oil to shop elsewhere. This legislation implies that in exchange for the privilege of Trading with America, one cannot buy Russian energy. This mostly eliminates sanction evasion. When the presidential endorsement ink dries, the Kremlin may realize it could be game over for their war economy.
On Sept. 11, House Speaker Mike Johnson (R-LA) surprised everyone by suddenly scheduling consideration with the House Rules Committee setting up a potential House Floor vote. Advocates for Ukraine currently meeting in Washington want immediate passage. Every day of House inaction is a day of death and terror. The UN reports that “every month this year the number of Ukrainian civilians killed or injured has increased.”
Last century the Kremlin orchestrated the murder millions of Ukrainians in a man-made famine called the Holodomor. Russia’s current war is a genocide that is a continuation of the Holodomor. The Kremlin is trying to erase Ukraine’s culture and its 40 million people, off the planet.
At this point Congress members should be asking whether or not it will tolerate Russia’s continuing genocides, whether or not it will be complicit.
House members irrespective of partisan affiliation who value the sanctity of human life and democracy should stand in solidarity with Ukraine by fast-tracking the SRA for immediate passage. This could mark a historical moment ending centuries of Russian imperialism.
The views expressed in this opinion article are the author’s and not necessarily those of Kyiv Post.

Rural teacher cultivates musical dreams of children in mountainous township
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
14:37:24 CST
15:37:24 KST

Li Shuying guides the choir members' practice at Dulongjiang Nine-Year Comprehensive School in Dulongjiang Township of Gongshan Dulong and Nu Autonomous County, southwest China's Yunnan Province, Sept. 8, 2026.
In a classroom at Dulongjiang Nine-Year Comprehensive School, 27-year-old music teacher Li Shuying guided students in chorus practice.
Li came to Dulongjiang, a township deep in the Hengduan Mountains, from Baoshan City in Yunnan to teach music. She trains and manages the school's Firefly Choir.
The choir once suspended practice because of scheduling conflicts and a shortage of teaching staff. After Li's arrival, the choir practiced two to three times a week, and its singing once again resounded across campus. Today, the choir has 43 members and is jointly guided by Li and a Dulong ethnic music teacher named Di Songmei.
"I hope to cultivate children's ability to discover beauty through music," Li said, "By enjoying music of various styles, children can embrace and learn about different cultures, gradually forming their own understanding of beauty."
In recent years, the choir has performed in cities like Shanghai and Kunming, and won awards in prefectural and provincial competitions.
Li observed that as performance opportunities increased, the originally shy and reserved children gradually became confident and cheerful, and dared to express themselves. The dream of music also gradually took root and grew in the children's hearts.
"As long as the children want to sing, I will continue to teach them," Li said, adding that she wants more people to know them and hear their voices in the future. (Xinhua/Wang Guansen)

BUENOS AIRES, Sept. 13 (Xinhua) -- Angel Di Maria converted a second-half penalty as 10-man Rosario Central secured a 1-0 win at Tigre in Argentina's Clausura tournament on Sunday.
The Argentina World Cup winner broke the deadlock on the hour after Colombian midfielder Jaminton Campaz was brought down by Valentin Moreno.
The visitors were forced to endure a tense final 20 minutes after Emanuel Coronel's ugly challenge on Nahuel Banegas earned him a second yellow card.
Rosario Central is fourth in the 15-team Zone B standings with 15 points from nine outings, three points ahead of 10th-placed Tigre.

South Australia reports nation's 1st bird flu case in pelican
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
14:37:16 CST
15:37:16 KST

CANBERRA, Sept. 14 (Xinhua) -- Australia's first case of the H5N1 strain of avian influenza in a pelican has been detected in South Australia (SA), authorities said on Monday.
The SA Department of Primary Industries and Regions on Monday reported five new confirmed H5N1 bird flu events, including a pelican that was found in the coastal town of Edithburgh about 80 km west of Adelaide.
It marks Australia's first confirmed case of H5N1 bird flu in a pelican and takes the total number of confirmed events in SA to 295.
As of Friday, there had been 521 confirmed events nationally since the deadly strain was first detected in mainland Australia in June.
In addition to the new confirmed events, SA also on Monday reported that samples from a dead sea lion that was found south of Adelaide would be tested for bird flu.
The state on Sunday reported the first confirmed case of the H5N1 strain in an endangered sea lion that was found dead on Kangaroo Island about 100km southwest of Adelaide.
Environment Minister in SA Emily Bourke told reporters on Monday that authorities will not be able to test a dead dolphin that washed ashore at a beach in Adelaide's southern suburbs for bird flu due to decomposition. ■

Netanyahu's Likud moves to disqualify Arab-majority parties from Knesset election
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:37:15 UTC
15:37:15 KST

Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party said on Sunday that it would seek to bar two Arab-majority parties from contesting the October 27 Knesset election.
Likud said it planned to file requests to disqualify the Joint List and the United Arab List, known as Ra’am.
The party accused both of supporting “terrorism”, claiming they disparage Israeli soldiers and refuse to designate Hamas and Hezbollah as “terrorist organisations”. The statement provided no evidence to support the accusations.
The move comes as Likud intensifies its attacks on former army chief of staff Gadi Eisenkot, whose Yashar party has emerged as Netanyahu’s main electoral rival.
Likud portrayed the two Arab parties as Eisenkot’s “natural partners” and claimed he would rely on them, along with opposition leaders Yair Golan and Avigdor Lieberman, to form a government.
The Joint List brings together Arab political parties in Israel, while Ra’am, led by Mansour Abbas, has contested elections separately since breaking away from the alliance.
Ra’am became the first Arab party to join an Israeli governing coalition in 2021.
Recent polls have placed Eisenkot’s Yashar ahead of Likud, while neither Netanyahu’s bloc nor the opposition has secured a clear path to the 61 seats needed to form a government.
The stalemate could give Arab parties a significant role in post-election coalition negotiations.

Trump's Irish unification remarks draw opposition in Britain
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
14:37:09 CST
15:37:09 KST

LONDON, Sept. 14 (Xinhua) -- The remarks by U.S. President Donald Trump on the unification of Ireland and Britain's Northern Ireland have sparked strong opposition from Britain.
During a two-day visit to Ireland, Trump said on Saturday he would "love to see" the unification of Ireland and Northern Ireland.
"I don't want to cause any problems, but I will tell you, I'd love to see it unified," Trump said at a joint press conference with Irish Prime Minister Micheal Martin in Dublin. He said he believed unification would "happen eventually" and "may as well happen now."
Trump later acknowledged that the issue was controversial, saying there was "no good answer" to the question of Irish unification.
Martin urged politicians to "keep a calm perspective" on Trump's comments, saying that there was no need for an overreaction. He added that Trump's view on the issue was consistent with remarks he had made previously.
British Conservative Party leader Kemi Badenoch said on X Saturday that Trump was "shooting from the hip as usual, just like he did with Greenland and Canada becoming the 51st state."
Gavin Robinson, leader of the Democratic Unionist Party, rejected Trump's remarks, saying on X that "Northern Ireland's constitutional future will be decided by the people of Northern Ireland, not by commentary in London, Dublin or Washington."
"Northern Ireland is part of the United Kingdom because that is the settled democratic will of its people," Robinson added.
Reform UK leader Nigel Farage also said on X that he disagreed with Trump, adding that "Northern Ireland is a proud and integral part of the United Kingdom."
The British government has maintained that there is currently insufficient support for a referendum on Northern Ireland's constitutional status. British Prime Minister Andy Burnham said in late August that a referendum on Irish unification was "off the table" for now.
Ireland and Northern Ireland are separate political entities. The 1998 Belfast Agreement established the principle of consent, stipulating that Northern Ireland will remain part of the United Kingdom unless a majority of its people vote for a united Ireland. ■

Gallardo will take charge of Ecuador for the first time in a friendly against South Korea in Suwon on Sept. 24. ■

====================================================================================================

Across China: In Yan'an, visitors see how CPC turns history into progress
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
14:36:49 CST
15:36:49 KST

YAN'AN, Shaanxi, Sept. 14 (Xinhua) -- When Ivan Racan was still a boy, not yet 10 years old, his father returned to Croatia from China in 1981, carrying with him a sanxian -- a traditional three-stringed Chinese instrument.
The instrument hung on the wall of his childhood room, bringing a piece of distant China into his everyday life. More than 40 years later, Racan came across a similar instrument at a museum in Shanghai. He was struck by the realization that while the sanxian had remained much the same, China itself had transformed dramatically.
For Racan, a member of the Croatian Parliament and son of former Croatian Prime Minister Ivica Racan, the instrument connects two periods of China's modern history. His father had visited China as an official of the former Yugoslavia. When he returned, he brought back stories that captured the early days of a transformation, one that would go on to reshape the country over the decades to come, Racan said.
Decades later, Racan visited China himself and made a point to travel to Yan'an, which was once a center of the Chinese revolution. During his journey through the city in northwest China's Shaanxi Province, he encountered a modern country that would have been difficult to imagine in 1981.
On Sept. 10, more than 200 representatives from over 50 countries attended a thematic briefing in Yan'an. Through stories of rural development, ecological conservation, industrial upgrading and cultural heritage protection, the event demonstrated how the Communist Party of China (CPC) has translated long-term goals into changes that reach communities and ordinary families.
"This is something that has to be commended, appreciated, studied," Racan told Xinhua.
For Petre Danci, a parliamentary advisor at the Office of the Secretary of the Romanian Senate and secretary of the Romania-China Friendship Group within the Alliance for the Union of Romanians, China's transformation is not an inherited memory. It is part of his own life.
Danci, who speaks fluent Mandarin, arrived in Beijing in 2011. He studied Chinese at the Capital University of Economics and Business before completing bachelor's and master's degrees. During seven years of study and life in Beijing, he experienced China through lectures, friendships and the everyday norms of student life.
"Every single time I come to China, I see a lot of development," he said. "Even if I go to a very familiar city, like Beijing, or Shanghai, or some other big cities, I still see the improvement."
His latest journey to Yan'an brought him face to face with an earlier chapter of China's history.
Danci learned about the period when Yan'an served as a center of the Chinese revolution and said he could still feel the Yan'an spirit in China today.
That connection became clearer when he heard about the development of Liangjiahe Village, the technological upgrading of traditional industries and the restoration of environmentally fragile land through years of afforestation.
"It takes function, leadership and organization," Danci told Xinhua.
He believed China's experience in agriculture, ecological restoration and industrial modernization could offer useful references for Romania. Such experience should not be copied mechanically, he said, but adapted to local conditions through exchanges among people, businesses and governments.
Having studied the Chinese language and experienced the country as a student, Danci said he hoped to help strengthen communication between Romania and China.
The connection between Yan'an's revolutionary history and China's present-day development was expressed most directly by Leevameng Leebouapao, deputy director general of the Land Department at the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment of Laos.
During a visit to Nangou Village in Yan'an, he saw how poverty alleviation, public infrastructure and improvements to the rural environment had changed people's lives.
"Socialism creates conditions for equality," he said. "People have equal access to education, healthcare, transport and other public services. China has demonstrated this in practice."
What impressed him was that development had not been limited to major cities such as Beijing and Shanghai. Roads, communications networks and public services had also reached rural communities.
In Nangou Village, he said, the practical work of the CPC and the government had made life more convenient for villagers and significantly improved their living environment.
He added that China's experience in poverty alleviation and improving people's livelihoods deserved to be studied by other developing countries, including Laos.
Beneath Pagoda Hill in Yan'an, visitors did not simply listen to stories about the CPC's past, but also to how the spirit formed in Yan'an continues to shape China's development today. ■

====================================================================================================

Pickleball in China: How a tiny ball is driving a big industry
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
14:36:36 CST
15:36:36 KST

ZHENGZHOU, Sept. 14 (Xinhua) -- The 2026 China National Pickleball Championship was held in early September in Hebi, central China's Henan Province, drawing more than 30 teams from across the country.
Preliminary statistics show that pickleball, once a niche sport, has been included as a mass-participation event in the games of 10 provincial-level regions this year, including Henan, Guangxi and Beijing. The China Pickleball Circuit, which had just over 80 stops when it launched in 2024, has expanded to 1,000 stops this year.
Originating in the United States and combining elements of tennis, badminton and table tennis, pickleball is rapidly gaining popularity across China. The sport is bringing new opportunities to cities and creating another platform for sporting exchanges between China and the rest of the world.
A CITY TRANSFORMED BY A BALL
Every morning, the sharp crack of pickleballs echoes along the banks of the Qi River in Hebi.
Zhang Shaohua, a Hebi resident born in the 1970s, has been hooked on pickleball since she first tried it in 2024. To promote the sport more widely, she set up a players' community and built her own professional pickleball venue. She also travels regularly around the country to play and connect with other enthusiasts.
"In Hebi, pickleball started as a trend, but it has become a daily routine," Zhang said.
In just three years, the once-obscure sport has taken off in Hebi.
"Hebi has a solid industrial foundation and rich cultural and tourism resources, but its urban visibility and influence still need to be enhanced. We urgently need to build a highly recognizable city brand that can connect with both domestic and international markets," said Zhao Hongyu, secretary of the CPC Hebi Municipal Committee.
By 2025, the city had built 1,600 pickleball courts, giving it one of the highest densities of pickleball courts in China and forming a network covering urban areas, counties and townships.
Pickleball has not only raised Hebi's profile, but also helped spur the development of related industries.
Leveraging its existing coal chemical, rubber and plastics industries, Hebi has built a pickleball industrial chain that spans from raw materials such as polypropylene to the production of high-end equipment.
At a factory in Xun County, Hebi, machines hum as pickleball paddles, nets and sports flooring roll off the production line. The company, which started as a rubber and plastic products manufacturer, began making pickleball equipment in 2024. Its pickleball equipment sales exceeded 25 million yuan in 2025, up more than 50 percent year on year, and are projected to top 50 million yuan in 2026.
"We export pickleball-related products to Europe, the Americas and Southeast Asia," said Xing Weimin, general manager of Henan Geli Sports Goods Co., Ltd. "Recently, customers have come to the factory almost every day to discuss business. The pickleball market has huge potential."
A SPORT SPREADING WIDELY IN CHINA
In 2026, pickleball is surging across China with unprecedented momentum, sweeping through cities large and small and drawing participants from schoolchildren to retirees alike.
"There are currently eight million people in China who regularly participate in pickleball, and about 20 million who have tried it," said Bai Xilin, director of the Tennis Administration Center of China's General Administration of Sport. "The number of active pickleball players in China may reach 30 million in the future."
Beyond Hebi, cities across China are exploring creative ways to weave pickleball into urban life.
Hangzhou's Gongshu District has turned unused space beneath an elevated highway into a 6,000-square-meter pickleball park with five standard courts. Beijing's Fengtai District has converted a former indoor basketball arena into a pickleball club that now hosts the Beijing Pickleball Super League. Suzhou, Jiangsu Province, has opened a 23-court complex, the largest in the province, which offered four hours of play for just 29.9 yuan during the summer, drawing scores of young players. Nanjing has 11 standard courts on the 51st floor of a shopping mall, offering panoramic city views.
Hosting pickleball tournaments has also emerged as a new way to blend culture, sports, tourism and commerce while stimulating local consumption.
"I believe that pickleball has the potential to become the next marathon-like popular sport," said Song Hongfei, general manager of the sports business division at Viva Goods Co., Ltd.
According to the China Sporting Goods Federation, China's domestic market for pickleball equipment and services exceeded 1.8 billion yuan in 2025 and is projected to grow at a compound annual rate of 47 percent over the next five years.
Enthusiasm for hosting tournaments is also surging. In just over two years, China Pickleball Circuit events alone have been staged in 99 cities across more than 20 provincial-level regions, while grassroots tournaments have proliferated as well.
"Many cities host marathons, which not only promote national fitness but also play an important role in enhancing the city's brand, and pickleball is developing in the same direction," said Qiao Liang of Henan Dongcheng Sports Industry Co., Ltd.
MORE THAN JUST A GAME
Bai Xilin described pickleball's broad appeal as "three lows and three highs."
The "three lows" start with a low barrier to entry: men and women, young and old, and even complete beginners can pick it up quickly. Second is low cost-courts are flexible and equipment is relatively inexpensive. Third is low risk: as a non-contact sport, it suits a wide range of ages.
The "three highs" are its strong social dimension, high overall value and high safety rating. Its overall value encompasses fitness, social, economic and cultural benefits.
Pickleball has also seen rapid international growth in recent years.
According to 2026 statistics from a sports and fitness industry association in the United States, where the sport originated, pickleball has been the fastest-growing sport in the country for five consecutive years. In Southeast Asia, including Vietnam and the Philippines, it has also built a broad participation base.
In 2025, the Hebi International Pickleball Invitational brought together professional players from 14 countries and regions, including Spain, Italy and Sweden, to compete alongside Chinese athletes and exchange skills.
In February and August this year, two groups of American teachers and students visited Hebi. They paired up with local teenagers for friendly matches and shared pickleball skills. They also visited an ancient temple fair, experienced a molten-iron fireworks display and made traditional clay whistles known as "Nigugu," gaining deeper insight into Chinese culture.
Through friendly matches and cultural exchanges, pickleball is creating lasting bonds that transcend borders and bring people closer together.
■

====================================================================================================

Feature: U.S. scholar finds harmony with nature in China through Lao Tzu
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
14:36:35 CST
15:36:35 KST

Misha Tadd, an American scholar of Laozegetics, holds a book launch at the School of Philosophy, Nankai University in north China's Tianjin Municipality, on Nov. 11, 2025. (Xinhua)
by Xinhua writers Song Rui and Li Kun
TIANJIN, Sept. 14 (Xinhua) -- For 46-year-old American associate professor of philosophy Misha Tadd, the mountains and rivers in China unfold like living pages from the classic work Tao Te Ching, waiting to be read by those who know how to understand them, rather than just scenery.
This summer, Tadd became a special tour guide for his sister, who was visiting China for the first time. Their journey took them from the historical landmarks of Tianjin and Beijing to the rural landscapes of Shanxi Province in north China.
Tadd's account of this trip was about much more than sightseeing. It played out like a traveling lecture on Chinese philosopher Lao Tzu's ancient classics, with each landscape revealing a new lesson along the journey.
Tadd, who directs the Global Laozegetics Research Center at Nankai University in north China's Tianjin Municipality, has devoted years to studying the Tao Te Ching. In his view, tracing the classics back to their origins and engaging with the very landscape that inspired those philosophical ideas is itself a way of finding harmony with nature, a living encounter with the world that shaped Lao Tzu's way of thinking.
TAO ON TOUR
As they wandered through the Mazu Temple and the historic Five Great Avenues in Tianjin and stood before the Forbidden City and the Great Wall in Beijing, Tadd showed his sister how China breathes new life into its ancient sites. "Letting old buildings tell their stories on the modern stage, this practice itself embodies the wisdom of 'The Way follows nature' (dao fa zi ran in Chinese)," he said.
At the Foguang Temple and Nanchan Temple near Mount Wutai in Shanxi, the unassuming, centuries-old structures inspire another Taoist insight -- "The Sage wears coarse cloth externally, but embraces jade internally."
"These temples have survived for a millennium precisely because they are hidden in remote mountains and never sought to glorify themselves," Tadd observed. "Many once-splendid, lavishly decorated palaces have long been lost to history." He reflected that qualities like softness and obscurity, often seen as passive, can nurture the greatest longevity.
Traveling to Datong, Shanxi, the landscape opened up to reveal hillsides covered with solar panels, gleaming blue under the sun. Tadd pointed to them as a practical example of "wuwei," often translated as "non-action" or "effortless action."
"Using solar energy aligns well with the principle of 'wuwei,'" he explained to his sister. The panels convert sunlight into electricity with minimal disruption to the environment, he noted, a beneficial result of non-interference, especially when compared to the environmental impact of burning wood or coal.
But for Tadd, the relevance of "wuwei" extends far beyond solar panels. He sees it as a philosophical corrective to the human-centered thinking that has dominated the West since the Renaissance. While that mindset has driven scientific and social progress, it has also diminished humanity's sense of humility before the cosmos, a humility that Tadd believes is urgently needed today.
"As science and technology advance, we seem to be escaping the constraints of nature," he said. "But solving small problems, like protecting ourselves from the cold or heat, has created bigger ones, like climate change." The Tao Te Ching, he argues, offers a timely reminder: respect nature's rhythms, and learn to live with the universe rather than against it.
"On one hand, we need to reduce our energy consumption," he said. "On the other, we need to produce energy in a way that follows nature's course -- in a 'wuwei' way."
ROOTS OF REVELATION
These insights often bring Tadd back to his first encounter with the Tao Te Ching. At 14, growing up in a remote village in western Massachusetts, he found an English translation of the classics in his local library.
"It was a special edition -- each page of English translation was accompanied by a black-and-white photo of China's natural scenery, with the original Chinese text written in brushstroke over the images."
The beauty mesmerized the boy, who had always been enchanted by the forests and mountains. "The book describes the relationship between humans and the universe in a subtle way. It resonated with me deeply," Tadd said.
Tadd began learning Chinese, determined to draw closer to the ancient philosophy. As a junior in college, he chose to study in Xi'an, the ancient capital of northwest China's Shaanxi Province -- a decision that set him on a path to a lifetime of studying Chinese culture.
Tadd finds that many of Lao Tzu's ideas stem from observations of China's unique geography that still resonate today. "Xi'an is in the Yellow River basin. In such an environment, you cannot ignore the power of water, because the river was once so difficult to control," he said.
Lao Tzu's idealization of water's softness, adaptability, and compliance is likely linked to these regional characteristics. By immersing themselves in such landscapes, ancient philosophers could more easily elevate local observations into universal principles about the cycles of yielding and unity in the cosmos, Tadd added.
HARMONY IN ACTION
Though the Tao Te Ching contains only about 5,000 characters, its concise verses carry profound meaning. It has found a global audience and become a source of cross-civilizational philosophy. This very phenomenon inspired Tadd to pioneer the field of "Global Laozegetics."
In 2022, he published "The Complete Bibliography of Laozi Translations: A Global Laozegetics Reference," which cataloged 2,052 translations in 97 languages. Today, the special collection at Nankai University's Global Laozegetics Research Center houses over 340 translations, a testament to the long and colorful history of cultural exchanges between China and the world.
Tadd champions experiential learning over purely academic research, convinced that the Tao Te Ching's legacy has a profound connection with living people and their stories and that within those natural landscapes, one can best appreciate the unfettered spirit of the Tao.
As more foreign students come to study in China, Tadd offers this advice: "Beyond experiencing the rapid technological development in big cities, they should also go to the countryside, to the mountains, even to the desert, to feel China's millennia-old beauty and the friendliness of its ordinary people."
He hopes international visitors will recognize China's environmental strides over the past two decades. "Environmental degradation knows no borders," he said. "Seeing China's progress, I think, can inspire people elsewhere to embrace sustainability."
The wisdom of the Tao Te Ching has become inseparable from Tadd's own character. "In China, I try to be like water, taking the shape of my surroundings, but not as a disguise or a loss of self. It's about adapting while staying true to who I am," he said. ■
Misha Tadd, an American scholar of Laozegetics at Nankai University, attends an event related to Laozegetics in Luyi, central China's Henan Province, on April 3, 2026. (Xinhua)
