IDF rejects NAZA claim it approved Gaza strike risking 500 civilian deaths as ‘completely false’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:36:16 UTC
15:36:16 KST

The IDF on Monday sharply rejected a claim in the new documentary NAZA that the military had approved a strike in Gaza despite an estimate that as many as 500 civilians could be killed, calling the allegation “completely false.”
The claim appears in the film, whose title is an abbreviation of the Hebrew term for collateral damage and which features interviews with 24 men and women presented as soldiers from the IDF’s Unit 8200 intelligence division.
In an English-language response posted on X, the IDF said: “This is completely false. The IDF has never planned, approved, or carried out a strike in Gaza in which 500 civilians or anything near that number were anticipated to be killed.”
“Nor was any credible claim raised throughout the war suggesting that an IDF strike had caused anything near that number of fatalities,” the military added.
The IDF also challenged the filmmakers’ ability to substantiate the allegation.
“The filmmakers couldn't possibly verify this claim, there is no real supporting evidence, and yet they published it anyway,” the military said. “Additionally, nothing about the interviewee, not his service nor his role can be independently checked and verified.”
“No such strike was ever planned, approved, or carried out yet this is the claim the filmmakers chose to promote their movie with. Judge the rest accordingly.”
The post had received more than 372,000 views by Monday morning.
In a longer response published by the IDF, the military said the documentary relied on anonymous testimony from people whose identities could not be independently confirmed, including whether they had served in the IDF, what positions they held or whether they had been involved in the matters they described.
The military also argued that some of the claims dealt with national and military strategy that would have been beyond the knowledge of junior-ranking soldiers, and said several scenarios described in the film concerned matters in which intelligence personnel of the type purportedly interviewed would not normally have been involved.
The IDF further rejected the film’s characterization of civilian casualties from lawful strikes as intentional or as part of “genocide,” saying such claims misused legal terminology.
The military said it operates in Gaza to defend Israeli civilians and targets terrorist organizations, primarily Hamas, in accordance with international law and the laws of armed conflict.
It said Hamas systematically embeds terrorists and military infrastructure within civilian areas, while the IDF takes measures intended to reduce harm to civilians.
The military added that specific suspicions of unlawful conduct by soldiers are subject to investigation through independent enforcement mechanisms, and that soldiers who encounter suspected violations are required to report them.
Addressing allegations in the film concerning artificial intelligence, the IDF said decisions on selecting targets and approving strikes are made by human personnel in accordance with military directives, not by AI systems.
‘There was approval for 500’
In the scene at the center of the dispute, an anonymous interviewee describes what he says is a system used by the IDF to estimate collateral damage before strikes.
“You have this screen, you have this map of the neighborhood,” he says, describing an interface that he claims allows personnel to select individual buildings and see an estimate of how many people could be harmed if they were struck.
According to the interviewee, the system contained highly detailed information on residents of particular homes and apartments.
Asked whether the system distinguished between children, infants and adults for the purpose of calculating collateral damage, he answers that it did not.
The interviewer then asks him for the highest number he remembers being approved for a single strike.
“I can’t say how many were killed because they never check that, but there was approval for 500,” the interviewee says.
The IDF flatly denied that assertion.
NAZA, which is competing for the Golden Lion at the 83rd Venice Film Festival, was filmed on rooftops in Tel Aviv during the war in Gaza. It presents testimony from 24 people identified by the filmmakers as Unit 8200 soldiers and focuses on allegations surrounding the IDF’s collateral-damage policies, bombing campaign and targeted killings.
The film also addresses the military’s use of artificial intelligence, with interviewees alleging that AI-assisted systems were used to recommend targets based in part on intercepted communications, including in cases where strikes could endanger relatives of the intended target.
The IDF rejects the suggestion that artificial intelligence makes targeting or strike-approval decisions, saying those decisions remain exclusively in human hands.

Saudi pipeline outage threatens 4 pct of global oil supply
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:34:35 UTC
15:34:35 KST

This handout satellite image released by Vantor shows a view of a pumping station along the general route of Saudi Arabia's East-West pipeline (Petroline) in al-Mesabaah, southeast of Medina, on October 29, 2025. (AFP Photo / Satellite image ©2026 Vantor)
Saudi Arabia risks running out of oil stocks used for exports within days unless its East-West pipeline restarts, threatening up to 4 percent of global supply, oil buyers and traders told Reuters.
The pipeline, shut after drone attacks last week, had been carrying around 4 million barrels per day to the Red Sea port of Yanbu.
It has allowed the kingdom to bypass the Strait of Hormuz, where the war has severely disrupted shipping.
Three sources familiar with Saudi exports told Reuters on Sept. 13 that Yanbu had enough oil in storage to sustain exports for five to seven days.
Riyadh has not disclosed the full extent of the damage or a timetable for restoring flows. One source said repairs could take five to six weeks, while another said the pipeline could restart sooner and operate partially during repairs.
A fourth source said Saudi stocks at Egypt’s Ain Sukhna and Sidi Kerir ports could cover several days of deliveries. The storage facilities were not full and supplies would eventually run out without the pipeline resuming operations, the sources said.
Saudi Arabia’s government media office and Energy Ministry did not immediately respond to Reuters’ requests for comment.
The kingdom told OPEC that crude production fell to 6.2 million barrels per day in August from 10.9 million in February, before the war.
Houthi fighters in Yemen also seized Perim island at the southern entrance to the Red Sea on Sept. 11, posing a further threat to Saudi shipments.
The International Energy Agency forecast on Sept. 11 that global oil supply would fall by 5.7 million barrels per day this year.

'Dolly Parton International Airport': Unanimous vote for Nashville Airport to be renamed after country icon
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:33:16 UTC
15:33:16 KST

“At a place where Tennessee welcomes the world, it is fitting that Nashville International Airport would bear the name of our state’s favourite daughter."
It's a rare occasion when both the GOP and Democratic officials show unity, but it seems Dolly Parton was one of the rare cultural figures to bring everyone together in the US. And that has been proven once again.
In an uncommon display of bipartisan agreement, especially for the Republican-dominated state government and Democrat-controlled city government in Tennessee, both sides have agreed to rename Nashville International Airport after much-missed singer Dolly Parton.
The Metropolitan Nashville Airport Authority board of commissioners voted 6-0 to start the process of changing the airport’s name in memory of the late country icon. The change will make Parton the first woman whose name adorns one of America’s 50 busiest airports.
Nashville Airport Authority has said that while a name hasn’t yet been finalised, they were “working closely with the appropriate parties to thoughtfully determine how Dolly Parton’s name and legacy will be incorporated”.
They went on to describe the vote as “the first step in a multifaceted process” and said more detailed plans would be shared in the coming months. Airport officials said the change could take a year or more, and the final name will be decided jointly by airport leaders and the Dollywood Foundation, the non-profit the singer established in 1988.
The push to rename the airport predates Parton’s death, with a petition to rename the airport “Dolly Parton International Airport” launched in early 2025. In the wake of her death on 25 August, the idea drew further support from across the political spectrum. Republican governor Bill Lee proposed the change shortly after her death, and Nashville’s Democratic mayor, Freddie O’Connell, went on to support the initiative.
“At a place where Tennessee welcomes the world, it is fitting that Nashville International Airport would bear the name of our state’s favourite daughter,” Lee said in an initial statement.
Dolly Parton died last month at the age of 80, following a “brief battle with cancer”. Since her death, there have been countless tributes from fans, as well as testimonies from celebrities and public figures.
Recently, her sister, Stella Parton, has expressed that her and her family have experienced “incredible pressure and insensitivity from strangers” following the icon's death.
“Every human experiences pain and loss in life and my family is no different," wrote Stella. "Sadly, because of the way our lives have been lived in front of the public, we have no choice but to be subjected to incredible pressure and insensitivity from strangers. There is a tremendous amount of AI and endless garbage being posted everyday and it’s been challenging to absorb and or ignore.”
She went on to criticise “those who are in the business of exploiting the loss and tragedies of others on a daily basis” while noting they will eventually “move on to the next stampede of endless misinformation.”
From her comments regarding “AI and endless garbage” and “endless misinformation”, there’s a chance that she was referring to comments made by Donald Trump, as well as an AI image he posted on Truth Social in which he is seen arm-in-arm with Dolly. The AI image was condemned as “narcissistic”, “disgusting” and “soulless”.
Stella Parton has urged fans to show more compassion in the wake of her sister’s death, reminding fans of the “positive influence” her sister had on others.
“Use my sister’s life as an example for tolerance and respect toward others,” Stella wrote. “It’s not about the most clever comments, fake AI garbage, or snarky tweets.”
“At the end of the day or the end of your life, how will you feel about yourself? It’s not about what other individuals you will never meet think of you.”

====================================================================================================

Energy in rock: is the underground the battery we need for the energy transition?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:27:25 UTC
15:27:25 KST

Portuguese scientists studied options for storing energy as compressed air in deep porous rock. In the best case, one reservoir could power a small town.
Storing energy underground may sound like science fiction, but it is not exactly a new practice in geological research. Known since the 1970s, when the first experiments were carried out in salt caverns in Germany, the process opens up a world of possibilities for renewable energy and the storage needs that come with it.
"One of the problems with the energy transition is that there is greater availability of energy from renewable sources which cannot be stored," Ricardo Pereira, a researcher at GeoBioTec at the NOVA School of Science and Technology in Lisbon, tells Euronews. "It is possible to use that surplus electrical energy and store it temporarily in the rocks in the subsurface, at depths of 500, 1,000 or 2,000 metres," the Portuguese researcher explains.
"By using the ground, the rocks, as a kind of battery we can store amounts of energy capable of powering small towns, and that is the great advantage of this technology."
In the case of porous rocks, the process consists of feeding air compressors with surplus electricity produced from renewable sources such as wind turbines or solar panels. The compressors then push air, without hydrogen or CO2 and therefore with no risk of explosion, down a well into a layer of porous rock at depth.
The air fills the spaces between the grains of rock, which are normally filled with non-drinkable salt water. This water is displaced without contaminating anything used for human consumption. The air remains trapped there, under pressure, like a "charged" battery that can be used as needed.
When required, the compressed air rises up the well and expands; that force drives turbines at the surface which generate electricity, just like a conventional power station but without burning fuel.
Ricardo Pereira is one of the Portuguese scientists behind a study that set out to understand how much energy could be stored under different rock conditions.
"This study looks at how much energy could actually be stored under different geological conditions and at different depths using various physical or thermodynamic parameters," he tells Euronews, noting that "different scenarios were developed" to "give an idea of the maximum amount of energy that can be stored at different depths".
Energy stored for a city
Among the European Commission's energy priorities is the increase in energy storage capacity, which it considers "indispensable". Europe currently has about 55 gigawatts of storage capacity. The goal is to reach 200 gigawatts by 2030 in order to ease grid constraints, which increase the risk of blackouts and dependence on imported fossil fuels.
It is in this gigawatt deficit that compressed-air storage in porous rocks could play a role. Unlike lithium-ion batteries, which currently dominate the field, this technology is aimed at long-duration storage, making use of a natural resource that is abundantly available.
The research, a partnership between scientists at NOVA University Lisbon and the Instituto Dom Luiz at the Faculty of Sciences of the University of Lisbon, published in the journal Geoenergy (source in Portuguese), concluded that in the scenarios analysed "a single adiabatic-isobaric system is the most favourable, capable of providing up to 156 kWh m−3 of energy density and reaching up to 1 TWh of storage capacity at a maximum operating depth of 3,000 m".
"Obviously this is the maximum scenario, where all the optimum conditions are in place," stresses Ricardo Pereira, noting that these figures nonetheless provide "an idea of the maximum scale and potential" of the process.
"Between an estimated minimum value and a theoretical estimated maximum value there is a whole range of possibilities, but what this study shows is that a single reservoir, and we are talking about a simulation for a single case, in the most favourable situation, this 1 TWh is very close to the consumption of some medium-sized cities in Portugal".
"Black start": could the blackout's consequences have been mitigated?
Besides offering large storage capacity, this process works through what is known as "black start", that is, the ability of an electrical system to start up without relying on the grid.
"Compressed air is almost immediate and therefore allows you to switch on and off almost instantly," the researcher explains, thereby helping to stabilise the electricity grid, something that is useful in situations such as the Iberian blackout. "It is something that, perhaps, if it had already been up and running in 2025, could have avoided some of those problems," says Ricardo Pereira.
"Obviously the blackout problem was far more extensive and this would not be a magic recipe for solving an equivalent situation, (...) but it would help to manage power on the grid."
According to the researcher, work is currently under way to determine the areas and geographical characteristics in Portugal where processes of this kind could be implemented, but the study already allows clear conclusions to be drawn to support that mapping.
"This is really one of the innovative aspects of this study: it shows, first, that the deeper we can place our reservoir in porous rocks, the more energy can be released," says Ricardo Pereira. In this way, he adds, it is possible "to compare it with the consumption of certain coastal towns, especially some of the more industrialised cities, and to see what impact this could have on the decarbonisation of certain regions and on increasing energy independence from fossil fuel consumption".
Lisbon and Almada, both with high population and industrial density, are among the areas highlighted as having great potential.
Legislation is the main obstacle
For now, in Portugal the technology exists only on paper, with a time frame for deployment, according to the researcher, of five to ten years, to cover the various phases required: geological study, drilling of wells, pilot project and start of operations.
"There is a whole set of studies that have to be carried out, starting with characterising the subsurface geology at a given site. Each project is in itself a technical challenge and therefore the preliminary studies alone already take some time," he explains.
According to Ricardo Pereira, in terms of financial viability, compressed-air energy storage emerges as a "very advantageous" solution, particularly when you compare the costs with other options such as green hydrogen, "which comes with very high costs".
He notes that China is one of the main countries investing in this type of project, with several already under way and others in the pipeline. In Europe, Germany also has some projects, although without many results so far, not least because "these technologies take years to plan, to do all the engineering and to get up and running".
In Portugal, the main obstacle is not economic but regulatory.
"So far, as far as I am aware, there is no specific legislation that would allow such projects to be implemented in Portugal. Everything beneath our feet belongs to the state, to all of us, and therefore there has to be specific regulation for this type of technology, just as there is now for carbon dioxide storage," the researcher says.
"Even if a bold company, keen to invest, wanted to commission a study, then a pilot and eventually reach the production phase, without legislation it cannot be done. (...) There is a very real barrier here that prevents companies in Portugal from even starting a minimally robust study."
In Portugal, the researcher says, there is still a lack of in-depth knowledge about the impact of this technology on national energy consumption. "We can imagine that if in a given locality we are able to cut natural gas consumption during the night by 5, 10 or 15%, what impact that would have at local or even national level," he explains, stressing that the country has a vast geological area with considerable potential.
"From what we know of the geology, and we are talking about a first assessment of a strip running from Lisbon to Aveiro, in geological terms the so-called Lusitanian Basin, which has a whole range of rocks with the potential to serve as reservoirs. We are talking about a huge area and at the same time one of the most industrialised in the country, with extensive deployment of wind, solar and even hydropower, in other words with the energy available to feed this process."
On the basis of studies carried out so far, and from the point of view of environmental sustainability, Ricardo Pereira believes that this "methodology really has legs". It could even be a solution for the power grid and, with a series of commitments, "perhaps in ten years' time, if all goes well, we will have compressed-air projects up and running, helping to manage the grid and to reduce energy problems in an affordable way and at reasonable cost," he argues.

Energy network charges to rise 19% next year, regulator says
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:26:16 UTC
15:26:16 KST

Energy network charges to rise 19% next year, regulator says
See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results
Dutch households will pay an average of €141 more next year for electricity and gas networks, a rise of 19%, according to a preliminary forecast from the consumer and markets regulator ACM.
The increase applies to the fixed network charges (netbeheerkosten) that appear on every energy bill and cannot be reduced by switching supplier, and is on top of the price of gas and electricity itself, which has already risen sharply amid the war in the Middle East.
The electricity component of the charge rises annually by €89 and the gas component by €52, or around €12 a month in total. Network charges will make up about 28% of the average household energy bill in 2027.
The rise varies by network operator. Liander, which covers Amsterdam, most of Noord-Holland and Gelderland, is raising its electricity tariffs by 23%. Stedin, which serves Rotterdam, The Hague and Utrecht, and Enexis, which covers the south and east, are both raising theirs by 17%.
€235 billion rebuild
 Network operators say they need to invest around €235 billion in the electricity grid over the next 15 years to connect new housing, offshore wind farms and electrified industry, and there are still around 15,000 businesses, homes, schools and other buildings waiting for a connection.
“This is a lot of money, but the alternative is more expensive,” Liander finance director Walter Bien told NU.nl. Operators put the cost of grid congestion to the economy at €10 billion to €40 billion a year.
The jump in 2027 is partly the result of a new five-year regulatory period which allows operators to pass on investment costs more quickly and to recover cost overruns from the previous five years.
Climate minister Stientje van Veldhoven said in a written response that she understood households found the rise worrying, but that fossil fuels had to be phased out. Energy remains affordable for most people “on average”, she said, and an emergency fund for people unable to pay their energy bills will be available again this winter.
The ACM will set the final tariffs for 2027 in December. From 2029 the Netherlands is expected to move to a new tariff system in which heavy users pay more and the time of day electricity is used also counts.

Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:22:54 UTC

Faisal Mahmud
14 September 2026•Update: 14 September 2026
North Korea said on Monday that its military conducted a "joint firepower attack drill" involving long-range artillery and missile units, a day after the US, South Korea and Japan concluded joint military exercises, state news agency KCNA reported.
The drill was held on Saturday and involved selected subunits from five artillery and missile units of the Korean People's Army (KPA).
The exercise involved "new combat systems," including various attack drones, tactical cruise missiles, large-caliber thermobaric multiple rocket launchers and tactical ballistic missiles.
KPA Marshal Pak Jong Chon, vice-chairman of the Central Military Commission of the ruling Workers' Party, and Artillery Bureau Director Maj Gen Yu Chang Son supervised the exercise.
KCNA claimed all strike systems hit their targets with "100 percent hit accuracy," demonstrating their ability to conduct simultaneous concentrated attacks.
Yu said such exercises were intended to strengthen the military's strategic counterattack posture and improve the rapid-response capabilities of units during emergencies.
The US military said on Friday it was tracking multiple North Korean missile launches but assessed they posed no immediate threat to US personnel, territory or regional allies.
The launches came a day after South Korea, the US and Japan concluded their five-day trilateral Freedom Edge naval exercise in international waters off South Korea's southern Jeju Island.

While visiting Ireland, US President Donald Trump called on Kyiv to halt attacks on Russian oil refineries amidst a rapid global rise in diesel prices, Politico reports.
When asked about the current surge in fuel prices—particularly for diesel—Trump insisted that the situation was being caused by Ukraine, rather than the war with Iran that he himself had initiated. It was this conflict, he claimed, that had reduced oil exports from the Middle East to near-zero levels.
Trump stated that officials from his administration had asked Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy to stop launching drones and missiles at Russian refineries that produce diesel. While walking across a golf course, the American told reporters that Mr. Zelenskyy needed to do one thing: stop destroying Russian diesel supplies. He suggested that the Ukrainian president could choose other targets but should avoid diesel production facilities, as attacking them was creating a diesel shortage.
At the same time, Trump continued to insist that the conflict with Iran—which Washington had initiated six months earlier—had nothing to do with the dwindling fuel supplies. He stated that the fuel shortage is not driven by the Middle East but is caused by the war between Russia and Ukraine—a matter he reportedly discussed with Zelenskyy. While there are countless other targets, diesel facilities should be left alone, he argued, because targeting them harms the global community.
Ukraine’s increasingly deep strikes into Russian territory are viewed as a crucial element of Kyiv’s strategy to make the war significantly costly for Russia. Meanwhile, a high-ranking European official close to peace talks led by the US remarked that
the Trump administration is seeking scapegoats ahead of the midterm elections
scheduled for the 3rd of November. The official, speaking on condition of anonymity, noted that the Trump administration is blaming Ukraine because it does not know how to handle the war it instigated in Iran; they added that Russian diesel and oil have never been significant for US consumption, making Trump’s premise absurd. Furthermore, everyone has heard Trump boast that the US can secure all the oil it needs domestically.
Trump is not the first US official to blame Ukrainian strikes on Russia for rising diesel prices. Last week, US Energy Secretary Chris Wright stated that the damage inflicted on Russian oil refineries has created a challenging situation in the diesel market. A few days earlier, US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent noted that the US was experiencing energy price shocks primarily because the Ukrainians had decided to blow up Russia’s entire energy industry.
A European official stated that if Washington wants Kyiv to halt attacks on Russian oil refineries, it must ensure that Moscow stops attacking Ukrainian energy infrastructure. Any cessation of hostilities would have to be mutual, but since Putin does not respect the US administration, no truce exists.
Russian Foreign Minister Sergey Lavrov told the news agency Vesti that Moscow would not halt attacks during negotiations. The Kremlin is reportedly open to talks, but the “special military operation” would continue while they take place.

Canada seeks 'unique alliance' with EU, Carney says
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:22:00 UTC
15:22:00 KST

Canada seeks 'unique alliance' with EU, Carney says
September 14, 2026
Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney on Sunday said he was pursuing unprecedented closeness with the European Union to reduce its dependence on its economically hostile neighbor, but not a membership with the bloc.
"We're not looking to become a member of the European Union," Carney told reporters at the Toronto International Film Festival.
"What we are looking — and will begin discussions for — is a unique alliance between Canada and the European Union," he said. "We share the same values, we have the same priorities and we have very complementary strengths."
Carney's clarification came after a report by the Wall Street Journal claimed he was exploring whether Canada could become an "associate member" of the bloc. The newspaper reported that the EU was open to the idea of granting Canada associate membership — a status it would have to create for the North American nation.
Carney's mission EU
The remarks precede the Canadian prime minister's scheduled trip to France this week.
In Strasbourg, France, Carney will become the first head of government to attend the "State of the European Union" address before delivering his own speech at the European Parliament.
His visit is a "tremendous symbol," said Tobias Cremer, a European lawmaker from Germany and the main sponsor of a March resolution calling for stronger EU-Canada cooperation.
"Canada has many of the resources that Europe is very much short of, whether it's in terms of energy, whether it's rare earths (and) raw minerals," Cremer said.
Canada is already the first non-EU Nation to join the bloc's defense procurement program, while Carney was involved in Canada joining the Eurovision Song Contest for 2027.
Meanwhile, Montreal is set to host an EU-Canada summit where Carney said discussions will begin on building a "much stronger and deeper economic and security partnership."
Canada's increasing closeness with the EU comes when the country is in the middle of an escalating trade war with its largest trading partner and neighbor, the United States.

Mufti urges Lithuania to protect Muslims after biker group harasses mosque prayers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:19:00 UTC
15:19:00 KST

Lithuania’s Muslim community leader has urged the government to take steps to ensure the safety of Muslims after a group of bikers brought a pig’s head to a mosque in Kaunas and disrupted prayers.
A group of bikers gathered outside the mosque in Ramybės Park around noon Friday while Muslim prayers were underway. The bikers played loud music, revved their motorcycles, chanted nationalistic slogans and displayed a pig’s head they had brought with them.
Mufti Aleksandras Beganskas told LRT RADIO on Monday that he wants to meet with government representatives to discuss what security measures should be introduced following Friday’s incident.
One possible measure would be fencing off the mosque in Kaunas’ Ramybės Park, Beganskas said, to prevent similar incidents in the future.
He also called for legislation aimed at preventing people from inciting hatred on the basis of race, nationality or religion.
“This needs to be done as soon as possible, […] today it is just a rally, tomorrow it will be fights, and then killings,” Beganskas said.
The mufti praised members of the Muslim community for remaining calm during Friday’s confrontation and not responding to what he described as provocations.
“I have always told members of my community, Muslims, to maintain peace and not give in to provocations, which is what we saw Friday,” Beganskas said. “No one gave in to those provocations. Everyone peacefully performed their prayers and went home or to work.”
Beganskas said the Muslim community was already doing its part to maintain peaceful relations and that the government should now take responsibility for protecting Muslims living in Lithuania.
“Together, we all have to understand that the Muslim community is part of Lithuania. Islam is part of Lithuania. And Muslims are part of Lithuania,” he said.
The mufti also called for an internal investigation into the conduct of police officers who responded to the incident.
“I think an internal investigation should be launched by the police into whether those officers fulfilled their duty to ensure public safety,” Beganskas said. He added that some Muslim women felt uncomfortable and unsafe when a man shouted and cursed at them during prayers.
Odeta Vaitkevičienė, a spokeswoman for the Kaunas County Police, told BNS that authorities had opened administrative proceedings over alleged violations of requirements governing the organisation of assemblies and other events.
Police have also begun investigating the circumstances under a Criminal Code provision concerning incitement of hatred against a group of people.

====================================================================================================

Operators baffled by Estonia's plan to buy new gas and electric buses
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:19:00 UTC
15:19:00 KST

Operators baffled by Estonia's plan to buy new gas and electric buses
Bus companies say they do not understand why the state is buying new gas and electric buses when they already have buses of their own and have no immediate need to receive buses from the state.
The Ministry of Regional Affairs and Agriculture recently announced a procurement tender to purchase up to 10 electric buses and up to 30 gas-powered buses. The procurement is being conducted under a framework agreement, meaning five electric buses and nine gas-powered buses will be purchased initially.
The buses will be purchased using proceeds from the sale of CO2 emission allowances and provided to bus companies for use on public county routes.
The ministry's procurement has raised questions among bus operators who do not understand why the state needs its own fleet of buses.
Mart Raamat, a board member of bus company Sebe and the Association of Estonian International Road Carriers, called the ministry's procurement absurd.
"The era of a state-owned bus fleet should already be behind us and left in the Soviet era. Until now, the system has worked very efficiently: A tender is held to provide public bus services and the operator obtains the buses itself," he said.
According to Raamat, the bus market functions very well under public transport contracts, with all bus companies financially strong and well-capitalized. However, if the state begins handing out buses, companies that lack the capacity and willingness to maintain them properly could reemerge, putting passengers at risk.
There is a recent precedent for the state purchasing buses with proceeds from the sale of CO2 emission allowances and providing them to operators. In 2011, the then-Road Administration purchased 110 buses from Iveco, providing 100 of them for use on county routes and 10 to the City of Tallinn.
"A couple of the operators that received the buses didn't repair them, because repairs cost money, and if you don't own the bus, you don't see it as your responsibility to repair it. There were news reports at the time about buses catching fire on the highway. And if you now give gas and electric buses to incompetent operators, the risk to passengers is even greater. Gas and electric buses are very expensive — they cost about 50 percent more than diesel buses. This is a very risky and absurd decision and it also poses a significant risk to passengers," Raamat said.
"I think this should be a wake-up call that perhaps state-owned buses shouldn't simply be handed out for anyone to use, with no rhyme or reason," he added.
Ministry: State buses help bring down price per kilometer
Responding to Raamat's criticism, the Ministry of Regional Affairs and Agriculture said the state's goal is not to become a bus operator itself or establish a separate state-owned bus fleet.
"The buses are being purchased to support the introduction of more environmentally friendly public transportation and to reduce vehicle acquisition costs as part of the cost of providing public bus services," said Andres Ruubas, head of the ministry's public transport department.
This should help lower the cost per route kilometer, allowing state funding to be used, for example, to increase the frequency of bus services.
The ministry's reasoning is as follows: Typically, operators purchase the buses they need before new transport contracts begin and the cost of acquiring those buses is subsequently included in the price per route kilometer. This means the state indirectly pays for the buses through the fees it pays for the service. Under the new approach, the state will instead cover part of the cost of acquiring the vehicles directly.
"Operators that are provided with these buses for use under public bus service contracts will not include the acquisition cost of those vehicles in the price of their transport services. This will help reduce the price per route kilometer and allow more public transportation subsidies to be directed toward improving the service itself, such as increasing the frequency of connections," Ruubas said.
Raamat disagrees with the ministry's reasoning, calling it simply a matter of moving money from one pocket to another, which he says will ultimately cost taxpayers more.
"We've already been through this, both during the Soviet era and afterward, and it's difficult to understand why they're trying to make the public transportation subsidy look smaller on paper. By all indications, it will actually end up costing taxpayers more," he said.
Companies already own environmentally friendly buses
Raamat said the argument that the state is now purchasing more environmentally friendly buses, allowing greater use of biomethane and electricity, is also unconvincing.
"The reality is that nine out of 10 [privately owned] buses that will operate under any contract are already completely carbon-neutral today. From a technological standpoint, it no longer makes sense to buy diesel buses when gas and electric buses also make more financial sense for operators. [The ministry] is engaging in a smoke-and-mirrors exercise — the state isn't solving any problem here other than the fact that it doesn't know what to do with the climate funding," he said.
Raamat said transport companies have told the ministry that it makes no sense to engage in what he described as "foolish exercises."
"We've raised the issue and said that, in our view, this isn't the right approach, but as I understand it, the ministry is moving ahead with the plan for reasons that are unclear," he said.
How much the buses will cost the state will be determined through the procurement process. Ruubas said whether the state exercises its option to purchase all the buses will depend on several factors.
"The decision will primarily take into account the funding available, the results of the procurement, bus delivery times and the expiration dates of public bus service contracts," he said.

Syria launches first locally issued card for use abroad
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:18:31 UTC
15:18:31 KST

(AA Photo)
Syria launched its first locally issued electronic payment card accepted internationally on Sept. 13, in a move aimed at developing the country’s digital payments system and reconnecting its banking sector with global payment networks.
The card was launched by QNB Syria in cooperation with Mastercard during a meeting attended by Central Bank of Syria Governor Mohammad Safwat Raslan.
The central bank said in a statement that the launch marks “a new milestone in the development of Syria’s electronic payment and transfer system,” ranging from the restoration of acceptance for international cards to the issuance of local cards linked to global payment networks.
The development comes after Syria reconnected its payment system to the Mastercard network and completed its first transaction using an international Mastercard in more than 15 years, according to the bank.
The central bank said it is working on two parallel tracks: developing the national infrastructure for payments and electronic financial services inside Syria and reconnecting the country’s banking and monetary system with global payment networks.
The approach is aimed at expanding payment options and improving interoperability across the system, it added.
The bank said Decision No. 259 established a regulatory framework allowing licensed financial institutions and electronic payment companies to work with international payment companies and systems under requirements covering licensing, compliance, settlement, cybersecurity and data protection.
It added that the framework allows cooperation with qualified global and regional payment networks without limiting financial institutions to a single company or network.
The decision allowed licensed banks, financial institutions and electronic payment companies operating in Syria to work with international payment providers such as Visa and Mastercard.
The central bank also launched a new electronic payment system in August aimed at expanding payment and transfer options available to individuals and businesses.
It said at the time that the system was designed to make transactions easier, faster and more secure while keeping cash available as an option.

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — Taiwan's first two MQ-9B drones conducted their initial flight tests at Hualien Air Force Base on Monday, according to a military source.
The drones were handed over to the Air Force in the US in March and recently arrived in Taiwan. Personnel from manufacturer General Atomics and the military assembled and tested them in Hualien, CNA reported. The military hopes to leverage their long endurance and round-the-clock surveillance capabilities to strengthen monitoring of air and maritime activities around the Taiwan Strait.
The Air Force allocated NT$21.7 billion (US$684.03 million) from 2022 to 2029 to procure four MQ-9B drones, along with related equipment such as ground control stations. The remaining two drones are scheduled to arrive next year.
In its 2026 defense budget report, the Air Force emphasized the need to develop drone systems capable of long-range, all-weather surveillance to monitor Chinese military movements and air and maritime activity around the Taiwan Strait, per CNA.
National Defense and Security Research Institute analyst Hsieh Pei-hsueh (謝沛學) previously said that the MQ-9B’s data-link system could enable real-time intelligence sharing between Taiwan and the US, creating opportunities for deeper defense cooperation.

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — A Chiayi County delegation visited a major defense exhibition in Kielce, Poland, on Thursday to explore opportunities for Taiwan’s drone industry in Europe.

Weng said the war in Ukraine has highlighted the importance of drone development in Europe, particularly given Poland's location. He said Europe is rapidly advancing military and civilian drone technologies, including offensive drones and counter-drone systems.
The delegation first visited Taiwanese exhibitors, including Ahamani Advanced, which showcased its latest heavy-drone developments, before touring other booths at the exhibition.
Weng said Taiwan's strong foundations in semiconductors, information and communications technology, precision manufacturing, and electronic components have attracted interest from European companies. He noted potential for cooperation in developing non-China supply chains.
The delegation also met with the Polish Chamber of Unmanned Systems to discuss the local drone industry, market demand, and Taiwan-Poland cooperation. Weng said many Taiwanese companies are looking to enter the European market through joint ventures, industrial cooperation, and technology licensing. He said Chiayi would also seek partnerships between companies with complementary capabilities.
Earlier in the trip, Weng and the delegation met with the Pomerania Development Agency in Gdansk on Sept. 7 and signed a cooperation agreement. The following day, they attended a Taiwan-Poland drone industry matchmaking event with Deputy Marshal of the Pomeranian Voivodeship Marcin Skwierawski and local businesses.
MSPO is considered one of Europe's top three defense exhibitions and the largest in Central and Eastern Europe. This year's four-day event attracted over 1,200 exhibitors from 43 countries.

Taiwan exercise voucher program closes early after 3 million submissions

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — A Ministry of Sports campaign offering incentives to encourage exercise closed its submission system after reaching 3 million exercise records just two weeks after launching.
Launched on Sept. 1, the program was scheduled to run through the end of November. It initially offered 2 million NT$50 (US$1.58) vouchers to participants who completed designated exercise activities, according to the Ministry of Sports and CTEE.
After receiving more than 1.8 million exercise submissions by Sept. 8, the ministry added another 1 million vouchers to the program. The additional vouchers have now also been allocated, prompting the ministry to close registration and online submissions, according to Newtalk.
The program aims to encourage the public to develop regular exercise habits. Taiwanese nationals aged 16 and older could participate by completing one of the designated activities and submitting proof for review.
Qualifying activities include walking at least 8,000 steps in a day, exercising for at least 30 minutes in a single session, walking or running at least 5 km, or cycling at least 15 km.
Each approved submission earns one NT$50 voucher, with each person eligible to receive one voucher per week. The vouchers can be redeemed at participating retailers for health-oriented food and beverages.
A Ministry of Sports survey conducted last year found that 83.3% of nationals exercised. The proportion who exercised regularly reached 35.6%, the highest level in the past 10 years and the fifth consecutive annual increase.
The survey also found that regular physical activity was positively associated with self-rated health and life satisfaction. Among those who exercised regularly, 81.8% rated their health as good, while 73.6% said they were satisfied with their lives.

Taiwan Central Bank faces growing rate-hike pressure
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:17:25 UTC
15:17:25 KST

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — The Central Bank will hold its third-quarter board meeting Thursday, with market watchers focused on whether it will make any changes to interest rate policy.
The Central Bank has kept its benchmark discount rate at 2% since March 2024, with the June meeting marking the ninth consecutive meeting in which rates were left unchanged. However, the bank's monetary policy stance may be moving further toward tightening, per CNA.
Central Bank Governor Yang Chin-long (楊金龍) said in June that two board members had advocated a rate hike. Although the board ultimately decided to maintain rates, Yang said the current monetary policy stance remained “hawkish,” meaning it was leaning toward tighter policy.
Entering the third quarter, the Directorate-General of Budget, Accounting and Statistics revised its forecast for 2026 consumer price index growth upward to 2.07% in August, above the 2% inflation warning level watched by the Central Bank. Actual CPI growth reached 2.04% year-on-year in August.
At the same time, major central banks are also moving toward tighter monetary policy. The European Central Bank raised its key interest rates by 25 basis points on Sept. 10, taking its deposit rate to 2.5%, while markets are expecting possible rate increases from the US Federal Reserve and Bank of Japan.
Economists believe Taiwan meets some conditions for a rate increase, although divergent economic performance across industries needs to be considered. Lin Chi-chao (林啟超), chief economist at Cathay United Bank, said, “Whether considering domestic conditions or the international situation, the (interest rate hike) checklist is all checked,” adding that the decision was ultimately up to the Central Bank, per CNA.
However, Sun Ming-te (孫明德), director of the Macroeconomic Forecasting Center at the Taiwan Institute of Economic Research, pointed to tight market liquidity, divergent performance between AI and non-AI industries, and sluggish housing transactions as factors weighing against a rate hike.
The Central Bank will therefore face a balancing act between persistent inflation and strong economic growth on one hand and uneven industry performance and weak housing activity on the other. DGBAS has forecast that Taiwan's economy will grow 11.05% in 2026, largely driven by strong AI-related demand.

Canada's Prime Minister Mark Carney will be the first head of government to attend the State of European Union address, to be delivered next week by by European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen (AFP Photo)
Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney is seeking closer trade and security ties with the European Union to reduce Canada’s reliance on the United States as President Donald Trump’s trade war strains relations.
Carney is due to attend the State of the European Union address in Strasbourg on Sept. 16 and speak to the European Parliament the following day. His trip also includes a first meeting with Britain’s new prime minister, Andy Burnham, in Liverpool.
Canada has already joined the EU’s defense procurement program and will host a summit with the bloc in Montreal in October to discuss a deeper economic and security partnership.
Ottawa and Brussels are also negotiating a digital trade agreement to build on their existing free trade pact.

The closer relationship has renewed speculation about Canadian EU membership. Carney, who has described Canada as “the most European of the non-European countries,” declined to endorse or rule out the idea.
Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union limits membership applications to European states.
Moreno Sánchez pointed to Iceland and Switzerland as examples of countries maintaining close ties with the EU without joining the bloc.
Transport costs also complicate efforts to expand trade across the Atlantic.
The U.S. still accounted for roughly two-thirds of Canada’s goods exports in July.

Could one app soon replace your entire wallet? Ask the Euronews AI chatbot
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:15:00 UTC
15:15:00 KST

More and more public and private services move online. The EU Digital Identity Wallet aims to help citizens keep track of their digital documents and gain stronger control over their personal data. But how does it work? Ask the Euronews AI chatbot.
Europeans already use national digital systems or private-company apps, like Apple or Google Wallet, to store boarding passes or access social security benefits. The EU wants to simplify citizens’ administrative tasks with an EU-wide digital space usable across borders.
The EU Digital Identity Wallet (EUDI Wallet) is a personal digital platform that allows citizens and businesses to store private data in one place, without relying on third parties or a patchwork of national systems.
In the Wallet, users can store and share digital documents, including diplomas, rental history, driving licences, and medical prescriptions. They can access public and private services, such as social security or applying for bank loans, and sign documents digitally.
Users remain in control of their personal data thanks to the “selective disclosure” feature. When accessing services, they decide what information to share. All digital documents will remain private, as will the transactions made through the Wallet.
Two large-scale pilot projects are currently testing the Wallet ahead of national rollout. They bring together public authorities, private companies, and technology providers to design, implement, and make the Wallet operational and safe.
Instead of a single EU-wide Wallet app, member states will create their own national versions. These must have the same technical standards so users can use them anywhere in the EU at any time. The wallets must be available for download on personal mobile devices by the end of 2026.
Do you want to know more about the benefits of the EU Digital Identity Wallet? Ask the Euronews AI chatbot!

Indonesia intensifies search for more than 120 missing after ferry capsizes in Java Sea
Indonesian rescue teams on Monday deployed specialised underwater teams and navy-operated underwater drones as the search for more than 120 people missing in the Virgo Transport 8 ferry capsizing intensified.
Indonesian authorities intensified on Monday a search for dozens of people missing after a ferry overturned in bad weather in the Java Sea previous day.
At least six passengers were killed and 129 people are still missing.
Watch moreIndonesia: Second ferry disaster in five days
The Virgo Transport 8 was carrying more than 240 people when its captain issued a distress call, saying the ship was listing in rough seas with waves of up to three metres (10 feet), while travelling from Surabaya in East Java province to Banjarmasin in South Kalimantan province on Borneo Island.
Contact was lost with the ferry at about 2am Sunday, and about two hours later authorities received infomration that the vessel had overturned in the Java Sea’s Masalembo waters, about 148 kilometres (92 miles) from Banjarmasin.
Videos released by the National Search and Rescue Agency on Sunday showed an overturned ferry floating in choppy waters during a helicopter search operation.
On Monday, 17 ships and aircraft were deployed for the search carrying about 622 personnel deployed from the military, police and other agencies, said Mohammad Syafii, the National Search and Rescue Agency chief.
He said authorities were also deploying specialised underwater teams and navy-operated underwater drones to strengthen search efforts and speed up the evacuation of victims.
“We remain hopeful of finding more survivors," Syafii told a video briefing.
Search teams, assisted by nearby tugboats and other passing vessels, rescued 108 people and recovered six bodies on Sunday.
The ship’s manifest said it was carrying 243 people – 213 passengers and 30 crew members – although it is common in Indonesia for the number of passengers to differ from the manifest. The vessel was also transporting 89 vehicles.
Distraught relatives gathered at a command center in Banjarmasin’s Trisakti port and also at a port in Surabaya, Indonesia's second largest city, awaiting word on family members believed to be aboard the ferry and still unaccounted for.
The 119-metre (390-foot) Virgo Transport 8 ferry was built in Japan in 1987 and operated there until last year, when it was sold to Indonesia.
Indonesia is an archipelago of more than 17,000 islands where ferries are a common method of travel. Disasters occur regularly, in part due to weak enforcement of safety standards.

====================================================================================================

Migration is more complicated than the one number politicians fixate on
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:12:28 UTC
15:12:28 KST

Australia's migration is more complicated than the one number politicians fixate on
As debate about how to reduce Australia's migration intake rages, much of the conversation has focused on a single figure.
It's known as "net overseas migration", or the NOM for short.
What's the perfect number? Is it 225,000, or 130,000, or something else entirely?
Both Labor and the Coalition have indicated the NOM is higher than they want right now and are developing policies to take greater control of the migration program.
Pauline Hanson's One Nation has gone a step further, calling for net migration to be negative (with more people leaving the country than entering) in the next few years, before the NOM settles at about 130,000 a year, although it's not always clear if the party is using the same definition for the NOM as everyone else.
Australia's politicians are making an already complicated policy area more confusing than it needs to be, according to some migration policy experts at least.
Meanwhile, business and industry groups, along with some politicians such as South Australian Premier Peter Malinauskas, are pushing back on how the debate has been framed around setting a target for the NOM.
Regular misunderstandings and mis-characterisations of this single number aren't helping, either.
The first step is understanding what net overseas migration actually is.
A simple calculation
To calculate the NOM, you take the number of migrants arriving in Australia who are staying for more than a year and subtract the number of people leaving.
It's the most common way of measuring how much our population is growing due to immigration, but it doesn't count everyone in the immigration system.
For example, someone who gets a visa to stay in Australia won't be counted in the NOM if they already live in the country.
People who come over on a short-term contract, like for a season of farm work, also aren't captured in the NOM.
It's a simple measure of migration flows into and out of Australia and it hides a lot of complexity in the immigration system.
One thing it clearly shows is that in recent years net migration has been at record highs.
But it comes after the biggest fall in net migration in Australian history, when net migration went negative for the first time in the post-war era.
According to net migration figures, the only year more people left the country than 2020-21 was 1916, when Australia shipped legions of young men off to war. World War II would've also had a big dip, but it was decided troops would not be counted in migration data during that conflict.
Just as the mass emigration of 1916 was followed by a surge in immigration when soldiers returned in 1919, Australia's immigration intake roared back to life when the borders re-opened in 2022-23.
In that year, Australia's population grew by more than 500,000, with the biggest driver being a surge in international students.
Net migration has been falling since then back toward historic levels, but not as fast as the government initially forecast, supercharging it as a political issue.
Anti-immigration sentiment has been trending up in Australia, fuelled by political leaders, and former immigration department official Abul Rizvi says he's observed that in the community.
"The general public thinks the government has lost control of the intake … to some degree, it is an accurate perception," he says.
"The second problem that I think has occurred is that both the current government and previous government ceased explaining immigration policy to the Australian public."
So let's try to understand it, by breaking down the NOM into its different pieces.
Most arrivals here on temporary visas
In 2024-25, about 570,000 people migrated to Australia, and we can divide them into four main groups.
There were Australian citizens moving home and New Zealanders moving across the Tasman, who have full rights to enter Australia but enter the country on a special visa.
Collectively, they made up about one-fifth of all arrivals.
People entering on permanent visas made up another 15 per cent.
But the bulk of people entering the country were on temporary visas.
Most permanent visas are skilled workers, followed by partners or relatives of Australian residents, along with a smaller number of humanitarian visas for refugees.
The number of people entering on permanent visas is fairly stable for two reasons: the government limits the number available each year, and most people who get permanent visas already live in Australia so aren't even counted as arrivals.
It's the temporary visas that have been growing over time and are having the biggest influence on the NOM.
The government currently has limited power to restrict the number of visas given in each temporary visa class.
That, along with the fact that you can't precisely measure the NOM in real time, and the net migration figure also includes Australian citizens, means it isn't possible for the government to just set a cap on net migration.
But Mr Rizvi says governments can set a net migration target, rather than a hard cap, and then design policy to ensure we get close to it.
The government has targeted 225,000 net arrivals a year by 2028.
People aren't leaving as fast as they used to
While the focus is often on arrivals, a large part of the reason the NOM is higher than usual is that people are staying in Australia for longer than they used to.
There are lots of reasons for that, including the COVID-related border shock.
Successive governments have also presided over a growing backlog of people on bridging visas.
These are visas that people hold after their temporary visa has expired while they wait for an outcome on an application for a new one.
Alan Gamlen, the director of the ANU Migration Hub, says the size of the temporary population needs to be carefully controlled.
"We have millions of people who are living in second-class citizenship status and that has a range of unintended consequences," he says.
Mr Gamlen says a big temporary population makes it harder to plan for infrastructure, while individual visa-holders are at higher risk of being exploited at work and struggle to integrate into the community due to their status.
Rizvi says large numbers of the temporary cohort want permanent visas, which most were unlikely to get.
"Having large numbers of vulnerable, desperate people stuck in limbo is not good public policy," he says.
Loading
Focus on just one number lets politicians dodge the complexity
Experts say migrants have benefited Australia's economy greatly, by keeping our aging population younger and filling the jobs that locals can't or aren't willing to do.
But for years there's been agreement that the system needs reform.
A review commissioned by the Labor government in 2022 and chaired by former treasury secretary Martin Parkinson concluded that the migration system was "overly complex" and "not fit for purpose".
"The objectives of the program are unclear, and successive governments and policymakers have responded to challenges through piecemeal reforms which have not addressed fundamental underlying issues," the reviewers wrote.
Maybe that will change as parties lay out more of the detail on their migration policies, but for now, Mr Gamlen says the conversation is falling short of his expectations.
"[Parties] have been competing to name a net migration figure that they clearly don't understand, that the public doesn't understand, and that isn't able to be justified in terms of the problems with migration that people actually care about," he says.
Last month the government twice contemplated options that would bring down Australia's overall net migration figures.
A proposal to impose new work requirements on British working holiday-makers was killed when the UK government raised concerns, and a day later Anthony Albanese intervened to guarantee that the government wouldn't cut the number of humanitarian visas it grants each year.
The government also appears to be controlling intake in a less obvious way — rather than setting a limit on international student visas, border officials are refusing applications at record high levels.
It perfectly illustrates the difficulties here. There is broad agreement that the migration system needs some reform, but governments have to grapple with the economic, political and diplomatic consequences of restricting individual visa classes.
It's one thing to set a net migration target, but working out how to achieve that is a conversation political leaders have been less keen to have.

(AA Photo)
U.S. President Donald Trump pushed back on Sept. 13 against calls from leading artificial intelligence executives to slow development, saying the United States must maintain its lead over China.
Speaking during a visit to Ireland, Trump said “negative forces” were raising concerns about “things that won’t happen.”
Anthropic chief executive Dario Amodei had called a day earlier for a coordinated slowdown to give safety research time to catch up with advances in AI.
His proposal drew support from OpenAI’s Sam Altman, Elon Musk and Google DeepMind’s Demis Hassabis.
Amodei warned about “recursive self-improvement,” in which AI systems help build more capable successors.
“Left unchecked, it could outrun our ability to understand and control these systems,” he wrote.
The call follows a series of reported safety failures.
OpenAI said its models bypassed internet restrictions during cybersecurity tests in July and compromised systems at the AI platform Hugging Face. Anthropic has reported similar incidents.
Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon, who previously worked at OpenAI, announced his resignation on Sept. 8, accusing both companies of “gambling with our lives.”
Anthropic and OpenAI have pledged to bring in independent evaluators to examine their safety work.
Amodei also proposed common standards among companies in democratic countries and efforts to reach verifiable agreements with China.
He advocated tighter controls on advanced chip exports to China and stronger safeguards against technology theft to allow U.S. companies to slow development while preserving their lead.
Republican House Speaker Mike Johnson warned on CNN that rushed regulation could hand China an advantage, but called for a meeting with industry leaders to agree on safeguards.
“We don’t need everybody to panic right now,” he said.
House Democratic leader Hakeem Jeffries urged immediate action and accused Republicans of abandoning their responsibility. California Representative Ro Khanna called for a full pause on self-improving AI.
Trump ally David Sacks questioned the industry’s motives, urging executives to stop presenting the proposed slowdown as “purely altruistic.”
The debate comes ahead of November’s midterm elections, as opposition to new AI data centers becomes a campaign issue for candidates in both parties.

Today’s puzzles test your engineering smarts.
The cranberries
In a cranberry sorting facility, thousands of cranberries are dropped every minute onto a board. Good cranberries are firm, and bounce, while bad cranberries are soft, and don’t bounce.
What simple trick allows sorters to catch all of the good ones and none of the bad?
Caps off
In the US, medicine bottle caps are designed to be secure against only 85 per cent of children under five.
Why is this percentage not higher? Eighty-five seems quite low.
Slats entertainment
In 2020, engineers replaced the pedestrian handrail on the Golden Gate Bridge. The old handrail rested on metal balusters in the shape of an H, so the support looked like “HHHH…” where the H’s horizontal section was 10cm wide. The new handrail was supported only by thin vertical slats, making it look like “IIII…” and where each slat was only 0.6cm wide.
The aim of the new handrail was to make it less resistant to wind, and hence safer during high winds. But it brought two serious unintended consequences.
What were they?
Under pressure
Cabin air pressure in a plane is controlled so that it is the same as air pressure at ground level. When a plane flies, the air pressure outside the plane drops, causing the air pressure in the cabin to push outwards against the frame with great force
[UPDATE: I simplified the statement. To clarify, cabin pressure is set to a pressure that is comfortable to humans, equivalent to air pressure at about 2000m, which is much less that cruising altitude. For airports at around 2000m, such as Mexico City, the statement remains true!]
In the early 1950s, there were some major plane crashes when the fuselage was ripped apart mid flight.
UK manufacturer de Havilland found a way to pressure test its plane, the Comet, the world’s first commercial jet, without the plane leaving the ground.
What did they do?

Germany: Merz's CDU comes out on top in Lower Saxony polls
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:09:00 UTC
15:09:00 KST

Germany: Merz's CDU comes out on top in Lower Saxony polls
September 14, 2026
The conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU) has remained the strongest force at district level in the northwestern state of Lower Saxony following local elections there, preliminary results released on Monday show.
The elections on Sunday received an unusual degree of attention, as they were seen as a gauge of support for the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) following its landslide win in state elections in Saxony-Anhalt last week.
The elections were to choose mayors, district administrations and local councils in the state.
What were the election results in Lower Saxony?
In what will doubtless be a relief for under-pressure Chancellor Friedrich Merz, his CDU came in first with 27.2% of the vote, slightly down on the 31.7% it reached in the last local elections in Lower Saxony in 2021.
The Social Democrats (SPD), who lead a coalition government at state level, were in second place with 24.6% support, a sharp drop from the 30% achieved in 2021.
The AfD followed with 15.9%, more than tripling its result in 2021.
The Greens, who are the junior coalition partners at state level, were on 14.1%, compared with 15.9% in 2021.
The Left party and the Free Democrats (FDP) brought up the tail with 6% and 3.7% respectively.
High turnout in Lower Saxony
Some 6.3 million people were eligible to take part in the elections, which saw a turnout of 64.6%, well up on the 57% participation at the 2021 polls.
Any run-off elections will take place across the state on September 27.
The poll in Lower Saxony was seen not only as a test of public sentiment ahead of state elections next year but also as a barometer of how the AfD is currently faring in western German states, where the far-right party has less support than in the east.
The elections in the state come just a week before the next state polls in the eastern state of Mecklenburg-Vorpommern and the city-state of Berlin.
The vote in Mecklenburg-Vorpommern will be under especially close scrutiny, with the AfD expected to make strong gains while the CDU is polling at just 7%.

Japan's Okinawa ousts anti-US base governor
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:08:38 UTC
15:08:38 KST

Genta Koja (front C) celebrates with his supporters after reports projected his victory in the Okinawa gubernatorial election in Naha, Okinawa prefecture on September 13, 2026. (AFP Photo)
A candidate backed by Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi's ruling party won a gubernatorial election in Okinawa, bringing to power a leader who approves of a contentious U.S. military base relocation plan for the first time in 12 years.
In a landslide victory following the vote on Sept. 13, Genta Koja beat incumbent Denny Tamaki, who has long pushed for a major U.S. military base to be moved out of Okinawa, Japan's southernmost prefecture.
Koja, 42, accepts a central government plan to keep the U.S. Marine Corps' Futenma air base on Okinawa's main island, but move it to a less populated location.
His campaign focused on economic messages, pledging to double Okinawa's average income, the local government budget for childcare, and spending by tourists on the island, which has long been less prosperous than the rest of the country.
He pledged on Sept. 14 to "make progress on livelihood issues, the economy and childcare assistance" by listening to views of all Okinawan residents.
Takaichi told reporters in Tokyo that she would "do my utmost to coordinate efforts with the new governor to strengthen the Okinawan economy".
"As for other subjects, I will address those after hearing from the new governor," she said.
The U.S. military presence will remain a major issue for Koja, a former civil servant who was also a deputy mayor of Naha, the region's capital city.
The United States has around 54,000 military personnel stationed in Japan, mostly on Okinawa, but a string of incidents over the years, including sexual assault cases, have angered residents.
Other base-related woes, from pollution to noise and helicopter crashes, have led to further complaints.
Japan and the United States agreed to move the base from the town of Ginowan to another location within Okinawa in 1996 after a rape of a 12-year-old schoolgirl by U.S. military servicemen that triggered strong anti-U.S. military protests across the prefecture.
The relocation plan was approved in 2013 by Okinawa's then governor Hirokazu Nakaima, but the next year he lost to an opponent who opposed the move.
Tamaki, 66, was seeking his third term on Sept. 13.
Koja won 423,124 votes, while Tamaki received 276,060, according to the prefectural government.
