Iranian nuclear chief blocked from IAEA conference in Vienna following US pressure - report
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:00:35 UTC
15:00:35 KST

Iran's top nuclear official was blocked from attending the International Atomic Energy Agency's General Conference in Vienna on Monday after pressure from the Trump administration, Bloomberg reported on Sunday, citing a US official who spoke on condition of anonymity.
Mohammad Eslami, Iran's vice president for nuclear affairs and head of the Atomic Energy Organization of Iran, had been scheduled to address the UN nuclear watchdog's gathering before Austria denied him entry, according to the official.
Iran's Mehr News Agency reported on Saturday that Eslami had left Tehran to attend the conference.
An Austrian government spokeswoman referred questions on the matter to the Foreign Ministry, which did not respond to Bloomberg's request for comment.
Austria's decision followed US pressure to deny Eslami a sanctions waiver requested by the UN that would have allowed him to enter the country, the US official said.
A lower-level Iranian representative may still address the conference later in the week, but Eslami's exclusion prevents one of Tehran's most senior nuclear officials from speaking at the opening of the annual gathering.
IAEA admits it has been 'blind' to Iranian nuclear program for over a year
The dispute comes just days after IAEA Director-General Rafael Grossi said the agency was "completely blind" regarding Iran’s nuclear program and has been since at least June 2025. He also reported that satellite imagery had picked up construction activity at Pickaxe Mountain
US President Donald Trump also repeatedly warned Iran against rebuilding sensitive nuclear infrastructure. Last week, he pointed to activity at Pickaxe Mountain and warned Tehran against attempts to rebuild there.
US Energy Secretary Chris Wright is expected to use the Vienna conference to deliver a direct warning to Tehran, according to excerpts of his prepared remarks provided to Bloomberg by a Trump administration official.
"Iran must fully cooperate with the IAEA, meet its safeguards obligations, and provide the access necessary for the agency to do its job," Wright is expected to say, per the excerpts.
Washington's pressure follows an IAEA Board of Governors resolution that reported Iran to the UN Security Council for breaches of its non-proliferation obligations.
Wright to emphasize IAEA's core mission of safeguards, peaceful nuclear technology
Wright is also expected to emphasize what he describes as the IAEA's core mission of nuclear safeguards, security, and promoting peaceful nuclear technology.
"We ask the agency to prioritize operational efficiency, eliminate redundant programs, and ensure that all funding contributed by member states is used to uphold the foundational promise that underpins the nuclear nonproliferation regime," according to the statement seen by Bloomberg.
"A disciplined IAEA, remaining focused on its core technical mandate, will prove beneficial to all member states."
Wright is expected to portray Washington as committed to both expanding nuclear power and maintaining strict non-proliferation standards.
"As America leads a new era of nuclear energy at home and around the world, we will prove that rapid growth and uncompromising standards go hand in hand," Wright is expected to say.
"American nuclear leadership means American technology backed by the world's highest standards of safety, security, and nonproliferation, protecting our national interests while strengthening the competitiveness of our companies and earning the trust of our partners."

====================================================================================================

A spoonful of sugar: how South Africa’s weigh-and-pay shops are tackling ‘poverty tax’ head on
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:00:26 UTC
15:00:26 KST

Every morning, on her way to the laundry shop she runs in Johannesburg’s Alexandra township, Tsholofelo Mphela stops at a bright yellow shipping container to buy a few spoonfuls of sugar.
Inside, shelves are lined with large jars of beans, rice and maize meal, and clear boxes filled with breakfast cereal and loose teabags. A tap dispenses cooking oil by the millilitre. The cashier weighs Mphela’s purchase before ringing it up for a few rand.
She is the sole breadwinner for a family of eight, and her monthly bulk grocery shop lasts only about a week. “At times, I don’t have enough money to buy a 2kg bag of sugar,” Mphela says. A 2kg bag costs about 60 rand (£2.80).
At the weigh-and-pay shop Gcwalisa – which means “fill up in isiZulu” – designed to tackle malnutrition in one of South Africa’s poorest communities, she can buy exactly what she can afford.
South Africa has a rich agricultural sector and produces more than enough food to feed its entire population. Yet millions of people still struggle with hunger.
A 2024 national survey found that nearly two-thirds of all households were affected by food insecurity, and nearly 30% of children under five suffered from stunting linked to chronic malnutrition.
Healthy food remains out of reach for many, contributing to issues such as widespread obesity and diabetes – which recently overtook HIV and tuberculosis as the No 1 cause of natural death in the country.
“A lot of townships are food deserts, where nutritious foods are not accessible,” says Josephine Katumba, Gcwalisa’s head of operations. “We are offering people that accessibility – with whatever amount of money you have, it will at least get you through the next few days.”
Launched in 2022, the social enterprise now runs six container-based shops across Alexandra, selling food and basic household products by weight. Customers are encouraged to bring their own refillable receptacles.
Gcwalisa’s weigh-and-pay model is designed to avoid what the founder, Miles Kubheka, calls the “poverty tax”: the fact that poorer families end up paying more for small pre-packaged quantities because they cannot afford to buy in bulk.
Products are sourced directly from local producers, with a focus on nutritious, high-quality foods.
Low-waste shops that sell by weight are not new, Katumba admits, but they are usually set up in more affluent neighbourhoods. “We said: ‘How do we target low-income earners, no-income earners, people who depend on government grants – which are the majority of the population?’”
The shops soon expanded their range to other household essentials, such as detergent, painkillers and period pads. They began stocking nappies after a grandmother asked whether she could buy disposable ones individually. “She said they let them dry in the sun, dust them off and reuse them,” Kubheka recalls.
In 2023, as South Africa endured regular power cuts of up to 12 hours a day, Gcwalisa installed solar panels on some of its containers. They now provide electricity to dozens of surrounding homes and the local school.
“These traffic lights are powered by us,” says Kubheka as he drives through an road junction at the entrance to Alexandra. “We have become a sort of alternative service-delivery platform.”
Outside the yellow container, a group of teenagers in school uniforms do their homework using the free wifi installed by Gcwalisa. One of the shops was recently registered as a medical pickup point, Kubheka adds, allowing patients to collect their HIV or diabetes medication alongside their groceries.
At the till, 65-year-old Sibongile Msomi watches as the cashier weighs out exactly 16 rands (£0.73) worth of Pine Gel cleaner, which she uses to wash her floors. “I just received my pension, but it’s small money,” she says.
South Africa faces a “food system paradox”, says Prof Julian May, who heads the Centre of Excellence in Food Security at Cape Town’s University of the Western Cape. The country is self-sufficient in food and has adopted policies that succeeded in eliminating stunting in children in other countries, such as child support grants and free primary healthcare. But here, he says, they have “failed to budge food insecurity”.
One of the main causes lies in an “astronomically high level of unemployment”, with about 43% of the working-age population without a job, he says. In addition, unlike in many other African countries, where markets and informal traders are important sources of food, supermarkets and formal retailers dominate South Africa’s food system.
“What is affordable in low-income areas is often unhealthy: low-cost, high-calorie, low-nutrient foods,” May says.
Standing behind the till of a Gcwalisa container set up directly opposite a busy supermarket, the shopkeeper, Lerato Khangale, fills a small paper bag with 100g of beans – enough for one person’s dinner.
Many of her customers rely on government grants of 350 rand (£16) a month. “They say we’re helping them, because they cannot afford life,” she says.
Khangale has started shopping at the refill shops herself. “Towards month’s end, when things become so difficult, I just spend my last money here,” she says. “Just those 20 rands, so that I can buy something for my kids.”

====================================================================================================

Burrata and beyond: a foodie tour of northern Puglia, Italy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:00:25 UTC
15:00:25 KST

‘I don’t eat mozzarella outside Puglia,” says our friend Savio. “It’s not the same.” Savio is a very opinionated foodie, so I take his words with a pinch of good Puglian sea salt (see below). He was adamant about the superiority of the stretched-curd cheese from his home turf.
Puglia is also the birthplace of ingredient du jour burrata, which originated in Andria, a few miles from Savio’s home town of Barletta. Andria forms one part of the triangular Barletta-Andria-Trani (BAT) province, often passed over for better-known parts of Puglia, despite the cheese connection and Barletta having miles of palm-backed, sandy beaches.
About a century ago, in the hills outside Andria, farmer Lorenzo Bianchino was snowed in and couldn’t get his milk to market. To save what he could, he revived an old technique for preserving butter (burro, hence burrata), stuffing cream mixed with mozzarella scraps (stracciatella) in a casing of the stretched-curd cheese.
There are now several burrata makers in Andria, the most famous being Caseificio Olanda, which offers demos, tastings and a museum. Late one morning, we watch Riccardo “Dino” Olanda, son of the founders, work his magic. He adds almost-boiling water to the curds, then mixes with hands red from doing this every day. As he stretches and massages the curds, a smooth velvety mass forms. “See how it’s shiny?” he says. “Means it’s ready.” Working quickly, he squeezes out round lumps between thumb and forefinger into the familiar mozzarella balls. This action is mozzare, hence the name.
He also makes little knots and plaits before moving on to burrata. Taking a warm mozzarella ball, he works it into a hollow bag with his fingers then fills it with cream and stracciatella before tying the top with string.
Afterwards, a dairy-heavy meal totally worth the cholesterol and calories: mozzarella and burrata to start, then bread with chunks of caciocavallo (a stretched-curd cheese), and a dessert of heart-shaped fresh ricotta with strawberry jam or cocoa.
And, I have to say, Savio is right. The mozzarella and burrata have a juicy, elastic texture and a gorgeous cheesy, umami flavour; the salt and flavour leached out by the whey the cheeses later sit in. How will I ever settle for Tesco Finest after this?
Over the next few days, eating our way around the province’s restaurants and food producers, I wonder whether it’s because the ingredients are so good that this area has so many food obsessives, or the other way round.
Barletta sees few foreign tourists, but it is full of Italian life. There are lots of independent shops, a Norman castle and cathedral to visit, and every evening people pour into the bars, clubs and gelaterie of the sette strade, seven parallel streets in the old town.
Down the coast, Trani, with its sea-facing romanesque cathedral and pretty harbour, attracts some tourists, but they are not in evidence at 7am when the fishing boats return. The men complain of rough seas and a poor catch, but from the back of the boats they’re soon selling mullet, octopus, skate, fat sea snails, monkfish and palombo (small shark) to restaurateurs and fishmongers. Thirtysomething Nicola says he went out at midday and got back at 3am. When does he sleep? “Never.” A fifth-generation fisher, he began helping on the boats aged six.
We’ll eat fish later, but today is for pizza. Portento in Barletta is run by food nut Luigi Lacerenza. He has us try so many local products as antipasti – caprino goat’s cheese tasting of green mountains, grilled caciocavallo from an old couple who make just six a week and small-batch salami – that we’re almost too full for pizzas. But they are wonderful: one with sausage, olives, oyster mushrooms and yellow tomatoes; another with aubergines and grated cacioricotta.
In Cerignola, west of Barletta, Rosario Didonna used the money he won on a TV standup competition to turn his family’s deli into a cosy, wood- panelled restaurant, U’ Vulèsce (dialect for “peckish”). Rosario knows fishers by name, works with small farmers and wine producers, and invites nonne from all over southern Italy to demonstrate recipes in his kitchens.
We start with tasty goat prosciutto and plum-sized local olives called bella di Cerignola. Then an unforgettable prawn tartare served in sea urchin shells, hairy crab spaghetti (“the juice is better than lobster”), octopus with bean puree, and sea bass with orange mayo. He waves away our thanks: “I’m doing the best job in the world!”
Nicola Barbera at Masseria Barbera, a farm restaurant in the Alta Murgia national park, is equally inspired, but rather by what grows on the estate founded by his great-great-great-great-grandfather Riccardo, who fought with Garibaldi. Exuberantly bouncing us around the farm – it produces 90% of what the 100-cover restaurant needs – he shows us olives and vines, but also the “weeds” growing around them. “They’re eatable,” he says, grabbing handfuls of peppery rocket, and bitter but delicate wild chicory. From the abundant kitchen garden, he makes eight varieties of tomato sauce.
We tuck into a lot of this for lunch: a pie with white onions, anchovy and sultanas; courgettes with mint and garlic; spaghetti with yellow tomatoes. (“I call it bastard sauce,” says Nicola. “Those tomatoes take three times as long to pick.”) Then, something I never thought I’d eat: grilled donkey fillet. I feel bad about the patient, mild-tempered beasts, but this is healthy, low-fat food, never intensively farmed. It’s also the best meat I’ve ever tasted.
Good food is made better by good olive oil, and producers here include Muraglia, known for organic oil in hand-painted, rainbow-stripe ceramic bottles, and Sabino Leone, offering tours of its ancient groves and oil mill for €15, plus tastings.
This area also offers plenty to do between meals. Barletta was the home of Italian painter Giuseppe De Nittis, who hung out with Degas and Manet, and took part in the first impressionist exhibition in Paris, in 1874. Over 200 of his works are now displayed in the 16th-century Palazzo della Marra (€6).
At Europe’s biggest salt pan, Salina Margherita di Savoia, there’s an expanse of peaceful ponds with a snow-white salt mountain to climb. Another day, an exhilarating ebike ride in the Alta Murgia with Filippo Tito of Cycling ’n’ Trek, through dense forest to peer with awe into an old bauxite mine that resembles a mini grand canyon. We get back ravenous, but in this corner of northern Puglia, that’s a blessing.
Where to stay
Until this century, Barletta had little tourist accommodation. Pick of the few B&Bs is Antica Dimora (doubles from €97), a restored 17th-century palazzo run by Lilly and Riccardo Manna. There are three smart bedrooms with lots of plants and breakfast features different local specialities each day.
Lama di Luna (doubles from €230) is an organic farm in deep countryside, with six rooms set around an 18th-century stone courtyard. A well and solar panels make it energy and water self-sufficient; half of its 200 hectares are left wild, the rest are planted with olives, vines, almonds and cherries. There’s a cinema, a library and a large pool shaded by 600-year-old olive trees.
Where (else) to eat
Barletta’s 60-year-old Mosé bakery is revered for its krapfen filled doughnuts and sourdough croissants. There are also chocolates, ice-cream and cakes, all housemade, and great coffee.
Puglia has hundreds of gelaterie but experts rate Scoop in Canosa (which is called Puglia’s “Little Rome” for its catacombs, temple ruins and Roman bridge). Francesco Di Nunno makes ice-cream with local seasonal ingredients: raspberry and strawberry sorbets are available only in early summer; percoca yellow peach until mid-August.
There’s affordable fine dining at chef Luciano Matera’s Il Turracciolo in central Andria. Highlights for me are broad bean and chicory “meatballs”, bright green inside; a “hidden mushroom” under baked eggy topping; and pasta discs with whole and pureed green beans and peas.
At A Sud dell’Anima, chef Nadia Tamburrano uses modern techniques and hyper-local ingredients to make food of astonishing quality for a small hilltop town (Minervino, population under 8,000). Her antipasti explode with flavour thanks to house-cured capers and homemade mustard, syrups and purees. A main of sweetbreads wrapped in intestines feels a bit out-there, but is delicious, the intestines crispy as crackling.
The trip was organised by Unexpected Italy, which offers personalised itineraries for Barletta and the surrounding area

====================================================================================================

UK credit scores: should you worry about a big shake-up?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:00:24 UTC
15:00:24 KST

Your credit score is the magic number that helps lenders decide whether or not to approve a mortgage, loan or credit card application.
Typically, the higher the number, the better your borrowing prospects. But a shake-up of the scoring system at TransUnion, one of the UK’s three main credit reference agencies, could mean a shock next time you log in.
Previously, TransUnion’s top score was 710, but later this month it will start rolling out a wider range, rating you out of 999.
And while the change means tens of thousands of people will be promoted to a higher band (bands are colour-coded like traffic lights, with “excellent” at the top and “very low” at the bottom), others will be demoted and find they have slipped down a rung.
The good news, though, is that even if your “score banding” changes, the underlying information held on your credit file will remain the same, and that is what lenders use to decide the outcome of an application – so credit decisions and approvals will not be affected, says the company.
In the UK there is no “universal credit score” that is used by lenders or credit reference agencies (CRAs). Each CRA has its own system: the other two main ones in the UK are Equifax and Experian. At Equifax your credit score is between 0 and 1,000, and at Experian it’s 0 to 1,250.
What is my score?
It is a number calculated based on your credit history. That is, it’s a measure of how likely you are to repay your debts on time.
Before you can take on any kind of debt – and for other purposes, too – banks, mobile phone companies and other businesses will check your creditworthiness with any of the three CRAs. They all use slightly different models, but rely on similar data, such as your history of paying bills, to calculate the score. Those in the top bands are considered a more reliable prospect for lenders, with your score also affecting how much you can borrow and the rate of interest charged.
What is TransUnion doing?
Previously, its top mark was 710, but this month it will start rolling out the new system that scores you from 0 to 999.
TransUnion says the overhaul gives consumers a “clearer and more detailed view of their credit health”.
It claims it is “more than a new number range” as it combines how consumers use credit over time, with a “moment-in-time view to provide a more considered picture of how consumers manage credit day-to-day”.
The new score draws on more data, including how account balances have changed over time, and how credit cards are used.
The company has also changed some of its terminology. The bottom two bands used to be called “poor” and “very poor” but have been renamed “low” and “very low”.
TransUnion says most people (58%) will remain in the same score band, while 36% will be boosted to a higher bracket, and 6% will move down a bracket.
Madhu Kejriwal at TransUnion says its new score is “clearer, more transparent, informative and better aligned with how lenders view consumers today”.
The new system is being rolled out in stages between late September and June 2027. As a result, some consumers may briefly see two different scores, depending on the app or provider they use, and when it adopts the new model.
Even if your score ends up looking worse, TransUnion says that nothing about your underlying credit report will have changed – only the number that you see.
TransUnion says: “The information shared with organisations about an individual’s credit history will remain unchanged.
“As a result, the presence of different scores during the transition will not impact or influence lenders’ decisions on credit applications.”
Tom Eyre, the chief executive of the credit-building platform Loqbox, says the changes to scoring systems are not something to worry about. “If a new model moves someone into a lower band, it doesn’t mean they have become less creditworthy overnight. Their behaviour and their history are the same. What’s changed is how the credit rating agency calculates or presents their score.”
Eyre adds: “It’s far more important to understand what’s in your report and what shapes it, rather than a specific band or number, as it’s those pieces of information that credit providers actually look at, alongside their own criteria, when making a decision.”
What about Experian and Equifax?
Experian rejigged its scoring system last year, increasing the top of its range from 999 to 1,250, and factoring in items such as rental payments for the first time.
Equifax changed its score range from 0-700 to 0-1,000 in 2021 and is not thought to be planning another change any time soon.
Experian says its new system “better reflects more of the everyday financial behaviours that matter – like paying rent or reducing overdraft use”.
It also gives people a “more personalised view of how they’re doing financially, and more practical ways to improve their score”, the company says.
Experian also got rid of the “poor” and “very poor” labels, as well as the use of the colour red. Its bands are now called “excellent”, “very good”, “good”, fair” and “low”.
After the change, Experian says 42% of people saw their score band improve, and a similar number recorded a drop, while 14% registered no change.
What if my score falls?
Small movements are not unusual – they can be caused by things such as the fact you’ve used a bit more of your credit card credit limit – and a slight dip is unlikely to affect your ability to borrow.
Also, remember that a “low” or “very low” score does not block access to credit. It may make it harder to obtain, though, and the available options may come with a higher interest rate.
John Webb at Experian says that, generally, there is “no need to worry” about small changes.
“Your credit score can change regularly as the information on your credit report is updated,” he adds.
It is worth keeping an eye on the direction of travel, and there are lots of free, and paid-for, ways to check your score – many high street banks include the feature in their apps.
Eyre says each agency works out its own score from the information it holds, but “it’s the credit file that gives you the detail behind it, such as your accounts, payments and searches”.
He explains: “Every credit reference agency is legally required to give you your statutory credit report at no charge, and services such as ClearScore, Credit Karma and the Experian app will show a score, and often basic credit report insights, for free, although you might need to pay to access some additional features.”
A steep drop in your credit score is a red flag and you should investigate. It could be something straightforward such as the closure of an account after paying off a longstanding credit card bill, or something more serious, such as a missed payment or identity fraud.
How can I improve my credit score?
Start by checking that the information in your credit report is accurate.
You need to check all three credit reference agencies as Loqbox chief executive Tom Eyre says no single one gives you the whole picture. “They don’t all hold exactly the same information about a person, because lenders and other providers may report to one, two or all three – and some lenders lean on a particular agency when assessing applications.”
Experian’s guide to improving your credit score advises using 30% of your credit card limit, registering on the electoral roll (this helps lenders verify your identity), avoiding new applications for credit unless needed, and keeping up to date with minimum payments.
Craig Tebbutt at Equifax says it is important to “make full repayments on time. Missed payments go on your credit report. Paying back what you owe in full and on time shows lenders you are able to manage your finances, which supports your overall credit health.”
Tebbutt says that if you already have a lot of credit available – for example, several credit cards or a large overdraft – lenders may take this into account.
Also think about closing accounts you do not use.
You should also “be careful when applying for credit”, says Tebbutt. “Every time you formally apply for credit, it leaves a ‘hard search’ on your credit report. Try to space out your applications, and only apply for credit you need.”
Finally, review your financial associations: if you have a joint bank account or mortgage with someone, you are “financially associated” with them. If their credit history isn’t great, it could affect your own application.

====================================================================================================

We Are Family: photography from Images Vevey 2026 – picture essay
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:00:24 UTC
15:00:24 KST

Every two years, on the shores of Lake Geneva at the foot of the Swiss Alps, the town of Vevey transforms its public spaces and buildings with 50 installations of artistic projects from 21 countries, which are free and open to the public. With such a breathtaking natural setting as its backdrop, the photography displayed at Images Vevey has to strike its own particular note of magnificence to hold the gaze.
Rising six storeys high, Sian Davey’s portrait of the renowned photographer Martin Parr, who died at the end of last year, sets the tone for this year’s festival. Taken in a splendid garden that seems to have flourished straight from Eden, the monumental portrait overlooks the town.
Martin was a significant and much-loved member of the Images Vevey family, having exhibited his own work here on a number of occasions. It is fitting then, that he should embody this year’s theme, “We Are Family”.
When Martin arrived to visit Sian he had recently received a terminal cancer diagnosis but Sian recalls the serenity he experienced in the garden. That sense of peace radiates from the portrait as he watches over the town in a fitting tribute.
The creation of the garden itself was a labour of love for Sian and her son Luke. As you can see in the portrait of Martin and in the other photographs in the display, it’s also the playground of Sian’s daughter, Alice, making this in every sense a family affair.
Many of this year’s family-themed projects are a study of love and loss, connection and separation. In Barbara Davatz’s series As Time Goes By the starting point is the relationship between couples who visited her studio from 1982 onwards. Over the course of three decades, she charts their changing relationships creating a fascinating kind of family tree.
Uncomplicated by props or elaborate staging, her subjects are presented in their purest form, inviting us to contemplate the course of their lives and the shifting nature of family bonds over time.
One of the most joyful series on display is Martina Bacigalupo’s Showing How Big the Sky Is, a project that charts 10 years of personal correspondence and amusing snapshots exchanged via WhatsApp and Skype with her former nanny, Chiou Taur Wu, who she affectionately describes as “auntie”.
“I love being barefoot. Because when your feet are not tied you are free.” Chiou told Martina, offering a glimpse into the context of her life.
Born into poverty in Taiwan in 1948 and deprived of an education, Chiou left the country to marry an Italian sailor and began new life with him in Portfino. But her hopes for a brighter future were dashed when she discovered he had mounting gambling debts which she then spent decades paying off.
One of the jobs she took on was caring for Martina and her brother, Tomaso. In all she spent 32 years with the family until retiring back to Taiwan. There, at the age of 70 she resolved to live on her own terms. She returned to education, took dance classes and began an adventure travelling around Asia.
Now she is playing and sharing her enchantment with the world with Martina. These vibrant and witty photographs tell of the love and the lasting bond between the two.
When Karolina Wojtas was a little girl, she was perfectly content as an only child. The very idea of a sibling filled her with dark thoughts. “I’ll kill them and eat them,” she told her parents when the subject was raised. So when her little brother arrived when she was 13, turbulence was perhaps inevitable.
Her project bravely delves into the complex territory of sibling rivalry. The scenes she creates with her brother are infused with humour, tenderness, and at times, a touch of cruelty – decorating him with frankfurters or vegetables for example. Presented at the festival in an anarchic, staged playroom, the images confront us with the messy, funny and sometimes uncomfortable truth of growing up together.
The chains of generations of oppression and poverty link together the story of the Appalachian people explored by the photojournalist Stacy Kranitz, winner of the Images Vevey book award 2025/2026 for her photobook project Ain’t No Grave Gonna Hold My Body Down.
Drawing on archive photographs dating back more than a century, alongside newspaper reports charting the heroic early pioneers’ descent into poverty and isolation, the work examines the purpose of photography: is it to bear witness or a tool of propaganda?
Stacy tells the story of how these once proud and courageous people made their way to the mountain ranges of the eastern United States, only to be exploited by mining companies who left them economically impoverished and socially isolated.
During the 1960s, the American author and historian Harry M. Caudill further stigmatised the Appalachian people by arguing that their circumstances couldn’t be fixed. In film footage unearthed by Stacy, Caudill claims that genetic deficiencies had amplified their plight, a theory later echoed in JD Vance’s memoir Hillbilly Elegy, which traces his own family’s Appalachian roots.
Fast forward to the present and, in the photographs Stacy has made of Pat Greene and his friends and family, we see a cycle of deprivation that remains unbroken. Through this project, Stacy challenges stereotypes and assumptions that allow a single image to define people and their circumstances. She draws attention to a system stacked against those who need help most.

Pixel 11 Pro Fold review: the foldable with the best triple camera
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:00:24 UTC
15:00:24 KST

Google’s latest folding phone sticks with its tried and tested design of a traditional phone shape when closed and almost square when open, in the face of new competition from Apple’s squat iPhone fold.
The Pixel 11 Pro Fold is Google’s fourth-generation folder and is still one of the only models with a full water and dust resistance rating similar to standard slab phones. It costs from £1,799 (€1,999/$1,899/A$2,799), which is £50 (€100/$100/A$100) more than last year as the pressure of RAMageddon continues to push up prices.
The 11 Pro Fold looks identical to previous models, but it is 19g lighter and fractionally slimmer in all directions with a slightly larger outside screen. Unlike Samsung’s slender Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra or squat models such as Apple’s iPhone Duo or the Galaxy Z Fold 8, the Pixel has the same ratio screen as Google’s slab phones and so handles precisely like them when shut with a bright and smooth 6.5in display.
Opened up, the internal screen is essentially square, which allows you to run two full-size apps side by side but is less ideal for video. The display is very bright, crisp and smooth, but the crease where it folds is more visible and detectable with your fingers than the best rivals. Like all foldable phones the screen is also delicate compared with standard hardened glass phones.
The back of the phone hides the excellent Qi2 magnetic charging and accessory support from last year, while Google’s gimmicky HiLight notification LED, as seen on the other Pro Pixels, replaces the camera’s flash.
Specifications
- 
  Outside screen: 6.5in FHD+ 120Hz OLED (399ppi)
- 
  Inside screen: 8in 120Hz OLED (372ppi)
- 
  Processor: Google Tensor G6
- 
  RAM: 16GB
- 
  Storage: 256, 512GB or 1TB
- 
  Operating system: Android 17
- 
  Camera: 48MP wide, 10.5MP ultrawide, 10.8MP 5x telephoto; two 10MP selfie cameras
- 
  Connectivity: 5G, eSim, USB-C, wifi 7, NFC, Bluetooth 6, UWB, Thread, GNSS
- 
  Water resistance: IP68 (1.5 metres for 30 minutes)
- 
  Dimensions folded: 155.2 x 76 x 10.1mm
- 
  Dimensions unfolded: 155.2 x 150.4 x 5mm
- 
  Weight: 239g
Faster chip but slightly shorter battery life
The 11 Pro Fold has the same Tensor G6 chip from the other Pixel 11 models with 16GB of memory, which works very well for all kinds of multitasking, remaining snappy and responsive with multiple apps on screen. It handles light gaming, but can’t match rivals for raw performance.
The battery life is a little shorter than last year’s model and rivals, lasting about 36 hours between charges when used on a mix of 5G and wifi with the screens in active use for about 2.5 hours each for browsing, messaging, apps and photos. Most will have to charge it nightly, but even on the heaviest of use days the Pixel managed to end the day without requiring a top-up.
Android 17, Magic Cue and Gemini
The Fold runs the latest version of Android 17 with all of Google’s AI tools, including Gemini’s proactive system, the new supercharged voice dictation feature and six months of Google AI Pro subscription.
Many of Google’s more creative AI tools, including the Omni video creation tool, Circle to Search and Gemini’s information cards benefit from the larger internal screen. Google has made a number of modifications to Android to better fit the folding form, including a floating taskbar and a split-screen mode that can resize apps beyond the bounds of the physical display introduced last year.
The bubbles system, which was primarily used for messaging apps, has been revamped to allow as many as five apps of any kind to be opened as floating windows on top of a single or split-screen apps and minimised to a little taskbar. But it isn’t as flexible as Samsung’s true desktop-like floating window system that can be resized and placed anywhere on the screen.
The 11 Pro Fold will be supported until August 2033 with operating system and security updates, making it one of the longest supported devices available.
Sustainability
The battery is rated to last in excess of 1,000 full charge cycles with at least 80% of its original capacity. The phone is repairable by Google and third-party shops, and self-repair with manuals and parts is due to be available.
The Pixel 11 Pro Fold contains 24% recycled materials by weight. The company breaks down the phone’s environmental impact in its report and will recycle old devices for free.
Camera
The Pixel Fold has some of the best cameras on a foldable phone, including three on the back and two very good selfie cameras, one in each screen.
The rear cameras include a new 48MP main camera with a larger sensor, matching that in the regular Pixel 11, which captures better detail and has significantly better lowlight performance than its predecessor. The 10.5MP ultrawide camera is the same as last year and is still as solid this year. The 10.8MP 5x telephoto is also the same and shoots good photos that meaningfully reduce the distance to objects. It has a solid digital zoom that stretches up to 30x magnification, but not the generative AI zoom of the Pixel 11 Pro.
The Fold also has the new software features from the other Pixel 11 phones, including Camera Looks, which allows you to customise the processing of your photos for different styles. Magic Capture shoots video and photos automatically with one button, while the Creator suite helps you storyboard, trim and produce video content for social media.
The camera also has tricks to make use of the folding form, including modes to shoot when propped up and the excellent Made You Look feature that displays an animated character that reacts to your kid’s smiling to help take better photos.
Overall, the 11 Pro Fold has an excellent set of cameras that don’t feel a significant downgrade on top regular smartphones.
Price
The Google Pixel 11 Pro Fold costs from £1,799 (€1,999/$1,899/A$2,799).
For comparison, the Pixel 11 costs £879, Pixel 11 Pro costs £1,079, the Pixel 11 Pro XL costs £1,279, the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra costs £1,899, the iPhone Duo costs £1,999 and the Honor Magic V6 costs £1,499.99.
Verdict
The Pixel 11 Pro Fold is a fairly minor hardware update for Google’s folding phone line, which makes it slightly lighter, smaller, brighter and faster, while keeping its overall look and feel the same.
It has great AI tools, slick software and some of the very best cameras. The inside screen is super bright and crisp, but the crease down the middle is more noticeable than rivals, the battery life is a bit short and it is thick and heavy compared to the best from rival Samsung.
As with all foldables the inside screen can be permanently marked by a fingernail if you press hard enough, so you must still be careful with it compared with a standard Pixel.
But if you want a folding phone to be exactly the same as a regular one when closed, the Pixel Fold is the one to get.
 Pros: just like a regular Pixel when closed, good tablet screen, dust and water resistant, Qi2 magnetic accessory and charging, good performance, great cameras with 5x zoom, great software packed with cutting-edge AI, six months of Google AI Pro included, seven years of software updates.
Cons: very expensive, more fragile and costlier to repair than a regular Pixel, thick and heavy, cameras can’t match regular Pixel 11 Pro, battery life could be better.

Syrian women fight to reclaim property lost during the war
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
06:00:16 UTC
15:00:16 KST

Breadcrumb
The homes Syria's women lost: A fight for property, justice and a future
For Syrian women, losing a home or land during years of conflict has often meant losing more than a place to live.
A recent report by the Syrian Network for Human Rights found that various factors have led to violations affecting Syrian women's private property rights since 2011.
The report, issued on 23 July and titled Property violations against women in Syria since 2011: Patterns of seizure and their impact on women in the context of conflict and the transitional phase, explained that the war in Syria was not a direct cause of these violations.
Instead, they resulted from a combination of previous government policies and factors including exceptional legislation, forced displacement and weak legal protections.
The network also highlighted social norms in Syria that have limited women's ability to prove ownership or reclaim property. Many properties were registered in the names of husbands, fathers or male relatives who were killed, detained, forcibly disappeared or displaced, leaving the women concerned unable to manage or claim these properties.
The fight to keep what is theirs
The Syrian Network for Human Rights based its report on testimonies from 35 women from several Syrian provinces, collected between January 2025 and March 2026.
It classified violations of women's private property rights into three categories: direct property violations, arbitrary legal or administrative measures, and discriminatory social and economic factors.
The issue of property rights is among the key challenges facing Syria during the transitional phase.
The network identified 11 main patterns behind Syrian women's loss of property ownership. These include forced displacement and the loss of effective control over homes and land; destruction of property as a result of widespread or deliberate bombardment; converting civilian property into military sites or uses; precautionary and executive seizure and arbitrary confiscation; property seizure due to the detention of a family member or accusations of political opposition; and expropriation through urban planning laws and exceptional legislation.
This particularly includes Law No. 10 of 2018; loss of ownership documents that prevent proof of rights; forgery and tampering with property records; difficulties in accessing justice and the absence of effective remedies; deprivation of inheritance rights due to conflict or family disputes; and financial extortion in exchange for retaining property or allowing its use.
For Fadel Abdul Ghany, executive director of the Syrian Network for Human Rights, these violations cannot be explained by the conflict alone.
"Women's property rights in Syria were not violated solely because of the conflict, but also because of an integrated system of repressive legislation, discriminatory practices and social norms that accumulated over decades," he told The New Arab.
He added: "The former Assad regime exploited them to turn property ownership into a tool for collective punishment and political subjugation."
However, Fadel explained that "the fall of the regime did not automatically end violations of women's property rights in Syria."
He said: "If the authorities (currently) do not take the initiative to establish effective mechanisms for returning property, providing compensation and reforming the legislative framework, thousands of women will remain deprived of their rights even after the end of the conflict."
He stressed that "it is impossible to build a new Syria on a fair foundation without restoring seized property rights and enabling women to exercise their legal and economic rights on an equal footing (with men)."
The impact of these violations is evident in the testimonies on which the Syrian Network for Human Rights based its report.
One of those women is Mona Suleiman, 52, from Quneitra province in southern Syria. She said she had been denied access to her agricultural land for years after it was used for military purposes, first by forces of the former regime and later due to the presence of Israeli occupation forces in the area.
The loss of access to the land deprived her of her main source of income.
In central Syria, Alia al-Faouri's testimony from the Baba Amr neighbourhood in Homs also highlights the impact of property destruction.
Her home was reduced to rubble as a result of shelling after she was displaced from the neighbourhood in 2012.
"Losing the house was not only the loss of shelter, but I also lost everything I owned: furniture, gold and ownership documents," she said. "I feel that I have lost the stability I built throughout my life."
A path towards property justice
Property disputes do not stem only from displacement, destruction, or seizure. Family disputes also stand out as one of the violations of women's property rights in Syria.
This pattern is reflected in attempts to deprive women of inheritance or force them to give it up.
As an example, 50-year-old Fatima M., from Homs, told The New Arab that she has been trying for several months to reach an agreement with her siblings to resolve the issue of her inheritance from her father, but they have been delaying the process.
She noted that "large costs will result from taking the matter to court and appointing a lawyer, and I will not be able to afford them."
Fatima believes that "a specialised judicial department in this field could benefit us so that we do not enter the maze of lawyers and high costs, or lawyers could be appointed at the government's expense to resolve this type of case."
For women facing these problems, resolving property disputes remains closely linked to access to justice and the ability to prove ownership.
In its latest report, the Syrian Network for Human Rights presented a series of recommendations regarding women's property rights in Syria.
These included establishing an independent body to return properties and settle property disputes, reforming legislation that has been used to expropriate property and to freeze the disposal of disputed real estate, and introducing flexible mechanisms to prove ownership, among other recommendations aimed at limiting these violations.
The network called on the United Nations and donor organisations to support reform of Syria's property system, link reconstruction funding to guarantees that property rights are respected, and prevent the financing of projects built on illegally confiscated properties.
It also called for support for the rehabilitation of land registries and strengthening legal assistance programmes for Syrian women, while empowering them economically in a way that ensures the restoration of their rights and creates conditions for the safe and sustainable return of displaced people.
Abdullah Al-Bashir is a Syrian journalist and the correspondent for Al-Araby Al-Jadeed in Syria

Beijing puts a total ban on drones in update to regulations after deadly crash

Unknown Publisher

Beijing puts a total ban on drones in update to regulations after deadly crash
China’s capital will enforce the country’s toughest rules on UAVs from November 15 but a no-fly zone will be in effect from Sunday
The newly revised regulations on the management of unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) were announced on Sunday on the municipal government website. They will apply across the city.
The authorities will also establish a no-fly zone in the capital and its surrounding areas, effective from September 20, primarily restricting non-essential flights, official media in Beijing said on Sunday. Commercial aviation and emergency rescue operations will be exempt.
According to the document, Beijing’s entire administrative area is designated as restricted airspace for UAVs. It is forbidden not only to fly drones but also to possess, store, transport or bring them and their core components into the city, except in exceptional circumstances.

‘Conscious unbossing’: China youth reject promotions to avoid intense corporate stress

Unknown Publisher

06:00:07 UTC
15:00:07 KST

‘Conscious unbossing’: China youth reject promotions to avoid intense corporate stress
Employees share scripts online on how to decline promotions, choosing better pay and recognition over career advancement
A growing number of young Chinese employees are increasingly turning down promotions, challenging the traditional notion that career advancement is the ultimate marker of success.
This trend has gained significant traction on mainland social media, where related posts have attracted over 40 million views.
Workers have shared scripts online that highlight their decisions to decline promotions, often expressing gratitude, citing feelings of insufficient readiness, and seeking better compensation or recognition of their value in their current roles.
One online user, known as June, has turned down promotions three times during her decade-long career.
Her first declined offer occurred while she was working as a management trainee at an internet company, when she chose travel over accepting a new role.
Later, after being promoted to lead a training centre at an education firm, she reverted to her previous position within two weeks, overwhelmed by escalating pressure.

Air Force officer shot down over Iran describes "miraculous" survival and rescue in first interview
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
05:59:27 UTC
14:59:27 KST

- i24NEWS
- International
- US / Canada
- Air Force officer shot down over Iran describes "miraculous" survival and rescue in first interview
Air Force officer shot down over Iran describes "miraculous" survival and rescue in first interview
In his first interview, the weapons systems officer known by his call sign "Bravo" told 60 Minutes he survived a damaged parachute, a 7,000-foot climb with broken bones, and evaded Iranian forces
Bravo and his pilot, call sign Alpha, ejected from their F-15E jet, Dude 44, after it was struck by an Iranian missile five weeks into the war. The two officers were stranded miles apart in a mountainous desert region with Iranian bounties on their heads, triggering a massive rescue operation to bring them home. Now, months later, Bravo sat down with 60 Minutes for the first interview by either officer since the crash.
"That day was just another day in the war for us, until the moment we got hit by an Iranian weapon," Bravo said, describing the impact as being hit by a "freight train." He credited Alpha with saving both their lives by ejecting them in time: "I will be thankful, to my dying day, for my pilot and crewmate Alpha for saving our lives and ejecting us."
Bravo's parachute was damaged in the strike, and he described looking up mid-fall to see it missing. "That was the most terrifying thing I've ever seen," he said, adding that he prayed, "Lord, your will be done. But if I'm getting through this, I need help."
Bravo and military debriefers estimated he was falling between 70 and 100 miles per hour. Despite breaking his back, shoulder, and arm in the landing, Bravo called his survival "a miracle" and "a testament to God's provision."
After the crash, Bravo did what any trained soldier would do: find the high ground. The soldier climbed a 7,000-foot ridgeline despite his injuries, saying, "never let a lack of motivation put you on Iranian TV," and crediting the military's "warrior ethos" for helping him push through the terrain.
Bravo also praised the airmen who maintained his ejection seat and survival gear, saying it "outperformed the design specifications" despite damage from the missile.
https://x.com/i/web/status/2099288255250223304
This post can't be displayed because social networks cookies have been deactivated. You can activate them by clicking .
After nearly 48 hours, rescuers reached Bravo on the ridgeline as Iranian soldiers massed at the base of the mountain, a moment he called "one of the greatest moments of my life." According to US military sources, Iranian forces came within a few hundred meters of Bravo as they searched for him.
Gen. Dan Caine addressed the decision to launch the extraction in daylight, saying, "Any time you go behind enemy lines in daylight it's going to be dicey. We would much rather go at night, but given the threat, the decision was taken to boldly go behind enemy lines to get to him before anyone else could."
A clandestine US Air Force unit ultimately extracted Bravo and more than 90 special operators after the rescue aircraft's landing gear became stuck in desert sand; the US later bombed the marooned plane to keep it out of Iranian hands.
Bravo said he never doubted the military would come for him, citing his faith and "the men and women of the United States military." Of his wife, he said, "I was really proud of her and grateful for the strength that she showed" after their first call following his rescue.
Asked what he wants Americans to take from his story, Bravo said, "This is not a story about what I went through. Instead, it's a story of the teamwork, the training, of the faith, of the decisions, the leadership, to do something that will live in history as an example of what America is willing to do to uphold our promise and leave no man behind."

Train carrying Estonian leaders crossed Ukraine border just before Russian drone strike

Unknown Publisher

Train carrying Estonian leaders crossed Ukraine border just before Russian drone strike
Estonian politicians and leaders were traveling by train through western Ukraine shortly before a Russian drone struck a station near the Polish border, Delfi reported.
The strike hit a station at Yahodyn on Ukrainian territory, around two kilometers from the Polish border, shortly after a diplomatic train carrying high-level figures passed along the same line.
Ukraine says the diplomatic train may have been the intended target. No one was injured.
According to Ukrzaliznytsia, Ukraine's state rail operator, the "YES Express" crossed the border about an hour and a half before its shunting locomotive at Yahodyn was hit. "We cannot confirm the exact target of the attack, but no one was injured," the company said.
The political figures and former leaders had been travelling back from Kyiv, where they had attended the annual YES (Yalta European Strategy) forum.
Former President of Estonia Kersti Kaljulaid was among those travelling from Ukraine to Poland by train. She told "Ukraina stuudio" that her train was stopped and passengers were temporarily evacuated. Other leading Estonian figures on the same train included former economy and information technology minister Tiit Riisalo and Taavi Linnamäe, who had headed up Kaljulaid's office and foundation.
Delfi reported that Reform party MPs Mati Raidma, Marko Mihkelson and Eerik-Niiles Kross were also on the train, along with Foreign Ministry Undersecretary Mariin Ratnik and Kristi Raik, director of the Estonian Foreign Policy Institute. Former Finnish prime minister Sanna Marin was reportedly on board as well.
The BBC reported that former British Prime Minsiter Boris Johnson, former CIA chief David Petraeus and senior European security officials had passed through the same line. The strike, just two kilometers from Polish territory, prompted an emergency meeting in Poland and renewed calls for more air defenses and sanctions.
It follows a Russian escalation of attacks on Ukraine's rail network, and a pattern of suspected Russian sabotage across Europe — including the fire at an Estonian defense firm's plant.

War update: 208 combat engagements on frontline over past day
According to Ukrinform, the General Staff of the Armed Forces of Ukraine stated this on Facebook on the situation as of 08:00 on September 14.
The enemy carried out 90 airstrikes, dropping 286 guided aerial bombs. In addition, Russian forces used 11,727 kamikaze drones and carried out 2,872 shelling attacks, 45 of them using multiple-launch rocket systems.
The following settlements came under Russian fire: Sopych, Korenok, Vilna Sloboda, Tovstodubove, Neskuchne, Yastrubine, Pysarivka, Kindrativka, Mohrytsia, Bachivsk, Ulanove, Peremoha, Hirky, Bezzalivka, Rohizne, Neskuchne, Nova Sich, Mariine, Oleksiivka and Mtolpillya in Sumy region; Kliusy, Mykhalchyna Sloboda, Leonivka and Buchky in Chernihiv region. In the Northern Slobozhanshchyna and Kursk directions, no Russian assault operations were recorded. Russian forces carried out 148 shelling attacks, 10 of them using MLRS, and launched four strikes with 10 guided aerial bombs.

In the Lyman direction, Russian forces attacked three times near Novosergiivka and Novoselivka.
In the Sloviansk direction, the enemy launched eight assaults near Yampil, Ozerne, Kryva Luka, Rai-Oleksandrivka and Mykolaivka.

In the Oleksandrivka direction, the enemy launched three assaults toward Ternove.
In the Huliaipole direction, Russian forces attacked eight times, attempting to advance near Hirke and Charivne and toward Rizdvianka.
In the Orikhiv direction, the enemy attempted to improve its positions toward Chervona Krynytsia.
On the Dnipro River direction, the invaders did not conduct any offensive operations.
No signs of Russian offensive group formations were detected in the Volyn and Polissia directions.

Animated map shows spread of illegal Israeli settlements across Palestine

Animated map shows spread of illegal Israeli settlements across Palestine
Some 750,000 settlers live across more than 500 illegal settlements and outposts, as Israeli pogroms hit record highs.
Israeli settlements are Jewish-only communities built illegally on Palestinian land in the occupied West Bank and East Jerusalem.
These settlements often look like ordinary suburbs: uniform townhouses, red-tiled roofs, and neat lawns, perched atop hills that give them a commanding view of the Palestinian towns and villages carved up to make room for them.

- list 4 of 4Who are the ‘Hilltop Girls’ behind Israel’s ‘soft settlement’ strategy?
In this visual explainer, Al Jazeera maps the spread of illegal Israeli settlements and outposts, and the settler pogroms accompanying them, now at their highest levels on record.
The spread of Israeli settlements and outposts
Settlements predate the creation of Israel in 1948, but their number and scale expanded dramatically after Israel occupied the West Bank and East Jerusalem in 1967.
Over the past six decades, the settlement network has grown from scattered hilltop outposts into a sprawling system of settlements, roads and highways binding them to each other and to Israel.
According to Peace Now, an Israeli anti-settlement watchdog, there are at least 148 illegal settlements in the occupied West Bank and 15 in occupied East Jerusalem, 163 in total, now home to some 750,000 Israelis, roughly 10 percent of the country’s Jewish population.
The animation below tracks the founding of each settlement and outpost, along with their growing populations. In 2025 alone, nearly 28,000 housing units were advanced within existing settlements in the occupied West Bank, far more growth than new settlements or outposts added on their own.
By 2024, these 163 illegal settlements were home to 744,700 Israeli settlers: 511,100 in the West Bank and 233,600 in East Jerusalem, alongside a record number of illegal outposts, now more than 400, that are not officially counted but are often absorbed into recognised settlements over time, making a true tally difficult to pin down.
How outposts become settlements
Settlements are illegal under international law as they violate the Fourth Geneva Convention, which bans an occupying power from transferring its population to the area it occupies.
The United Nations Security Council, the International Court of Justice (ICJ) and the International Committee of the Red Cross, among others, have affirmed this position, most recently in the ICJ’s July 2024 advisory opinion.
Under Israeli law, settlements are legally sanctioned, planned, funded and built with direct government approval, ranging from small rural communities to large towns like Ariel and Maale Adumim.
Outposts are different. They are smaller communities built without formal authorisation, though many are later legalised or absorbed into existing settlements.
How settlements carve up Palestinian land
Some Israeli settlements have grown into sizable urban communities, complete with their own mayors, schools, shopping malls, medical centres and even universities.
Modi’in Illit is the largest, with 89,301 residents, followed by Beitar Illit with 70,251 and Pisgat Ze’ev with 48,419.
Like most settlements, they are linked by a dense network of roads built exclusively for settlers, many of them bypass roads that cut through Palestinian land, fragmenting communities and, backed by checkpoints and roadblocks, restricting the movement of Palestinians who live alongside them.
Nowhere is this clearer than E1, an area east of Jerusalem planned for the expansion of Maale Adumim, which would sever East Jerusalem from the rest of the West Bank and further fragment Palestinian territorial continuity.
In August 2025, Israeli Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich approved more than 3,000 homes in the E1 settlement project, a plan frozen for decades under international pressure, declaring the move would “bury the idea of a Palestinian state”.
The decision drew global condemnation, including from nearly 30 governments and the European Commission, who called it “unacceptable and a violation of international law”.
Rising pogroms
Israeli settlers can be broadly divided into two types.
The first are largely drawn by economics: cheaper rents, subsidised mortgages, discounted land and government incentives that make housing in the illegal settlements far more affordable than in Israel proper.
The second are religious-nationalists, who believe the land was promised to them by God. This group includes the “hilltop youth”, known for establishing outposts and attacking Palestinian communities, often with the Israeli military standing by or backing them, as in the weeks-long siege of Qusra, which began in August and continues under military enforcement today.
Experts and UN officials increasingly describe these attacks as pogroms, organised, often state-backed violence against a targeted minority, a term Israeli military officers themselves have used after past incidents such as the 2023 attack on Huwara.
According to data from the UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA), settler attacks resulting in casualties and/or property damage have risen sharply, particularly since October 7, 2023.
In 2025, 1,836 such attacks were recorded, more than double the 852 recorded in 2022, an average of about five attacks a day.
Since October 7, 2023, 5,131 attacks have been recorded in total. The increase has continued into 2026, with 1,418 attacks recorded between January and July, though the year is not yet complete.
The violence and the creation of new outposts are two different aspects of settlement activity, but together they show how illegal settlement expansion is occurring not only through established communities and government planning, but also through new activity on the ground.

France submits new proposal to ban social media for under-15s
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
05:52:56 UTC
14:52:56 KST

France's President Emmanuel Macron looks on during a visit of the Chaptal High School on the first day of the new academic year in Mende, southern France on September 1, 2026. (AFP Pool Photo)
The French government submitted a reworked proposal to ban social media for children, after its previous ban was struck down by the country's top constitutional authority, President Emmanuel Macron said on Sept. 14.
France's Constitutional Council struck down the previous legislation in mid-August, finding that part of it constituted an unnecessary infringement on the freedom of expression of those affected.
"After rigorous technical work the government today is notifying" the European Commission of the new draft, Macron wrote on X.
The notification is a key step, as it ensures that the legislation is in line with European Union laws.
Macron has made banning social media use for children and teens one of his top priorities and hopes to see a working ban in force by the time he leaves office next year.
He has called for similar legislation to be adopted throughout the European Union.
EU chief Ursula von der Leyen is due to address a possible bloc-wide social media ban for children in a speech in Brussels this week.
A growing number of countries are taking steps to restrict social media access amid multiplying warnings over its harmful effects on children.

Indonesia intensifies search for 129 people missing after ship capsizes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
05:51:35 UTC
14:51:35 KST

Indonesia intensifies search for 129 people missing after ship capsizes
Officials say more than 600 rescuers battle stormy seas in search for missing people.
Hundreds of rescuers including the coastguard have joined the search for 129 people missing after an Indonesian passenger ship capsized in the Java Sea, authorities said Monday.
The National Search and Rescue Agency (Basarnas) said crews had rescued 108 people and that at least six others had died after the ferry capsized on Sunday.
Recommended Stories
list of 3 items
- list 1 of 3Seven more people rescued after boat sinks in Indonesia, 18 still missing
- list 2 of 3Five passengers found drifting days after boat sinks in Indonesia
- list 3 of 3At least five dead, 41 missing after ferry catches fire off Indonesia
The Virgo Transport 8 ship was carrying 243 people from Surabaya on the Indonesian island of Java to Banjarmasin, a port city on Borneo, when it lost contact with its operator during poor weather conditions, according to Basarnas.
“We have continued the search for the missing since early in the morning [Monday]. We are deploying 622 personnel and around 12 ships,” I Putu Sudayana, head of the Banjarmasin rescue agency, told the news service Reuters.
Choppy waters with high waves and strong winds were impeding the search on Monday, Mohammad Syafii, head of the national rescue agency, said.
“We would also deploy personnel with underwater rescue skills but the current conditions do not allow us to,” he said.
The capsized boat is about 80 nautical miles (equivalent to about 150km) from the command centre at Trisakti Port in Banjarmasin, Arianto Ardi, another official at the rescue agency, told Reuters.
“The conditions there are quite windy, making it difficult for the vessel to dock,” he said.
In a video released by the rescue agency, a large red-and-black ship lies capsized and partially submerged in choppy blue water, with its red-painted underside visible above the surface.
Two small boats, each carrying people, were seen floating near the capsized ship amid rough water.
On Sunday, Edy Prakoso, another official at the national rescue agency, said the ship leaned heavily to one side after strong waves hit its starboard side.
A passing helicopter reported the vessel had capsized, Transport Minister Dudy Purwagandhi said on Sunday, with the cause still being investigated.
He said the boat had the capacity to carry 500 passengers, so it was not overloaded.
Indonesia relies on ferries across its 17,000 islands, where sea travel is cheaper and more accessible than flying, and there have been numerous accidents.
In separate incidents last month, five people died when a ferry caught fire off Madura Island, and one person died in a fire on a ferry travelling from the tourist island of Bali.

Russia attacked Ukraine with 108 unmanned aerial vehicles, 85 of which were destroyed or suppressed by air defense
Kyiv • UNN
Overnight, Russia launched 108 drones of various types at Ukraine. Air defense destroyed or suppressed 85 drones; impacts were recorded at seven locations.
During the night of September 14, Russian forces attacked Ukraine with 108 strike drones of various types. Air defense forces destroyed and suppressed 85 enemy drones. This was reported by the Air Force of the Armed Forces of Ukraine, UNN writes.
Details
Starting in the evening of September 13, the occupiers used Shahed-type attack UAVs, including jet-powered ones, as well as Gerbera-type drones and Parody decoy drones.
The launches were carried out from the areas of Orel and Kursk in Russia, as well as from the temporarily occupied territories of Donetsk region and Crimea — from the area of Hvardiiske.
The air attack was repelled by fighter aircraft, anti-aircraft missile forces, electronic warfare and unmanned systems units, as well as mobile fire groups of Ukraine’s Defense Forces.
According to preliminary data, as of 08:00, air defense had destroyed or suppressed 85 enemy Shahed-, Gerbera- and other types of UAVs
At the same time, hits by enemy air attack weapons were recorded at seven locations.
The Air Force emphasized that the attack is ongoing. Enemy drones remain in Ukraine’s airspace.

Poland, Ukraine slam Russian strikes near border as 'escalation'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
05:48:00 UTC
14:48:00 KST

Poland's Minister of Foreign Affairs Radoslaw Sikorski (L) gestures as he addresses the media next to Ukraine's Minister of Foreign Affairs Andriy Sybiga (R) as they take part in a joint briefing in Kiev on September 13, 2026, amid Russian invasion in Ukraine. (AFP Photo)
Poland and Ukraine denounced on Sept. 13 Russian strikes close to the Polish border that hit a Warsaw-bound train near a frontier checkpoint just after another train carrying several EU officials and British ex-PM Boris Johnson passed through.
Ukraine's rail operator said Johnson and former U.S. CIA director David Petraeus, who were in Kiev for the annual Yalta European Strategy forum, had been travelling on two separate trains around the time the Warsaw-bound locomotive was hit.
Petraeus told the New York Times in a telephone interview on Sept. 13 that he could hear Russian drones above followed by an explosion — prompting passengers to be quickly evacuated.
"For those who have not been under fire, this was terrifying... This is the act of a truly barbaric leader trying to terrorize Ukrainian civilians," he added.
Johnson, who was travelling with several EU officials on a separate train, condemned the attack in a post on X.
"I don't know what warped logic drove Putin to blow up a stationary Ukrainian locomotive on the Polish border this morning," he said.
"What we can say for sure is that this is the kind of random and senseless attack Ukrainians are enduring every day — even on civilian railways."
Poland and Ukraine said a Kiev-Warsaw passenger train was struck just two kilometers (1.2 miles) from the Polish border, but reported no casualties.
Warsaw has been one of Kiev's main allies and, with Ukrainian airspace closed, trains to Poland have been one of the main routes out of the war-torn country, used by refugees.
The Ukrzaliznytsia rail operator confirmed the attack on Sept. 13 but did not say how far the officials were from the strike site.
"A diplomatic train was at Yagodyn station... among others, security advisers from several EU member states and former European leaders (were) on board, including former U.K. Prime Minister Boris Johnson," the operator said.
"In another train that was at Yagodyn station at the very moment of the attack was former CIA Director David Petraeus."
The strikes came as U.S. President Donald Trump called on Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky to halt attacks on Russian refineries, saying it was causing a shortage of diesel fuel and "there are plenty of other targets".
Ukraine has increasingly targeted Russian oil refineries in a bid to hurt Russia's war effort.
Russia attacked western Ukraine with drones Sept. 13, just days after U.S. envoys visited Kiev and Moscow in a bid to revive talks to end the war.
Zelensky said Moscow "deliberately" struck the locomotive.
"It is precisely to prevent these strikes from spreading further into Europe that Ukrainian men and women fight every day, give their lives, and do not allow the aggression to expand," Zelensky said.
Poland's Prime Minister Donald Tusk chaired an emergency meeting in Warsaw following the strikes, warning he expected Russia to escalate the war "in the coming weeks".
He added: "We cannot exclude that the escalation will also affect our territory... I hope not."
Russia said it had hit "railway infrastructure used to transport military cargo" from European countries.
Speaking in Kiev, Poland's foreign minister Radoslaw Sikorski said the "escalation" should lead to allies to "double" their support for Ukraine.
"This was just a few hundred meters from the Polish border, this affects the safety of my countrymen," Sikorski said.
Ukraine's Foreign Minister Andriy Sybiga said President Vladimir Putin considered Russia was "de facto" at war with Europe.
"Putin's terror is literally knocking at the door of the European Union and NATO," Sybiga said.
Russian attacks killed at least two people on Sept. 13 in the frontline city of Kramatorsk.
Twenty people, including two children, were also wounded in a second day of attacks on the southern port city of Odesa, Ukrainian officials said.
The Kremlin, meanwhile, said negotiations with Ukraine and the United States on the war could take place in October.
"We do not rule out such a possibility; we are talking about the foreseeable future," Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told journalists, cited by Russia's Ria and Tass news agencies.
Several rounds of negotiations have failed, while U.S.-led efforts stalled during the Middle East war.
In an interview released on Sept. 12, Zelensky said he would be willing to meet Putin at the G20 summit in Miami in December.
But Peskov said that was "impossible", speaking to AFP in New Delhi on Sept. 12, repeating Moscow's demand that a meeting could only take place in the Russian capital.

Ukraine's prosecutor general says he approved charges against anti-graft agency chief after resigning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-14
05:45:00 UTC
14:45:00 KST

On his way out, Ukraine's disgraced prosecutor general hits back, charges anti-graft agencies investigating him
Editor's note: This story is being updated.
Prosecutor General Ruslan Kravchenko, who has submitted his resignation, had approved the charges against Semen Kryvonos, director of Ukraine's National Anti-Corruption Bureau (NABU), he said in an address released on Sept. 14.
"I suspect (Kryvonos) of falsifying official documents in order to obtain especially large illicit benefit, as well as of a sham adoption intended to create artificial grounds for avoiding liability in cour," Kravchenko said, publishing the texts of the alleged charges.
He also claims to have approved charges against a person close to Oleksandr Klymenko, head of the Specialized Anti-Corruption Prosecutor's Office (SAPO), without naming them.
The statement comes days after Kravchenko resigned on Sept. 7 following a NABU investigation into an alleged criminal group protecting scam call centers to launder millions of hryvnias. Kravchenko denied involvement in the scheme, allegedly involving a senior Prosecutor General's Office official, Serhii Kropyva.
Ukrainska Pravda reported, citing sources, that Kravchenko left Ukraine in a government car at 2:30 a.m. local time on Sept. 14. Kravchenko later claimed he was headed abroad on a business trip to brief partners on the "real state of affairs" in Ukraine's anti-corruption system and "signs of uncontrolled influence" within the agencies.
NABU said Kryvonos had not been formally charged as of Sept. 14, adding that Kravchenko's statements "remain public rather than procedural at this stage."
"NABU and SAPO view these actions as a continuation of the systematic attack on the independence of anti-corruption agencies," the bureau said.
The NABU investigation has also raised questions about Kravchenko's alleged ties to the group.
The bureau's recordings refer to a "boss" allegedly protecting the group, a figure whose patronymic, Andriiovych, and prior role as head of the State Tax Service correspond to Kravchenko.
An earlier Telegram post by the Prosecutor General's Office confirms that the investigators understand the term "boss" as referring to Kravchenko.
An anti-corruption prosecutor also described Kropyva as a confidant of Kravchenko during court hearings.
According to NABU recordings, Kropyva arranged repair work at Kravchenko's house in Kozyn, an elite residential area south of Kyiv. Kravchenko said he had chosen the house last winter and had been living there since May.
Last July, Kravchenko, long seen as a staunch loyalist of President Volodymyr Zelensky, took part in Zelensky's team's failed attempt to destroy the independence of NABU and SAPO, which sparked nationwide protests.
Kravchenko now claims that Zelensky had previously sought to prevent him from approving the charges against the NABU chief. The prosecutor general called on Kryvonos and Klymenko to resign.
After Kravchenko's statement, Zelensky called on parliament to back his dismissal as prosecutor general.
"This prosecutor general has only one path: dismissal from office. That is exactly what I told him," Zelensky said on social media.
"Ukraine has seen all the reasons. If he considers this political pressure, then let him call it that."
Parliament is set to vote on Kravchenko's dismissal on Sept. 15.
